Volume Conjecture and quantum hyperbolic invariants: the figure eight knot complement
Stéphane Baseilhac 1 , Fathi Ben Aribi 2

Abstract
. We compute the real part of the semi-classical limit of the sequence of quantum hyperbolic invariants (QHI) of the figure-eight knot complement M \displaystyle M . We show that it is rigid, in the sense that it does not depend on the choice of holonomy representation of M \displaystyle M , and it is either 0 \displaystyle 0 or equal to the hyperbolic volume of M \displaystyle M divided by 2 ​ π \displaystyle 2\pi , depending on a parity condition satisfied by logarithms of the holonomy eigenvalues on the canonical longitude, where the logarithms are parameters of the QHI of M \displaystyle M . Along the way we also survey some relevant general features of the QHI.



1
IMAG, Univ Montpellier, CNRS, France

2
Sorbonne Université, Université Paris Cité, CNRS, IMJ-PRG, F-75005 Paris, France

1
stephane.baseilhac@umontpellier.fr ,
2
fathi.ben-aribi@imj-prg.fr

Keywords: quantum invariants, volume conjecture, hyperbolic manifolds, saddle point method.

AMS subject classification 2020: 57K16, 57K32, 57R05.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main result

In this paper we compute the real part of the semi-classical limit of the sequence of
quantum hyperbolic invariants
(or
QHI
) associated to the (oriented) complement
\displaystyle M:=S^{3}\setminus K
of the figure-eight knot
\displaystyle K:=4_{1}
. We notably obtain exponential growth of the invariants, with rate equal to the hyperbolic volume
\displaystyle{\rm Vol}(M)/2\pi
, or subexponential growth, depending on a choice of parameters of the QHI.

Let us state our main result. Denote by
\displaystyle\lambda_{K}
the canonical longitude of
\displaystyle K
, and by
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
the irreducible component of the
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-character variety of
\displaystyle M
containing the character of the discrete faithful representations. The terms
\displaystyle h_{\rho}
and
\displaystyle k_{c}
in the statement below will be described later (see Section
3.3
), but should be thought of as additional geometric and topological structures on
\displaystyle M
. In particular
\displaystyle h_{\rho}
is a function on the set of curves on
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
, the boundary torus at infinity, with values in
\displaystyle(2i\pi)\mathbb{Z}
. The QHI, denoted
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
, depend on an odd integer
\displaystyle N\geq 3
and take values in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
modulo multiplication by
\displaystyle 2N
-th roots of unity.

Theorem 1.1
.

For every character
\displaystyle\rho\in X_{hyp}(M)
and charge weight
\displaystyle k_{c}
, the following holds:

(a) If
\displaystyle h_{\rho}(\lambda_{K})\in 2\pi i(2\mathbb{Z}+1)
, then

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}|{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})|={\rm Vol}(M).

(b) If
\displaystyle h_{\rho}(\lambda_{K})\in 4\pi i\mathbb{Z}
, then

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}|{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})|=0.

This result belongs to the realm of the
Volume Conjectures
, a set of conjectures first considered by Kashaev-Murakami-Murakami
[
40
,
45
]
, which predict that the semi-classical limit of certain sequences of quantum invariants of finite volume complete hyperbolic manifolds is equal to their volume. Theorem
1.1
shows new phenomena, due to the fact that the QHI depend on geometric data: they satisfy some kind of semi-classical rigidity, since the choice of character
\displaystyle\rho
has no effect on their semi-classical limit, and their growth, exponential or not, is determined by a geometrico-topological datum, the parity of

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle h_{\rho}(\lambda_{K})
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on an (easily obtained) integral representation of the QHI, a description of the so-called
quantum gluing varieties
, which form parameter spaces of the QHI and come from previous works of the first author with R. Benedetti, and elementary uses of the saddle point method and Perron’s method to estimate the integrals when
\displaystyle N\to+\infty
. The crux of the proof lies in finding deformations of the integration contours to “good positions” in order to apply these methods. A technical difficulty is that the endpoints of contours converge to poles of the integrand as
\displaystyle N\to+\infty
. The analysis of case (b) is far more demanding than that of case (a).

Many tools of this proof should generalize to study the QHI asymptotics for arbitrary cusped manifolds, in situations more general than those implemented by the two kinds of values of
\displaystyle h_{\rho}(\lambda_{K})
in Theorem
1.1
. For this reason, since the QHI theory is not well known, in some parts of the paper we allow ourselves to present relevant general aspects of this theory, in a hopefully pedestrian way, and introduce notions that should appear to be useful mainly for generalizations of Theorem
1.1
(we do this in Sections
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.4
,
3.3
and
4.1
, see the plan of the paper below). In particular, we discuss the space of logarithmic limits of log-parameters, on which the QHI asymptotics can be studied, and where the cases (a)-(b) of Theorem
1.1
correspond to very peculiar situations.

We remark that, beyond the semi-classical limit shown in Theorem
1.1
, sub exponential terms can be obtained (see Remark
5.8
). Also, the alternative between the cases (a) and (b) of Theorem
1.1
seemingly reflects that the QHI could detect lifts to
\displaystyle SL(2,\mathbb{C})
of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters; we plan to develop this in a future work.

In a forthcoming version of this work we will consider also similar asymptotics of QHI for hyperbolic Dehn fillings of
\displaystyle M
.

In the rest of this introduction we present the QHI and the structure of the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The QHI and their relations to other invariants
. The QHI refer to two main types of invariants:

•

either invariants of a pair
\displaystyle(N,L)
, where
\displaystyle N
is a compact oriented
\displaystyle 3
-manifold (with or without boundary) and
\displaystyle L\subset N
is a properly embedded tangle;

•

or invariants of a
cusped
manifold
\displaystyle M
, i.e.,
\displaystyle M
is an oriented non-compact
\displaystyle 3
-manifold admitting a complete hyperbolic structure with finite volume.

In both cases the manifold is endowed with additional geometric structures, including a
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-character of the fundamental group. We will refer to the first type as
QHI of pairs
. These QHI generalize the Kashaev invariants defined in
[
38
,
39
]
(see
[
3
,
6
]
). In particular the Kashaev invariants
\displaystyle\langle L\rangle_{N}
of a link
\displaystyle L
in
\displaystyle S^{3}
, which are specializations of the colored Jones polynomials (
[
45
]
), coincide with the QHI of the pair
\displaystyle(S^{3},L)
(
[
6
]
). The QHI of cusped manifolds have been defined in
[
4
]
, and further refined in
[
7
,
8
]
. By means of a hyperbolic Dehn filling theorem proved in
[
5
]
, we can relate some QHI of
\displaystyle M
with the QHI of pairs
\displaystyle(N,L)
where
\displaystyle N\setminus L=M
.

Both types of QHI are defined by state sums over triangulations very similar to Neumann’s simplicial formulas for the Chern-Simons invariant of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters (
[
48
]
); in these state sums the classical dilogarithm functions are replaced with tensors called “matrix” dilogarithms, satisfying a five term identity similar to the Roger’s one for dilogarithms. Because of this strong structural relationship, it is natural that expect that a “Volume Conjecture for the QHI” should occur, relating their semi-classical limit with the Chern-Simons invariant of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters. This was proposed in
[
2
]
; Theorem
1.1
(a) proves it for the figure-eight knot complement. Numerical evidences of the parity phenomenon (a) vs.​​ (b) in Theorem
1.1
were provided in
[
7
, Section 9]
also for the complement of the knot
\displaystyle 5_{2}
.

The QHI of mapping cylinders are easier to deal with, essentially because the mapping cylinder axis trivializes asymmetrical features of the QHI construction. It was shown in
[
9
]
that the
reduced
QHI (see Section
3.3
) of mapping tori of pseudo-Anosov diffeomorphisms of a punctured surface
\displaystyle S
are the trace of intertwiners between
local
representations of the quantum Teichmüller space of
\displaystyle S
(in the sense of
[
10
]
). This has been greatly clarified recently by Ishibashi (
[
35
]
), who provided, in particular, an alternative construction of these reduced QHI based on quantum cluster algebras associated to “dotted” ideal triangulations of
\displaystyle S
, in the sense of Kashaev (
[
41
]
). Also, Garoufalidis-Yu (
[
33
]
) obtained a decomposition of
these reduced QHI (in the case where the holonomy is boundary-parabolic and the log weight
\displaystyle l\kappa=0
) in terms of the Bonahon-Liu-Wong-Yang intertwiners of irreducible representations of the Kauffman bracket skein algebra of
\displaystyle S
(
[
16
]
,
[
18
, Theorem 16]
) and the
\displaystyle\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
-invariants of mapping classes studied by Gocho (
[
30
]
) and Murakami-Ohtsuki-Okada (
[
46
]
).

In particular, the results of this paper can be compared with those of Bonahon-Wong-Yang in
[
19
]
, which uses a different approach. In another direction, the Andersen–Kashaev
[
1
]
partition functions, which are derived from infinite dimensional quantum Teichmüller theory, make a kind of infinite dimensional version of the QHI and share many structural features with them. It is thus an interesting problem to clarify the relations between the results of this paper and those of Guéritaud, Piguet-Nakazawa and the second author in [11], and of Guilloux, Wong and the second author in [BAW, BAGW] concerning FAMED triangulations.

The relations between the QHI and the quantum invariants of Reshetikhin–Turaev or Turaev-Viro type are very intringuing. As mentioned above, the QHI of
\displaystyle(S^{3},L)
coincide with the Kashaev invariant
\displaystyle\langle 4_{1}\rangle_{N}
. As an example of relations between these invariants and the QHI of
\displaystyle S^{3}\setminus L
for a hyperbolic link
\displaystyle L
, we exhibit in Appendix
A
a discrete Laplace-transform type formula connecting the QHI of
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
and
\displaystyle\langle 4_{1}\rangle_{N}
. This formula seems to generalize to other knots. It may remind the reader of a conjecture of Andersen-Kashaev
[
1
, Conjecture 1]
. Clarifying such relationships could be useful to understand the Volume Conjecture for the colored Jones polynomials, since the QHI are intrinsically geometric, being rational functions on varieties covering the geometric component of the variety of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters (see the formula (
80
) and the comments thereafter).

Even more intringuing is the problem of extending Zagier’s quantum modularity conjecture (
[
64
,
34
]
) to the QHI.

In this direction, we note that a “parity” phenomenon similar to the alternative (a) vs.​​ (b) in Theorem
1.1
happens with the Turaev-Viro invariants
\displaystyle TV(M,q)
of cusped manifolds
\displaystyle M

[
21
]
, and with the colored Jones polynomials
\displaystyle J_{L,N}(q)
of hyperbolic links
\displaystyle L
at roots of unity
\displaystyle q

[
26
]
. Namely, it is conjectured that the sequence of invariants
\displaystyle\textstyle TV(M,\exp(\frac{2i\pi}{N}))
, with
\displaystyle N
odd, grows exponentially with growth rate Vol
\displaystyle(M)/2\pi
as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
, whereas
\displaystyle\textstyle TV(M,\exp(\frac{i\pi}{N}))
is expected to have polynomial growth (according to Witten’s asymptotic expansion conjecture). Similarly, it is expected that
\displaystyle\textstyle J_{L,N}(\exp(\frac{2i\pi}{N+1/2}))
has semi-classical limit Vol
\displaystyle(S^{3}\setminus L)/2\pi
, whereas it is known that for any integer
\displaystyle l
,
\displaystyle\textstyle J_{L,N}(\exp(\frac{2i\pi}{N+l}))
grows polynomially with
\displaystyle N
.

Plan of the paper.
In Section
1.2
we give a state sum formulation of Theorem
1.1
that may be more accessible to readers acquainted with the volume conjectures. Section
1.3
presents the structure of the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The geometric setup of the QHI is described in Section
2
. Sections
2.1
and
2.2
form a self-contained toolbox, providing material and, we hope, enough explanations in order to understand the QHI far beyond the case of
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
, which is developed in Section
2.3
. So Sections
2.1
and
2.2
could be skipped at a first reading. Also, Section
2.4
may be skipped at a first reading; it introduces a logarithmic limit space for the tower of quantum gluing varieties, which is useful to understand geometrically all asymptotical manipulations to follow.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
begins in Section
3
. Sections
3.1
and
3.2
provide basic but fundamental asymptotic results on the functions used in the QHI state sum formulas. Section
3.3
presents general features of the QHI of cusped manifolds; in particular, the notation
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
, which was not used previously in the literature on QHI, is explained. The combination of Sections
3.4
,
3.5
and
3.6
gives an integral representation of the QHI of
\displaystyle M:=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
.

The core of the analysis is developed in Section
4
and
5
. In Section
4
we consider as a warm-up situation the asymptotics of simplified, so called “classical” integrals. These integrals are defined in Section
4.1
, where it is also shown how the critical sets of the integrands recover a neighborhood of the geometric solution in the gluing variety. The rest of Section
4
is devoted to the asymptotics of the classical integrals for some specific choices of logarithmic limits of log-parameters, related to Theorem
1.1
. In Section
5
we adapt this analysis to the genuine, “quantum” integrals coming from the QHI of
\displaystyle M
.

Notations.
In all the paper
\displaystyle N
is an odd integer greater than
\displaystyle 1
. We put

\zeta:=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}}\ ,\ \mathbb{C}^{*}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\ ,\ \mathbb{C}_{*}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}.

We denote by
\displaystyle{\rm Log}
the complex (Neperian) logarithm with imaginary part in
\displaystyle]-\pi,\pi]
, and by
\displaystyle\arg(z)\in]-\pi,\pi]
the principal determination of the argument of
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{C}
. It is used in any evaluation of fractional or real powers of non-zero complex numbers; for instance
\displaystyle x^{1/N}:=\exp({\rm Log}(x)/N)
. Except for
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{*}
just defined, we reserve the notation
∗
to the complex conjugation. The real and imaginary parts of a complex number
\displaystyle z
are denoted
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(z)
. Finally, we denote by
\displaystyle=_{\mu_{N}}
the equality up to multiplication by
\displaystyle N
-th root of
\displaystyle 1
.

4.5.1
.

1.2.
Triangulation-version of Theorem
1.1

Again denote
\displaystyle M:=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
, the figure-eight knot complement. Let
\displaystyle(T,b)
be its usual geometric triangulation with two tetrahedra
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle V
, and with a
branching

\displaystyle b
(i.e. an orientation of edges without cycles) as shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
The face and edge identifications of a geometric branched ideal triangulation of the figure eight knot complement.

For the following notations, see Section
2.1
for details. Denote by
\displaystyle u=(u_{0},u_{1},u_{2})
and
\displaystyle v=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})
triples of complex shape parameters of ideal hyperbolic tetrahedra associated to the edges of
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle V
, respectively (so
\displaystyle u_{i}\in\mathbb{C}_{*}
,
\displaystyle u_{i+1}=(1-u_{i})^{-1}
, and similarly for the
\displaystyle v_{i}
’s, with indices mod
\displaystyle 3
). Assume that they satisfy Thurston’s gluing equations along the edges of
\displaystyle T
. Denote by
\displaystyle\rho\colon\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
the holonomy defined by
\displaystyle(u,v)
. For each odd integer
\displaystyle N\geqslant 3
, we take
\displaystyle N
-th roots
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{j,N}
and
\displaystyle{\bf v}_{j,N}
of
\displaystyle u_{j}
and
\displaystyle v_{j}
, respectively, thus getting new triples
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{N}:=({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf u}_{2,N})
and
\displaystyle{\bf v}_{N}=({\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{2,N})
. These
\displaystyle N
-th roots satisfy certain compatibility conditions.

The invariant
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
in Theorem
1.1
can be computed as the evaluation at some point
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N})
of a rational function
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{6}
. This function results from a
state sum
associated to
\displaystyle(T,b)
,
i.e.
, the total contraction of a tensor network, whose tensors correspond to the tetrahedra
\displaystyle U
,
\displaystyle V
(as is common in quantum topology) and are made of quantum dilogarithms. The function
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}
depends on a so-called charge
\displaystyle c
, which is a tuple of integers associated to the pairs of opposite edges of the tetrahedra
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle V
, again satisfying certain compatibility conditions. All these “certain compatibility conditions” are fixed by the choices of
\displaystyle h_{\rho}
and
\displaystyle k_{c}
in Theorem
1.1
. We can thus write

{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})={\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N}).

For details on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}
the most computation-oriented readers may want to look at the formulas (
81
) (where the charge
\displaystyle c
has been fixed to some specific value), (
46
) and (
82
).

We will see that Theorem
1.1
can be reduced to the following result, which will be our main objective:

Theorem 1.2

(Triangulation-version of Theorem
1.1
)
.

Let
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{N})_{N}
and
\displaystyle({\bf v}_{N})_{N}
be sequences as above. Then, for every charge
\displaystyle c
the state sum
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}
satisfies:

(a) If
\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf u}_{1,N}=-1
and
\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf v}_{1,N}=-1
, then

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}|{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N})|={\rm Vol}(M).

(b) If
\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf u}_{1,N}=1
and
\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf v}_{1,N}=1
, then

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}|{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N})|=0.

Moreover, (a) and (b) are the only two possibilities.

1.3.
Structure of the proof of Theorem
1.1

Let us denote by
\displaystyle PM(N)
(for
positive monomial
) the set of complex-valued functions of
\displaystyle N
of the form

\exp\left(O_{N\to\infty}(1)+\log(N)O_{N\to\infty}(1)\right).

In other words, a function
\displaystyle f(N)
is in
\displaystyle PM(N)
if there exist constants
\displaystyle C,C^{\prime}>0
and
\displaystyle\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that

CN^{\alpha}\leqslant|f(N)|\leqslant C^{\prime}N^{\beta}

for any
\displaystyle N
large enough.
Consequently, it satisfies
\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|f(N)\right|=0
.

The following basic lemma will be used several times in this paper.

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
\displaystyle f(N)
and
\displaystyle g(N)
be functions of
\displaystyle N
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|f(N)\right|=a>0
, and
\displaystyle g(N)
is in
\displaystyle PM(N)
. Then we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|g(N)\cdot f(N)\right|=a\quad,\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|f(N)+g(N)\right|=a.

Proof.

The first property is immediate. For the second one, note that since
\displaystyle a>0
we have
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{2}|f(N)|>|g(N)|
for any
\displaystyle N
large enough, and thus
\displaystyle\textstyle\left||f(N)|-\frac{|f(N)|}{2}\right|\leqslant\left|f(N)+g(N)\right|\leqslant\left|f(N)\right|+|f(N)|
by triangle inequalities; the limit follows.
∎

The proof of Theorem
1.1
takes the following steps I – X (for the sake of brevity we do not define all the terms that appear in the formulas):

I)

(On the QHI of cusped manifolds, Section
3.3
) This part recalls some features of the invariant
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
, using the structures introduced in Section
2
. In particular, we observe that:

•

(Formula (
80
))
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
can be split into the product of two invariants,
\displaystyle\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c};\mathfrak{s})
(the
symmetry defect
) and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
(the
reduced QHI
).

•

(Formula (
81
)) Refomulating this factorization on the triangulation
\displaystyle(T,b)
of
\displaystyle M:=S^{3}\setminus K
shown in Figure
1
, one can write the state sum
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N})
of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
(the subject of Theorem
1.2
) as

{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N})=({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})^{\frac{N-1}{2}}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N}).

II)

(Getting rid of the symmetry defect, Lemma
3.10
) With a quick computation, we check that
\displaystyle\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}(2\pi/N){\rm Log}\left|({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\right|=0
.

These two steps imply that in the proof of Theorem
1.2
we can replace
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N})
with
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})
. From known results it has the factorization formula (
82
):

{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})=\frac{g_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N})g_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*})^{*}}{|g_{N}(1)|^{2}}\ \Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})\ \Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*},{\bf v}_{1,N}^{*})^{*}.

III)

(Getting rid of
\displaystyle g_{N}(1)
,
\displaystyle g_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N})
and
\displaystyle g_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*})
, Formula (
68
) and Lemma
3.8
) With quick computations, we establish that
\displaystyle|g_{N}(1)|=\sqrt{N}
, and thus
\displaystyle g_{N}(1)
is in
\displaystyle PM(N)
. Also, by using a uniform estimate of the quantum dilogarithm functions (Lemma
3.3
) we show that in the situation of Theorem
1.2
the functions
\displaystyle g_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N})
and
\displaystyle g_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*})
are in
\displaystyle PM(N)
.

Together with the steps I-II, this further step implies that, in order to prove Theorem
1.2
, it is enough to show that
\displaystyle\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}(2\pi/N){\rm Log}\left|\Sigma_{N}(.,.)\right|
is equal to either
\displaystyle{\rm Vol}(M)/2
(case (a)) or
\displaystyle 0
(case (b)), both for
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1})
and for
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*},{\bf v}_{1,N}^{*})
.

IV)

(Writing
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}
as as an integral, Lemma
3.11
) Using properties of the quantum dilogarithm
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}(.)
, we re-write
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})
as in formula (
85
) :

\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})=1+\frac{1}{\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N-1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\beta^{2}-{\bf l}_{1}\beta}\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+2\beta i\pi/N),

where
\displaystyle({\bf l}_{k,N})
is a sequence of logarithms of
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{k,N})
of a specific form. Using the residue theorem, we re-write this formula as in (
88
):

\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})=1+\frac{1}{\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})}\frac{N}{4i\pi}\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz

where
\displaystyle C_{N}
is a closed rectangular contour circling the segment
\displaystyle\textstyle[\frac{2i\pi}{N},2i\pi-\frac{2i\pi}{N}]
and close to it. We denote by
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
and
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}
its intersection points with
\displaystyle i\mathbb{R}
; by construction
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{\pm}:=\alpha^{\pm}\frac{\pi}{N}
with
\displaystyle\alpha^{\pm}\in]0,2[
.

V)

(Getting rid of
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})
, Lemma
3.8
) With properties of the quantum dilogarithms, we establish that
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})
is in
\displaystyle PM(N)
.

VI)

(Equivalence of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
, Section
3.6
) Using the description of the quantum gluing variety in Section
2.3
, we show that the two cases (a) and (b) in Theorem
1.1
correspond to the cases (a) and (b) in Theorem
1.2
. Moreover, in the above integral these cases correspond to having
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k,\infty}:=\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf l}_{k,N}
such that
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=i\pi
, and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=2i\pi
respectively. The same facts occur for
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0}^{*},{\bf v}_{1}^{*})
.

We then turn to the semi-classical limit of the integral

(1)

\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz.

Somewhat counter-intuitively, we begin with simpler integrals, over the segment
\displaystyle[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]
instead of
\displaystyle C_{N}
, where we fix
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{-}=\alpha^{-}\frac{\pi}{N}
,
\displaystyle\alpha^{-}\in]0,1[
(whereas
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{+}=\alpha^{+}\frac{\pi}{N}
has
\displaystyle\alpha^{+}\in]0,2[
arbitrary), and with integrands defined by using functions
\displaystyle f_{\pm}(z)
, depending on
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}
, which can be regarded as semi-classical approximations of the above integrand when
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=i\pi
or
\displaystyle 2i\pi
.

VII)

(Saddle point method on a classical integral: contour
\displaystyle[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]
, case (a):
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=i\pi
, sections
4.3
and
4.4
) In this case we prove that

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|\int_{I_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{-}(z)}dz\right|=\frac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M)\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ \int_{I_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz=O_{N\rightarrow+\infty}(1).

VIII)

(Saddle point method on a classical integral: contour
\displaystyle[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]
, case (b):
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=2i\pi
, sections
4.3
and
4.5
) In this case we prove that

\int_{I_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{\pm}(z)}dz=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{\pm}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{\pm}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1))

where
\displaystyle\text{Constant}_{-}
,
\displaystyle\text{Constant}_{+}
are distinct non-zero complex numbers and
\displaystyle r_{+},r_{-}\in\mathbb{R}
are distinct as well (see (
117
)-(
118
)).

IX)

(Lemma
5.1
) We split the contour
\displaystyle C_{N}
in its subcontours
\displaystyle C_{N}^{+}
,
\displaystyle C_{N}^{-}
lying in the right and left half planes, and we show that the integral (
1
) is a linear combination of integrals of the form

\int_{C_{N}^{+}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{\pm}(z)}\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)dz,

where the functions
\displaystyle f_{\pm}
are as in steps VII and VIII, and
\displaystyle\rho(z)
,
\displaystyle\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)
and
\displaystyle R_{N}(z)
are holomorphic functions of
\displaystyle z
in a domain containing the strip
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}+i]0,2\pi[
with some cuts, with moduli bounded by constants independent of
\displaystyle N
on compact subsets of this strip with cuts. The function
\displaystyle\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)
measures
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)e^{-\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{\pm}(z)}
; its properties are studied in Lemma
3.5
.

X)

(Lemma
5.5
and Corollary
5.7
) By deforming the contours
\displaystyle C_{N}^{\pm}
to
\displaystyle[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]
relatively to the endpoints, one checks that the analysis of steps VII and VIII can be adapted to describe the asymptotics of the integrals of steps IX, and gives similar semi-classical asymptotics. Then, we can deduce easily that

\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz\right|

and finally

\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})\right|\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|\Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*},{\bf u}_{1,N}^{*})\right|

tend to either
\displaystyle{\rm Vol}(M)/2
when
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=i\pi
(case (a)), or to
\displaystyle 0
when
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=2i\pi
(case (b)).

By the conclusions of steps III and VI, this will complete the proof.

Remark 1.4
.

The factorization formula of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})
in step II is specific to the QHI of
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus K
. On another hand, the integral representation in step IV immediately generalizes to the QHI state sums of arbitrary cusped manifolds, as well as the steps I, III and V.

2.
Geometric setup

We introduce in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
the material required to define the reduced QHI state sums associated to the geometric ideal triangulations of cusped manifolds which support a branching. The case of the figure-eight knot complement is developed in Section
2.3
. The generalization to arbitrary (
i.e.
, not necessarily branched) geometric ideal triangulations, possibly with some flat tetrahedra (see Definition
2.2
), is described in
[
7
,
8
]
. Though any cusped manifold has a geometric ideal triangulation (see Section
2.1.2
), to our knowledge it is still an open question whether or not there is a branched geometric one. For hyperbolic links in
\displaystyle S^{3}
which have a reduced alternating diagram, the answer is positive; this follows from the main result of
[
32
,
56
]
.

In this section an odd integer
\displaystyle N\geq 3
is fixed. Therefore we will systematically omit the subscript “
\displaystyle N
” from the notation of the quantum parameters, thus hiding their dependence on
\displaystyle N
(as they are
\displaystyle N
-th roots). Hence we write
\displaystyle{\bf w}_{0}
for
\displaystyle{\bf w}_{0,N}
, and only bold characters indicate the quantum nature of the parameters.

2.1.
The quantum gluing varieties

These form the parameter spaces of the QHI state sum formulas.

2.1.1.
Quantum
\displaystyle 3
-simplices

(see
[
7
]
, Section 3) A
quantum
\displaystyle 3
-simplex
is a tuple
\displaystyle(\Delta,b,{\bf w})
where:

•

\displaystyle(\Delta,b)
is an oriented tetrahedron with ordered vertices, say
\displaystyle v_{0},\ldots,v_{3}
. The letter “
\displaystyle b
” denotes the system of edge orientations, called
branching
, which encodes the vertex ordering and is defined by the rule: an edge with endpoints
\displaystyle v_{i}
and
\displaystyle v_{j}
points towards
\displaystyle v_{j}
if
\displaystyle j>i
.

We call
\displaystyle(\Delta,b)
a
branched tetrahedron
. It has a natural
\displaystyle b
-orientation
, defined by the ordered triple of oriented edges
\displaystyle(v_{0}v_{1})
,
\displaystyle(v_{0}v_{2})
and
\displaystyle(v_{0}v_{3})
. We encode this
\displaystyle b
-orientation by a sign
\displaystyle*_{b}(\Delta)\in\{-1,+1\}
:
\displaystyle*_{b}(\Delta)=+1
if the
\displaystyle b
-orientation coincides with the given orientation of
\displaystyle\Delta
,
\displaystyle*_{b}(\Delta)=-1
otherwise.

•

\displaystyle{\bf w}:=({\bf w}_{0},{\bf w}_{1},{\bf w}_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
satisfies the following requirements:

(a)
\displaystyle w:=(w_{0},w_{1},w_{2}):=({\bf w}_{0}^{N},{\bf w}_{1}^{N},{\bf w}_{2}^{N})\in(\mathbb{C}_{*})^{3}
, and we have the relations

(2)

w_{i+1}=\frac{1}{1-w_{i}}\quad{\rm(indices\ mod}(3));

(b) We have

(3)

{\bf w}_{0}{\bf w}_{1}{\bf w}_{2}=e^{-*_{b}(\Delta)\frac{\pi i}{N}}.

We call
\displaystyle w_{i}
and
\displaystyle{\bf w}_{i}
a
shape parameter
and a
quantum shape parameter
, respectively.

One can regard
\displaystyle w_{0},w_{1},w_{2}
as the three cross ratios of a
\displaystyle 4
-tuple of points in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}=\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
, ordered up to even permutations. The triple
\displaystyle(w_{0},w_{1},w_{2})
up to cyclic permutations parametrizes the equivalence class of the
\displaystyle 4
-tuple up to the action of Moebius transformations on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
; equivalently,
\displaystyle(w_{0},w_{1},w_{2})
up to cyclic permutations parametrizes an orientation preserving isometry class of
\displaystyle 3
-dimensional hyperbolic ideal tetrahedra (see
e.g.

[
14
, Chapter E.6-i]
). Each shape parameter is naturally assigned to a specific pair of opposite edges of
\displaystyle(\Delta,b)
:
\displaystyle w_{0}
to the edges
\displaystyle(v_{0}v_{1})
and
\displaystyle(v_{2}v_{3})
,
\displaystyle w_{1}
to the edges
\displaystyle(v_{1}v_{2})
and
\displaystyle(v_{0}v_{3})
, and
\displaystyle w_{2}
to the edges
\displaystyle(v_{0}v_{2})
and
\displaystyle(v_{1}v_{3})
. This way, e.g.,
\displaystyle w_{0}
computes the dilation coefficient of the loxodromic element in
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
fixing
\displaystyle v_{0}
and
\displaystyle v_{1}
and mapping
\displaystyle v_{2}
to
\displaystyle v_{3}
.

Therefore a quantum
\displaystyle 3
-simplex
\displaystyle(\Delta,b,{\bf w})
encodes an hyperbolic ideal
\displaystyle 3
-simplex up to isometry, plus a choice of
\displaystyle N
-th root
\displaystyle{\bf w}_{i}
of each shape parameter
\displaystyle w_{i}
, assigned to the same pair of opposite edges of
\displaystyle(\Delta,b)
, as described above.

Let
\displaystyle(\Delta,b,{\bf w})
be a quantum
\displaystyle 3
-simplex. We can write the quantum shape parameters in the form

(4)

{\bf w}_{k}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(w_{k})+2\pi ie_{k})\right),

where
\displaystyle e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}/N
satisfy

(5)

\sum_{k=0,1,2}({\rm Log}(w_{k})+2\pi ie_{k})=-*_{b}\!(\Delta)\pi i\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi iN).

We call
\displaystyle e_{k}
a
(edge)
\displaystyle N
-color
. Alternatively, since
\displaystyle N
is odd the map
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
,
\displaystyle d\mapsto\textstyle\overline{\frac{(N+1)}{2}d}
, is well-defined and surjective. Therefore it is also possible to write

(6)

{\bf w}_{k}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\left({\rm Log}(w_{k})+\pi i(N+1)d_{k}\right)\right),

where
\displaystyle d_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
and

(7)

\sum_{k=0,1,2}({\rm Log}(w_{k})+\pi id_{k})=-*_{b}\!(\Delta)\pi i.

The usefulness of the expression (
6
) will be explained in Remark
2.4
. We call
\displaystyle d_{k}
a
(edge) color
, and
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(w_{k})+\pi id_{k}
a
log-parameter
. Since the shape parameters
\displaystyle w_{k}
have imaginary parts of the same sign and
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{k=0,1,2}{\rm Log}(w_{k})={\rm sign}(\mathfrak{I}(w_{0}))\pi i
, we have

(8)

d_{0}+d_{1}+d_{2}=-*_{b}\!(\Delta)-{\rm sign}(\mathfrak{I}(w_{0}))\in\{-2,0,2\}.

2.1.2.
Gluing varieties and the holonomy map

In this section we explain how the structures carried by quantum
\displaystyle 3
-simplices can be globalized on ideal triangulations.

Let
\displaystyle M
be a cusped manifold, i.e. an oriented non-compact
\displaystyle 3
-manifold admitting a complete hyperbolic metric of finite volume. We denote by
\displaystyle\bar{M}
the compact manifold with interior
\displaystyle M
;
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
is a union of
\displaystyle p
tori.

Let
\displaystyle T
be an ideal triangulation of
\displaystyle M
, i.e. a gluing by simplicial face pairings of tetrahedra with truncated (“ideal”) vertices, homeomorphic to
\displaystyle M
.

Denote by
\displaystyle\Delta^{1},\ldots,\Delta^{n}
the tetrahedra of
\displaystyle T
, with the orientation induced from
\displaystyle M
. We call
abstract tetrahedra
and
abstract edges
of
\displaystyle T
the tetrahedra
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
and their edges before their gluings in
\displaystyle T
. Give each tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
a branching
\displaystyle b^{i}
, as defined in Section
2.1.1
. Denote by
\displaystyle b
the collection of these local branchings
\displaystyle b^{i}
. We do not ask that they match along common edges (so we can choose each
\displaystyle b^{i}
so that
\displaystyle*_{b^{i}}(\Delta^{i})=1
). We note however that for such global branchings
\displaystyle b
, when they exist (like for
\displaystyle(T,b)
in Figure 1), the QHI state sums have the simplest form (
[
7
]
).

Definition 2.1
.

The
quantum gluing variety

\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)
is the set of tuples
\displaystyle{\bf w}:=({\bf w}^{1},\ldots,{\bf w}^{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{3n}
, where
\displaystyle{\bf w}^{i}=({\bf w}_{0}^{i},{\bf w}_{1}^{i},{\bf w}_{2}^{i})
is a triple of quantum shape parameters on
\displaystyle(\Delta^{i},b^{i})
,
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,n
, as in Section
2.1.1
, satisfying the following
edge relation
at each edge
\displaystyle e
of
\displaystyle T
:

(9)

\prod_{E\rightarrow e}{\bf w}(E)^{*_{E}(\Delta)}=e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}}.

Here the product is over all the abstract edges
\displaystyle E
identified to
\displaystyle e
in
\displaystyle T
(which is denoted
\displaystyle E\rightarrow e
),
\displaystyle{\bf w}(E)
is the quantum shape parameter of
\displaystyle E
in the corresponding tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
, and
\displaystyle*_{E}(\Delta)
is its branching sign.

Note that the branching
\displaystyle b
enters the definition of
\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)
only in that it specifies a concrete model of the space in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{3n}
. Different branchings yield isomorphic spaces. In
[
7
, Section 3B and 4C]
the space
\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)
was denoted
\displaystyle G_{0}(T,b,c)_{N}
.

For
\displaystyle N=1
,
\displaystyle G(T,b):=G_{1}(T,b)
is Thurston’s gluing variety associated to
\displaystyle T
, since in this case
\displaystyle{\bf w}^{i}=w^{i}
is a triple of shape parameters
\displaystyle(w_{0}^{i},w_{1}^{i},w_{2}^{i})
for the tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
, satisfying the edge relations
\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{E\rightarrow e}w(E)^{*_{E}(\Delta)}=1
(with the notations of (
9
)).

It is a well-known fact that
\displaystyle G(T,b)\neq\emptyset
if all the edges
\displaystyle e
of
\displaystyle T
are
essential
,
i.e.
,
\displaystyle e\cap\bar{M}
is not homotopic to an arc in
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
by a homotopy fixing the endpoints. In that case we have
\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)\neq\emptyset
(see Theorem
2.7
). Moreover, denote by
\displaystyle\tilde{M}
the universal cover of
\displaystyle M
. By “straightening” the tetrahedra of
\displaystyle T
by means of an isometry
\displaystyle\tilde{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
, one can see that if
\displaystyle G(T,b)\neq\emptyset
, then the character
\displaystyle\rho_{hyp}
of the discrete faithful representations of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(M)
into
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
is encoded by a point of
\displaystyle G(T,b)
.

More generally, when
\displaystyle G(T,b)
is non-empty it is related to the variety
\displaystyle X(M)
of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters of
\displaystyle M
as follows (see
e.g.

[
49
,
14
,
44
]
). Recall that
\displaystyle X(M)
is the affine algebraic set consisting of representations
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
up to the equivalence relation of having the same trace on each element of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(M)
. Denote by
\displaystyle\tilde{M}
the universal cover of
\displaystyle M
. Any point
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
determines a
pseudo-developing map

\displaystyle D_{w}\colon\tilde{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
, unique up to post-composition with an isometry, which sends a lift
\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}^{i}
of the tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
to a hyperbolic ideal tetrahedron with shape parameters
\displaystyle(w_{0}^{i},w_{1}^{i},w_{2}^{i})
. A pseudo-developing map
\displaystyle D_{w}
defines a representation
\displaystyle\rho_{w}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
by
\displaystyle D_{w}(\gamma.x)=\rho_{w}(\gamma)D_{w}(x)
for all
\displaystyle\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
,
\displaystyle x\in\tilde{M}
. Different choices of developing maps
\displaystyle D_{w}
yield conjugate representations
\displaystyle\rho_{w}
. The resulting holonomy map (denoting again by
\displaystyle\rho_{w}
the character of a representation
\displaystyle\rho_{w}
)

(10)

\begin{array}[]{lcll}hol:&G(T,b)&\longrightarrow&X(M)\\
&w&\longmapsto&\rho_{w}\end{array}

is regular, and generically
\displaystyle 2:1
. More will be said about it in Section
2.2.2
. For the moment, let us note that in general
\displaystyle hol
is not surjective (for instance, by the construction of pseudo-developing maps it is not hard to see that the image of
\displaystyle hol
cannot contain any character of reducible representations).

The Thurston gluing variety
\displaystyle G(T,b)
and the map
\displaystyle hol
have good properties when
\displaystyle T
is a geometric triangulation, in the following sense.

Definition 2.2
.

We say that
\displaystyle T
is a
geometric triangulation
of
\displaystyle M
when:

•

either the edges of
\displaystyle T
are isotopic to geodesics in the complete hyperbolic structure of
\displaystyle M
, and these geodesics triangulate
\displaystyle M
into tetrahedra with non zero volume and disjoint interiors;

•

or
\displaystyle T
is obtained by subdividing the Epstein-Penner canonical polyhedral decomposition of
\displaystyle M
(then some tetrahedra may be flat,
i.e.
, of zero volume).

In the former case we say that
\displaystyle T
is a
positive
triangulation, as it can be isotoped to a gluing of positively oriented hyperbolic ideal tetrahedra corresponding to the
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
, with classical shape parameters satisfying
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(w_{hyp}(E)^{*_{E}(\Delta)})>0
for every abstract edge
\displaystyle E
of
\displaystyle T
. Such triangulations are produced, e.g., by the software program SnapPy
[
24
]
. In general, when
\displaystyle T
is geometric there exists a point
\displaystyle w_{hyp}\in G(T,b)
such that

hol(w_{hyp})=\rho_{hyp},\ {\rm and\ }\mathfrak{I}(w_{hyp}(E)^{*_{E}(\Delta)})\geq 0\ {\rm for\ every\ abstract\ edge\ }E\ {\rm of\ }T.

For a geometric triangulation
\displaystyle T
the following facts hold true (see
[
49
]
,
[
14
, Chapter E.6 and Proposition 7.7]
,
[
53
]
and
[
52
, Proposition 3.5-3.6]
,
[
22
]
and
[
44
, Proposition 15.3.4]
):

(i)

\displaystyle{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(G(T,b))=p
, the number of cusps of
\displaystyle M
(this is also the dimension of the geometric component
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
of
\displaystyle X(M)
, see Section
2.2.2
);

(ii)

there is a unique point
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
as above, it is a smooth point of
\displaystyle G(T,b)
, and a neighborhood of
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
parametrizes a space of (non complete) hyperbolic structures on
\displaystyle M
.

Now let
\displaystyle w:=(w^{1},\ldots,w^{n})\in G(T,b)
, where
\displaystyle w^{i}:=(w_{0}^{i},w_{1}^{i},w_{2}^{i})
as usual.

Definition 2.3
.

We say that a tuple
\displaystyle d:=(d^{1},\ldots,d^{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3n}
is a
system of (edge) colors
for
\displaystyle w
if:

•

for every tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
of
\displaystyle T
,
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,n
, we have

(11)

\sum_{k=0,1,2}({\rm Log}(w_{k}^{i})+\pi id_{k}^{i})=-*_{b}\!(\Delta)\pi i

•

for every edge
\displaystyle e
of
\displaystyle T
we have (with notations similar to (
9
)):

(12)

\sum_{E\rightarrow e}*_{E}(\Delta)({\rm Log}(w(E))+\pi id(E))=-2\pi i.

In such a situation we say that
\displaystyle d
and
\displaystyle w
are
compatible
.

The existence and the structure of the set of compatible pairs
\displaystyle(w,d)
is discussed in Section
2.2
. By means of the formula (
6
) relating
\displaystyle d_{k}^{i}
,
\displaystyle w_{k}^{i}
and
\displaystyle{\bf w}_{k}^{i}
for every
\displaystyle i
and
\displaystyle k
, to any compatible pair
\displaystyle(w,d)
we can associate a point
\displaystyle{\bf w}:=({\bf w}^{1},\ldots,{\bf w}^{n})\in G_{N}(T,b)
, where
\displaystyle{\bf w}^{i}:=({\bf w}_{0}^{i},{\bf w}_{1}^{i},{\bf w}_{2}^{i})
. We say that
\displaystyle(w,d)
and
\displaystyle{\bf w}
are compatible.

We can also consider
systems of (edge)
\displaystyle N
-colors
,
i.e.
, tuples
\displaystyle e:=(e^{1},\ldots,e^{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/N)^{3n}
, where
\displaystyle e^{i}=(e_{0}^{i},e_{1}^{i},e_{2}^{i})
, satisfying the relations obtained from (
11
)-(
12
) by replacing
\displaystyle d_{k}^{i}
and
\displaystyle d(E)
with
\displaystyle 2e_{k}^{i}
and
\displaystyle 2e(E)
and working mod
\displaystyle(2\pi iN)
; clearly the pairs
\displaystyle(w,e)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the points of
\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)
.

Remark 2.4
.

The log-parameters in (
7
) are intrinsic to Neumann’s simplicial formulas (
[
48
]
) of the Chern-Simons invariant of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters. Indeed, these simplicial formulas are given by functions of the shape parameters
\displaystyle w=(w_{k}^{i})
and so-called
combinatorial flattenings

\displaystyle f=(f_{k}^{i})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3n}
, which are defined by the following relations, for every tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
and every edge
\displaystyle e
of
\displaystyle T
respectively:

(13)

\sum_{k=0,1,2}({\rm Log}(w_{k}^{i})+\pi if_{k}^{i})=0\ ,\ \sum_{E\rightarrow e}*_{E}(\Delta)({\rm Log}(w(E))+\pi if(E))=0.

The colors in (
7
) can be written

(14)

d_{k}^{i}=f_{k}^{i}-*_{b}(\Delta)c_{k}^{i},

where
\displaystyle c=(c_{k}^{i})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3n}
is a topological version of combinatorial flattenings, introduced in
[
47
]
and that we call a charge; by definition, it satisfies for every tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}
and every edge
\displaystyle e
of
\displaystyle T
the equations

\sum_{k=0,1,2}c_{k}^{i}=1\ ,\ \sum_{E\rightarrow e}c(E)=2.

2.2.
Weights

The structure of the set of systems of edge colors
\displaystyle d
compatible with a point
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
follows from Neumann’s theory of flattenings
[
48
,
47
]
. This set is a disjoint union of affine spaces over
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}
-lattices determined by two pairs of cohomology classes on
\displaystyle M
and
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
, that we call bulk weights and log weights. We refer to
[
7
, Section 4C]
for a complete description of these lattices; examples are given in
[
5
, Section 6.4]
,
[
7
, Section 9]
, and
[
8
, Section 9]
(in
[
8
, Definition 1.7]
we called the log weight (
18
) below a “fused weight”). For systems of
\displaystyle N
-edge colors things go similarly. In this section we describe the weights by limiting our discussion to what is strictly necessary for this paper.

2.2.1.
Log weights and boundary weights

As usual denote by
\displaystyle\bar{M}
the compact manifold with interior
\displaystyle M
; so
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
is a disjoint union of
\displaystyle c
tori. Recall the holonomy map (
10
).

It is well-known that, besides the holonomy
\displaystyle\rho_{w}
, a point
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
determines a group homomorphism

(15)

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\delta_{w}:&H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{C}^{*}\\
&\gamma&\longmapsto&\delta_{w}(\gamma).\end{array}

Thus we have a map
\displaystyle\delta\colon G(T,b)\rightarrow H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
,
\displaystyle w\mapsto\delta_{w}
. We call
\displaystyle\delta
the
dilation factor
. The scalar
\displaystyle\delta_{w}(\gamma)
is the square of one among the two (reciprocally inverse) eigenvalues of
\displaystyle\rho_{w}(\gamma)
, and can be computed as a signed monomial in the classical shape parameters
\displaystyle w^{i}_{k}
(see
[
49
]
, or the formulas in
[
14
, Lemma E.6.8]
; these formulas immediately follow from (
22
) below). If
\displaystyle T
is a geometric triangulation, as defined in Section
2.1.2
, and
\displaystyle w
is close enough to the point
\displaystyle w_{hyp}\in G(T,b)
, then
\displaystyle\delta_{w}
is the holonomy of a similarity structure on
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
(see, e.g.,
[
14
, Proposition E.6.5]
,
[
52
, Proposition 2.3]
).

Similarly, by adjoining to
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
a compatible system of edge colors
\displaystyle d
, one can define two classes
\displaystyle h_{2}\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
and
\displaystyle l\kappa\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C})
, such that for all
\displaystyle\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
we have (where
\displaystyle j^{*}\colon H^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{Z}/2)\rightarrow H^{1}(\partial\bar{M},\mathbb{Z}/2)
is induced by the inclusion
\displaystyle j\colon\partial\bar{M}\rightarrow\bar{M}
):

(16)

\displaystyle\displaystyle l\kappa(\gamma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\rm Log}(\delta_{w}(\gamma))\quad{\rm mod}(i\pi\mathbb{Z})

(17)

\displaystyle\displaystyle j^{*}(h_{2})(\gamma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(l\kappa(\gamma)-{\rm Log}(\delta_{w}(\gamma)))/i\pi\quad{\rm mod}(2\mathbb{Z}).

Let us explain this. To define the classes
\displaystyle l\kappa
and
\displaystyle h_{2}
, first we stipulate that
\displaystyle l\kappa(0):=0
and
\displaystyle h_{2}(0):=0
, for the
\displaystyle 0
classes in
\displaystyle H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
or
\displaystyle H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
respectively. Given a non-zero class
\displaystyle\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
, we represent it by a union of oriented simple closed curves in
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
which are essential (
i.e.
, do not bound a disk) and transverse to the
\displaystyle 1
-skeleton of
\displaystyle\partial T
, the triangulation of
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
induced by
\displaystyle T
. We can assume that these curves have no “back-tracking”,
i.e.
, they intersect each triangle of
\displaystyle\partial T
in a disjoint union of segments. Call such curves
normal curves
in
\displaystyle\partial T
. If a triangle
\displaystyle F
of
\displaystyle\partial T
is a section of the tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta
of
\displaystyle T
near one of its vertices, for every vertex
\displaystyle v
of
\displaystyle F
denote by
\displaystyle E_{v}
the abstract edge of
\displaystyle\Delta
containing
\displaystyle v
, by
\displaystyle w(E_{v})
the classical shape parameter of
\displaystyle E_{v}
, by
\displaystyle d(E_{v})
its color, and by
\displaystyle*_{v}
the branching sign of
\displaystyle\Delta
. Given a union of normal curves
\displaystyle C
, we write
\displaystyle F\colon C\rightarrow E_{v}
to mean that
\displaystyle C
intersects the triangle
\displaystyle F
in subsegments joining the two edges of
\displaystyle F
having
\displaystyle v
has endpoint. We count these subsegments algebraically, by using the orientation of
\displaystyle C
: if there are
\displaystyle s_{+}
(resp.
\displaystyle s_{-}
) such segments whose orientation is compatible with (resp. opposite to) the orientation of
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
as viewed from
\displaystyle v
, then we set
\displaystyle ind(C,v):=s_{+}-s_{-}
. Then, for any non zero class
\displaystyle\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
represented by a union of normal curves
\displaystyle C
in
\displaystyle\partial T
, we set (the sum is over all the triangles of
\displaystyle\partial T
)

(18)

l\kappa(\gamma):=\sum_{F\colon C\rightarrow E_{v}}*_{v}\ ind(C,v)\left({\rm Log}(w(E_{v}))+\pi\sqrt{-1}d(E_{v})\right).

Similarly, we define the non oriented normal curves in
\displaystyle T
as transverse to the
\displaystyle 2
-skeleton and intersecting each tetrahedron in a disjoint union of segments. For any normal curve in
\displaystyle T
, we write
\displaystyle\Delta\colon C\rightarrow E
to mean that
\displaystyle C
intersects the tetrahedron
\displaystyle\Delta
in subsegments joining the faces of
\displaystyle\Delta
adjacent to an abstract edge
\displaystyle E
of
\displaystyle\Delta
. For any such a tetrahedron, we denote by
\displaystyle ind(C,E)
the number of these segments, and by
\displaystyle d(E)
the color of
\displaystyle E
. Then, for any non-zero class
\displaystyle\gamma\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
represented by a union of normal curves
\displaystyle C
in
\displaystyle T
we have (the sum is over all the tetrahedra of
\displaystyle T
)

(19)

h_{2}(\gamma)=\sum_{\Delta\colon C\rightarrow E}\ ind(C,E)d(E)\quad{\rm mod}(2\mathbb{Z}).

We call
\displaystyle l\kappa
the
log weight
of
\displaystyle(w,d)
, and
\displaystyle h_{2}
the
bulk weight
. In the particular case where
\displaystyle M
is the complement of a knot
\displaystyle K
in
\displaystyle S^{3}
,
\displaystyle H^{1}(\bar{M};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
is generated by the meridian of
\displaystyle K
and therefore
\displaystyle h_{2}
is determined by
\displaystyle l\kappa
(and it vanishes on the canonical longitude).

Remark 2.5
.

One can associate classes
\displaystyle k_{f}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C})
and
\displaystyle k_{c}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
(and similarly classes
\displaystyle h_{2,f}
,
\displaystyle h_{2,c}\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
), respectively called
flattening weight
and
charge weight
, to flattenings
\displaystyle f
and charge
\displaystyle c
as in Remark
2.4
. Namely,
\displaystyle k_{f}
is defined by the formula obtained from (
18
) by replacing the color
\displaystyle d(E_{v})
with the flattening
\displaystyle f(E_{v})
, and
\displaystyle k_{c}
by the formula obtained from (
18
) by replacing
\displaystyle*_{v}({\rm Log}(w(E_{v}))+\pi\sqrt{-1}d(E_{v}))
with the charge
\displaystyle c(E_{v})
. Note that

(20)

k_{f}(\gamma)={\rm Log}(\delta_{w}(\gamma))\quad{\rm mod}(i\pi\mathbb{Z}).

Clearly

(21)

l\kappa=k_{f}-\pi ik_{c}.

The following statement follows from results in
[
47
,
48
]
. In the present notations, the first claim is Propositions 4.8 (2) and 4.9 (2) of
[
7
]
, formulated for
\displaystyle l\kappa=k_{f}-\pi ik_{c}
. The generators in the second claim follow from the last claim of Lemma 6.1 in
[
47
]
, and the rank is a standard computation in the Neumann-Zagier combinatorics (
[
49
]
).

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
. For any classes
\displaystyle h_{2}\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
and
\displaystyle l\kappa\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C})
satisfying (
16
) and (
17
) there is a system of edge colors
\displaystyle d
satisfying (
18
) and (
19
). Moreover, the set formed by these systems of edge colors is an affine space over a
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}
-lattice of rank
\displaystyle n-p
(where
\displaystyle p
is the number of cusps) generated by vectors in one-to-one correspondence with the edges of
\displaystyle T
.

In such a situation we say that
\displaystyle(h_{2},l\kappa)
and
\displaystyle(w,d)
, or
\displaystyle(h_{2},l\kappa)
and the corresponding tuple of quantum shape parameters
\displaystyle{\bf w}\in G_{N}(T,b)
defined by the formula (
6
), are
compatible
. For simplicity we will often omit
\displaystyle d
from the notations, and say that
\displaystyle l\kappa
and
\displaystyle w
, or
\displaystyle l\kappa
and
\displaystyle{\bf w}
, or
\displaystyle l\kappa
and the
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-character
\displaystyle\rho_{w}=hol(w)
, are compatible. For a link complement
\displaystyle M
the map
\displaystyle j^{*}\colon H^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{Z}/2)\rightarrow H^{1}(\partial\bar{M},\mathbb{Z}/2)
is an isomorphism, so
\displaystyle h_{2}
is given by
\displaystyle l\kappa
and
\displaystyle\delta_{w}
via the relation (
17
).

Similarly to (
18
), denoting by
\displaystyle{\bf w}(E_{v})
the quantum shape parameter of an abstract edge
\displaystyle E_{v}
, we can associate to any point
\displaystyle{\bf w}\in G_{N}(T,b)
a class
\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf w}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
defined by

(22)

\kappa_{\bf w}(\gamma):=\prod_{F\colon C\rightarrow E_{v}}{\bf w}(E_{v})^{*_{v}ind(C,v)}.

We call
\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf w}
the
boundary weight
of
\displaystyle{\bf w}
. Clearly
\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf w}(\gamma)^{N}=\delta_{w}(\gamma)
, and if
\displaystyle d\in\mathbb{Z}^{3n}
is a compatible tuple of edge colors with bulk and log weights
\displaystyle h_{2}
and
\displaystyle l\kappa
, for every
\displaystyle\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
we have

(23)

\kappa_{\bf w}(\gamma)=\exp\left(\frac{l\kappa(\gamma)}{N}+(\pi i)j^{*}(h_{2})(\gamma)\right).

Consider the covering map

(24)

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\pi_{N}:&G_{N}(T,b)&\longrightarrow&G(T,b)\\
&{\bf w}=({\bf w}_{k}^{i})&\longmapsto&w=(({\bf w}_{k}^{i})^{N}).\end{array}

Theorem
2.6
(or its proof, using systems of
\displaystyle N
-colors) implies:

Theorem 2.7
.

The map
\displaystyle\pi_{N}
has degree
\displaystyle N^{n+p}
.

Note that the exponent decomposes as
\displaystyle n+p=n-p+2p
, where
\displaystyle n-p
follows from Theorem
2.6
, and
\displaystyle 2p
comes from the degree of freedom in the choice of
\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf w}
.

2.2.2.
The spaces
\displaystyle G_{hyp,N}(T,b)
and
\displaystyle{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)

(See
[
7
, Section 4]
.) Let
\displaystyle T
be a geometric triangulation of
\displaystyle M
. We saw in Section
2.1.2
that
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
is a smooth point of
\displaystyle G(T,b)
. Denote its irreducible component by
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
. In this section we discuss some of its remarkable properties, and explain how the map (
24
) can be given an intrinsic definition over
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
.

We need to recall the following results:

(a) The character
\displaystyle\rho_{hyp}
of the discrete faithful representations of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(M)
into
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
is a smooth point of
\displaystyle X(M)
, and thus is contained in a unique irreducible component
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
of
\displaystyle X(M)
; moreover
\displaystyle{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(X_{hyp}(M))=p
, the number of cusps of
\displaystyle M
(see
[
54
, Chapter 4.5]
,
[
37
, Theorem 8.44]
). We call
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
the
geometric component
of
\displaystyle X(M)
.

(b) Dunfield
[
27
]
showed that the (regular) restriction map
\displaystyle r\colon X_{hyp}(M)\rightarrow X(\partial\bar{M})
is a birational isomorphism onto its image, in the case when
\displaystyle M
has a single cusp. This result has been extended by Klaff-Tillmann
[
42
]
to an arbitrary number of cusps.

(c) The
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters of
\displaystyle M
can be “augmented” (
[
20
,
17
]
): an augmented character
\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}
consists of a character
\displaystyle\rho\in X(M)
together with a conjugation invariant choice of a fixed point of
\displaystyle\rho(\pi_{1}(\partial\bar{M}))
, for each peripheral subgroup
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\partial\bar{M})
of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(M)
. Such a choice is equivalent to selecting the square of one among the two (reciprocally inverse) eigenvalues of
\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)
, for each peripheral element
\displaystyle\gamma
of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(M)
; hence it defines a class
\displaystyle\delta_{\tilde{\rho}}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
. The set of augmented characters
\displaystyle\tilde{X}(M)
is algebraic, and has a canonical irreducible component
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)
containing the augmentation of
\displaystyle\rho_{hyp}
(it is unique, since
\displaystyle\rho_{hyp}
maps elements of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\partial\bar{M})
to parabolic transformations).

Now, identify
\displaystyle H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
with
\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})^{p}
by fixing a basis
\displaystyle(\mu_{1},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\mu_{p},\lambda_{p})
of
\displaystyle H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
. The map
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)\rightarrow X_{hyp}(M)
,
\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}=(\rho,\delta_{\tilde{\rho}})\mapsto\rho
, is regular, generically
\displaystyle 2^{p}\colon 1
, and the map

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\delta^{X}:&\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)&\longrightarrow&H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})^{p}\\
&\tilde{\rho}&\longmapsto&\delta_{\tilde{\rho}}\end{array}

is regular. The map
\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})^{p}\rightarrow X(\partial\bar{M})=X(T^{2})^{p}
, which sends the
\displaystyle i
-th entry
\displaystyle(m_{i}^{2},\ell_{i}^{2})
,
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,p
, to the coset in
\displaystyle X(T^{2})
of the pair

\left(\pm\begin{pmatrix}m_{i}&0\\
0&m_{i}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\pm\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{i}&0\\
0&\ell_{i}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)\in PSL(2,\mathbb{C})^{2},

is generically
\displaystyle 2^{p}\colon 1
. Since
\displaystyle r\colon X_{hyp}(M)\rightarrow X(\partial\bar{M})
has degree one, it follows that
\displaystyle\delta^{X}
has degree one, and is therefore a birational isomorphism onto its image. We thus get the bottom right square of the following commutative diagram, whose description we complete below.

\displaystyle\textstyle{{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi_{N}^{X}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\kappa^{X}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})^{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{((.)^{N}\times(.)^{N})^{p}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{G_{hyp,N}(T,b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi_{N}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widetilde{hol}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\delta^{X}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{2^{p}:1}
\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})^{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{2^{p}:1}
\displaystyle\textstyle{G_{hyp}(T,b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{hol_{|}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\tilde{hol}_{|}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{X_{hyp}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{r}
\displaystyle\textstyle{X(\partial\bar{M})}

Let
\displaystyle T
be a geometric triangulation of
\displaystyle M
, and
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)\subset G(T,b)
the irreducible component of
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
. Using the dilation factor (
15
), the holonomy map
\displaystyle hol
in (
10
) lifts to a regular map
\displaystyle\tilde{hol}\colon G(T,b)\rightarrow\tilde{X}(M)
,
\displaystyle w\mapsto(\rho_{w},\delta_{w})
, such that that
\displaystyle\delta_{w}=\delta^{X}\circ\tilde{hol}(w)
. We have:

Proposition 2.8
.

(
[
7
, Proposition 4.6]
and
[
8
, Remark 1.4]
) The restriction

(25)

\tilde{hol}_{|}\colon G_{hyp}(T,b)\rightarrow\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)

is a birational isomorphism, and the dilation factor
\displaystyle\delta\colon G_{hyp}(T,b)\rightarrow H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})^{p}
is a birational isomorphism onto its image.

The proof combines Thurston’s hyperbolic Dehn filling theorem in the form of
[
52
,
44
]
with Dunfield’s arguments showing that
\displaystyle r
has degree one.

Set

(26)

G_{hyp,N}(T,b):=\pi_{N}^{-1}(G_{hyp}(T,b)),

with
\displaystyle\pi_{N}
the map (
24
). Define
\displaystyle{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)
as the set of pairs
\displaystyle(\rho,\kappa)
where
\displaystyle\rho\in X_{hyp}(M)
,
\displaystyle\kappa\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
, and
\displaystyle\kappa^{N}(\gamma)
is a squared eigenvalue of
\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)
for all
\displaystyle\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
. Thus
\displaystyle{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)
has the structure of a fibered product
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)\times_{X(\partial\bar{M})}H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
. Clearly, the map
\displaystyle\pi_{N}^{X}\colon{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)\rightarrow\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)
,
\displaystyle(\rho,\kappa)\mapsto(\rho,\kappa^{N})
, has degree
\displaystyle N^{2p}
. Consider the “boundary weight map” (see (
23
))

(27)

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\kappa:&G_{hyp,N}(T,b)&\longrightarrow&H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})\\
&{\bf w}&\longmapsto&\kappa_{\bf w}.\end{array}

Since
\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf w}(\gamma)^{N}=\delta_{w}(\gamma)
, we can lift
\displaystyle\tilde{hol}_{|}
to a regular map

(28)

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\widetilde{hol}:&G_{hyp,N}(T,b)&\longrightarrow&{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)\\
&{\bf w}&\longmapsto&(\rho_{w},\kappa_{\bf w}).\end{array}

It has degree
\displaystyle N^{n-p}
(see Theorem
2.7
and the comment thereafter), and by construction

\pi_{N}=(\tilde{hol}_{|})^{*}\pi_{N}^{X}.

Finally
\displaystyle\kappa^{X}\colon{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
is defined by
\displaystyle\kappa^{X}(\rho,\kappa)=(\kappa(\mu_{K}),\kappa(\lambda_{K}))
; so
\displaystyle\kappa=\kappa^{X}\circ\widetilde{hol}
.

Remark 2.9
.

(see
[
7
, Proposition 4.6 and pages 2017-2019]
and
[
8
, Remark 1.4]
). Denote by EP the set of triangulations obtained by subdividing the Epstein-Penner polyhedral decomposition of
\displaystyle M
. For every
\displaystyle T\in EP
, fix a branchings
\displaystyle b_{T}
of
\displaystyle T
as at the beginning of Section
2.1.2
, and set
\displaystyle Z_{T}:=G_{hyp}(T,b_{T})\subset G(T,b_{T})
. There is a maximal Zariski open subset
\displaystyle\Omega_{Z_{T}}
in
\displaystyle Z_{T}
containing
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
, such that
\displaystyle hol(\Omega_{Z_{T}})
is Zariski open in
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
and
\displaystyle hol\colon\Omega_{Z_{T}}\rightarrow hol(\Omega_{Z_{T}})
is a homeomorphism. Then

\Omega:=\bigcap_{T\in{\rm EP}}hol(\Omega_{Z_{T}})

is a canonical Zariski open subset
\displaystyle\Omega
of
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
which contains
\displaystyle\rho_{hyp}
and whose points can be (uniquely) encoded by points in the components
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
, for every
\displaystyle T\in EP
.

2.3.
The case of the figure-eight knot complement

Fix an orientation of
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
. Denote by
\displaystyle\lambda_{K}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{K}
the canonical longitude and the meridian of
\displaystyle K
, oriented so that the ordered pair
\displaystyle(\mu_{K},\lambda_{K})
followed by the outward normal to
\displaystyle\partial\bar{M}
defines a positive frame of
\displaystyle\bar{M}
. We denote also by
\displaystyle\lambda_{K}
,
\displaystyle\mu_{K}
their classes in
\displaystyle H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
.

In this section we implement on
\displaystyle M
the structures defined in Sections
2.1
and
2.2.1
. We will use the geometric, positive ideal triangulation
\displaystyle T
of
\displaystyle M
shown in Figure 1, with branching
\displaystyle b
as indicated. The two tetrahedra
\displaystyle\Delta^{0}
,
\displaystyle\Delta^{1}
have opposite
\displaystyle b
-orientations, and
\displaystyle\Delta^{0}
is the positively
\displaystyle b
-oriented one; note that they were denoted
\displaystyle U
,
\displaystyle V
in Section
1.2
.

Denote by
\displaystyle u:=(u_{0},u_{1},u_{2})
and
\displaystyle{\bf u}:=({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1},{\bf u}_{2})
triples of classical and quantum shape parameters on
\displaystyle\Delta^{0}
, and by
\displaystyle(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})
a triple of edge colors compatible with
\displaystyle{\bf u}
. Denote similarly by
\displaystyle v:=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})
,
\displaystyle{\bf v}:=({\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1},{\bf v}_{2})
and
\displaystyle(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2})
the corresponding data for
\displaystyle\Delta^{1}
.

The Thurston gluing variety
\displaystyle G(T,b)
is well-known, and can be described as follows. For the computation of the formulas (
29
) and (
30
) below, we refer to
[
44
, Section 15.1.4]
, where we note that both tetrahedra are given positively oriented branchings, the classical shape parameters are ordered differently (the parameters
\displaystyle w
and
\displaystyle z
are
\displaystyle u_{2}
and
\displaystyle 1-v_{2}=v_{0}^{-1}
in our notations), and the curves
\displaystyle m
and
\displaystyle l
are
\displaystyle\mu_{K}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{K}^{-2}\lambda_{K}
. Now,
\displaystyle G(T,b)
can be identified with the irreducible curve in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{2}
with coordinates
\displaystyle(u_{0},v_{0})
and defining equation

u_{1}u_{2}^{2}v_{0}^{-2}v_{1}^{-1}=1,

or equivalently

(29)

u_{0}^{2}v_{0}^{2}=(1-u_{0})(1-v_{0}).

The point
\displaystyle w_{hyp}\in G(T,b)
with coordinates
\displaystyle(u_{0},v_{0})=(e^{\pi i/3},e^{-\pi i/3})
realizes the complete structure on
\displaystyle M
, and any point
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
sufficiently close to
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
determines a (non-complete) hyperbolic structure on
\displaystyle M
(see (ii) in Section
2.1.2
).

For every point
\displaystyle w=(u,v)\in G(T,b)
the dilation factor
\displaystyle\delta_{w}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
in (
15
) is given by

(30)

\delta_{w}(\lambda_{K})=u_{0}^{2}u_{2}^{-2}=\frac{u_{0}^{4}}{(1-u_{0})^{2}}\ ,\ \delta_{w}(\mu_{K})=u_{2}v_{2}=\frac{(u_{0}-1)(v_{0}-1)}{u_{0}v_{0}}.

The quantum gluing variety
\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)
is the subspace of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{6}
formed by the pairs
\displaystyle({\bf u},{\bf v})
of triples of quantum shape parameters
\displaystyle{\bf u}:=({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1},{\bf u}_{2})
and
\displaystyle{\bf v}:=({\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1},{\bf v}_{2})
satisfying the tetrahedral and edge relations

(31)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\bf u}_{0}{\bf u}_{1}{\bf u}_{2}=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{N}}&,&{\bf v}_{0}{\bf v}_{1}{\bf v}_{2}=e^{+\frac{\pi i}{N}}\\
{\bf u}_{1}{\bf u}_{2}^{2}{\bf v}_{0}^{-2}{\bf v}_{1}^{-1}=e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}}&,&{\bf u}_{1}{\bf u}_{0}^{2}{\bf v}_{2}^{-2}{\bf v}_{1}^{-1}=e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}}.\end{array}

Using the tetrahedral relations it is easy to see that the two edge relations are equivalent. Thus we can identify
\displaystyle G_{N}(T,b)
with the set of tuples
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1},{\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})
satisfying
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{1}{\bf u}_{0}^{2}{\bf v}_{2}^{-2}{\bf v}_{1}^{-1}=e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}}
, for instance. Since
\displaystyle G(T,b)
is an irreducible curve, we have

(32)

G_{hyp}(T,b)=G(T,b)\ {\rm and}\ G_{hyp,N}(T,b)=G_{N}(T,b).

Given a point
\displaystyle{\bf w}:=({\bf u},{\bf v})\in G_{N}(T,b)
, let
\displaystyle w:=(u,v)\in G(T,b)
, and
\displaystyle d:=(a,b)
be a system of edge colors such that
\displaystyle(w,d)
is compatible with
\displaystyle{\bf w}
; hence they are related by the formula (
6
). Consider the log weight
\displaystyle l\kappa
associated to
\displaystyle(w,d)
by (
18
), and the boundary weight
\displaystyle\kappa:=\kappa_{{\bf w}}
associated to
\displaystyle{\bf w}
by (
22
). Recall the bulk weight
\displaystyle h_{2}\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
in (
19
), and the relation (
23
). As
\displaystyle M
is a knot complement we have
\displaystyle h_{2}(\lambda_{K})=0
. Therefore

(33)

\kappa_{\bf w}(\lambda_{K})={\bf u}_{0}^{2}{\bf u}_{2}^{-2}=e^{\frac{l\kappa(\lambda_{K})}{N}}\ ,\ \kappa_{\bf w}(\mu_{K})={\bf u}_{2}{\bf v}_{2}=(-1)^{h_{2}(\mu_{K})}e^{\frac{l\kappa(\mu_{K})}{N}}.

For
\displaystyle k=0,1,2
, let us write
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{k}
,
\displaystyle{\bf v}_{k}
as in (
6
), in terms of edge colors and rescaled log-parameters
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k}^{\bf u}:=N^{-1}({\rm Log}(u_{k})+\pi ia_{k})
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k}^{\bf v}:=N^{-1}({\rm Log}(v_{k})+\pi ib_{k})
; so

(34)

{\bf u}_{k}=\exp({\bf l}_{k}^{\bf u}+\pi ia_{k})\ ,\ {\bf v}_{k}=\exp({\bf l}_{k}^{\bf v}+\pi ib_{k}).

The relations (
11
), (
12
) and (
18
) provide logarithms of (
31
) and (
33
) of the form

(35)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\bf l}_{0}^{\bf u}+{\bf l}_{1}^{\bf u}+{\bf l}_{2}^{\bf u}&=&\textstyle-\frac{\pi i}{N}+\pi i(a_{0}+a_{1}+a_{2})\\
{\bf l}_{0}^{\bf v}+{\bf l}_{1}^{\bf v}+{\bf l}_{2}^{\bf v}&=&\textstyle+\frac{\pi i}{N}+\pi i(b_{0}+b_{1}+b_{2})\\
{\bf l}_{1}^{\bf u}+2{\bf l}_{2}^{\bf u}-2{\bf l}_{0}^{\bf v}-{\bf l}_{1}^{\bf v}&=&\textstyle-\frac{2\pi i}{N}+\pi i(a_{1}+2a_{2}-2b_{0}-b_{1})\\
2{\bf l}_{0}^{\bf u}-2{\bf l}_{2}^{\bf u}&=&\textstyle\frac{l\kappa(\lambda_{K})}{N}+\pi i(2a_{0}-2a_{2})\\
{\bf l}_{2}^{\bf u}+{\bf l}_{2}^{\bf v}&=&\textstyle\frac{l\kappa(\mu_{K})}{N}+\pi i(a_{2}+b_{2}).\end{array}\right.

Note that
\displaystyle h_{2}(\mu_{K})=a_{2}+b_{2}
mod
\displaystyle(2)
by (
19
); all other sums of edge colors on the right side of the system (
35
) are even integers (
e.g.
, by (
8
)).

The system (
35
) may be rewritten in a very convenient form by fixing the point
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
, and working solely with edge colors. To see this, assume at first that
\displaystyle w=w_{hyp}
, so we have
\displaystyle(u_{0},v_{0})=(e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}},e^{-\frac{\pi i}{3}})
. Then by (
7
) and (
12
) the tetrahedral and edge relations between edge colors are (they correspond to the first three relations in (
35
)):

(36)

\displaystyle\displaystyle a_{2}=-2-a_{0}-a_{1}\ ,\ b_{2}=2-b_{0}-b_{1}

(37)

\displaystyle\displaystyle a_{1}+2a_{2}-2b_{0}-b_{1}=-4.\quad\quad\quad

As
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
realizes the complete hyperbolic structure, we have
\displaystyle\delta_{w_{hyp}}(\mu_{K})=\delta_{w_{hyp}}(\lambda_{K})=1
, and the identity
\displaystyle\kappa^{N}=\delta_{w_{hyp}}
implies
\displaystyle l\kappa(\mu_{K})\in\pi i\mathbb{Z}
and
\displaystyle l\kappa(\lambda_{K})\in 2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
. The relations (
36
), (
37
) and the formula (
18
) give:

(38)

a_{1}=-2a_{0}+\frac{l\kappa(\lambda_{K})}{2\pi i}-2\ ,\ b_{0}=\frac{l\kappa(\mu_{K})}{\pi i}-a_{0}\ ,\ b_{1}=2a_{0}-\frac{2l\kappa(\mu_{K})}{\pi i}-\frac{l\kappa(\lambda_{K})}{2\pi i}+2.

Next consider the case when
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
,
\displaystyle w\neq w_{hyp}
. If the coordinates
\displaystyle u_{0}
,
\displaystyle v_{0}
of
\displaystyle w
satisfy
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(u_{0})>0
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(v_{0})<0
, the relations (
36
) and (
37
) are unchanged, and those in (
38
) still hold true if one replaces
\displaystyle l\kappa(\cdot)
with
\displaystyle h_{\rho}(\cdot)=l\kappa(\cdot)-{\rm Log}(\delta_{w}(\cdot))
(see (
77
)). For an arbitrary point
\displaystyle w\in G(T,b)
the relations are obtained by doing the same replacement in (
38
), possibly adding even integers to the colors
\displaystyle a_{k}
,
\displaystyle b_{k}
(depending on
\displaystyle w
).

2.4.
The logarithmic limit set

Recall the spaces
\displaystyle G_{hyp,N}(T,b)
and
\displaystyle{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)
, where
\displaystyle N\geq 3
is odd (see Section
2.2.2
); we saw that when
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
these spaces are respectively the solution set of the equations (
31
), and (up to birational isomorphism) the plane curve whose points are pairs
\displaystyle(\kappa_{\bf w}(\mu_{K}),\kappa_{\bf w}(\lambda_{K}))
as in (
33
). Here we introduce a “limit” of the tower of spaces
\displaystyle\{G_{hyp,N}(T,b)\}_{N}
, that will be used in Section
4.1
. It is related in a natural way (via holonomy maps) to a tropical curve associated to the tower of curves
\displaystyle\{{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)\}_{N}
, and to the
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle A
-polynomial of
\displaystyle M
(see Remark
2.11
).

We use the notations of the previous section. As we are going to let
\displaystyle N
vary and consider corresponding sequences of points in the spaces
\displaystyle G_{hyp,N}(T,b)
, we reintroduce the dependence in
\displaystyle N
in the notations; thus the points of
\displaystyle G_{hyp,N}(T,b)
are the tuples
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})
satisfying (
31
). Also for
\displaystyle{\bf x}\in\{{\bf u},{\bf v}\}
and
\displaystyle k\in\{0,1,2\}
, setting
\displaystyle u_{k,N}:={\bf u}_{k,N}^{N}
we get tuples
\displaystyle(u_{0,N},u_{1,N},v_{0,N},v_{1,N})
in
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
, possibly varying with
\displaystyle N
. As in (
4
) we write

(39)

{\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf u}:=\frac{1}{N}\left({\rm Log}(u_{k,N})+2\pi ia_{k,N}^{\prime}\right),\ {\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf v}:=\frac{1}{N}\left({\rm Log}(v_{k,N})+2\pi ib_{k,N}^{\prime}\right).

Therefore
\displaystyle a_{k,N}^{\prime},b_{k,N}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}/N
are
\displaystyle N
-colors, and
\displaystyle{\bf x}_{k,N}=\exp({\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf x})
. Put

(40)

{\bf l}_{k,\infty}^{\bf x}:=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf x}\in\mathbb{C}/2\pi i\mathbb{Z}

assuming the limit exists. In this case, by the relations (
33
) the sequence
\displaystyle(\kappa_{N})_{N}
converges in
\displaystyle H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
. Then we put

\kappa_{\infty}:=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\kappa_{N}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*}).

Consider the embedding

\Theta_{N}:={\rm Log}|\Theta_{N}|\times\mathcal{A}(\Theta_{N})\colon G_{hyp,N}(T,b)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{4}

where

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lcll}{\rm Log}|\Theta_{N}|\colon G_{hyp,N}(T,b)&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{4}\\
({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})&\longmapsto&\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}|u_{0,N}|,\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}|u_{1,N}|,\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}|v_{0,N}|,\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}|v_{1,N}|\right)\end{array}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lcll}\mathcal{A}(\Theta_{N})\colon G_{hyp,N}(T,b)&\longrightarrow&(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{4}\\
({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})&\longmapsto&\left(e^{2\pi i\frac{a_{0,N}^{\prime}}{N}},e^{2\pi i\frac{a_{1,N}^{\prime}}{N}},e^{2\pi i\frac{b_{0,N}^{\prime}}{N}},e^{2\pi i\frac{b_{1,N}^{\prime}}{N}}\right).\end{array}

The maps
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|\Theta_{N}|
factor to maps defined on
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
, for which we keep the same notation. The subspace
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{4}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{4}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
has the metric
\displaystyle d
induced from the Euclidean distance, and the set of its subsets inherits the corresponding Hausdorff (pseudo-)distance:
\displaystyle\textstyle d(X,Y)=\max(\sup_{x\in X}(d(x,Y)),\sup_{y\in Y}(d(X,y)))
for all
\displaystyle X,Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{4}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

The sequence
\displaystyle\left(\Theta_{N}(G_{hyp,N}(T,b))\right)_{N}
converges to
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)\times\mathcal{A}(T,b)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{4}
as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
,
\displaystyle N
odd, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(T,b):=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\in(S^{1})^{4}\ |\ z_{1}^{2}z_{2}z_{3}^{2}z_{4}=1\}
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)
is the union of the following four half lines in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{4}
(parametrized in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
):

\begin{array}[]{ccc}L_{1}=\{t((-1,0),2(0,1)),t\geq 0\}&,&L_{2}=\{t(2(0,1),(-1,0)),t\geq 0\}\\
L_{3}=\{t((-1,0),2(1,-1)),t\geq 0\}&,&L_{4}=\{t(2(1,-1),(-1,0)),t\geq 0\}.\end{array}

Proof.

The convergence of
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|\Theta_{N}|(G_{hyp}(T,b))
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)
is the only non obvious fact; it follows from the Neumann-Zagier combinatorics
[
49
]
, and is shown in Example 3.1 of
[
62
]
(changing notations
\displaystyle z_{1}
,
\displaystyle z_{2}
to
\displaystyle u_{0}^{-1}
,
\displaystyle v_{0}^{-1}
, respectively). The vectors
\displaystyle(-1,0)
,
\displaystyle(0,1)
,
\displaystyle(1,-1)
used to define the half-lines
\displaystyle L_{1},\ldots,L_{4}
are the directions where
\displaystyle({\rm Log}|u_{0}|,{\rm Log}|u_{1}|)
(resp.
\displaystyle({\rm Log}|v_{0}|,{\rm Log}|v_{1}|)
) tends as
\displaystyle u_{0}
(resp.
\displaystyle v_{0}
) tends to
\displaystyle 0
,
\displaystyle 1
or
\displaystyle\infty
