. The images of the bounded subsets of
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
by the maps
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|\Theta_{N}|
converge to
\displaystyle\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
, hence the limit of
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|\Theta_{N}|(G_{hyp}(T,b))
is the cone over its asymptotic directions, and the task is to compute these directions.

Consider now the sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\Theta_{N})(G_{hyp,N}(T,b))
. By rewriting the system (
35
) in terms of the parameters
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf u}
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf v}
in (
39
) and assuming the limits (
40
) exist, as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
we get

(41)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf u}}&=&0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i)\\
{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}}+{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}}+{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf v}}&=&0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i)\\
{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+2{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf u}}-2{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}}-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}}&=&0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i)\\
2{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}}-2{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf u}}&=&{\rm Log}(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i)\\
{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf v}}&=&{\rm Log}(\kappa^{\infty}(\mu_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i).\end{array}\right.

Note that the following limits take values in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
:

\alpha_{k}^{\infty}:=\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{k,\infty}^{{\bf u}})=\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{2\pi a_{k,N}^{\prime}}{N}\ ,\ \beta_{k}^{\infty}:=\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{k,\infty}^{{\bf v}})=\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{2\pi b_{k,N}^{\prime}}{N}.

In particular the classical shape parameters
\displaystyle u_{k,N}
and
\displaystyle v_{k,N}
do not contribute to these limits. Taking imaginary parts in the system (
41
), we find that
\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{\infty},\beta_{k}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, for
\displaystyle k\in\{0,1,2\}
, satisfy the relations:

(42)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\alpha_{0}^{\infty}+\alpha_{1}^{\infty}+\alpha_{2}^{\infty}&=&0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi)\\
\beta_{0}^{\infty}+\beta_{1}^{\infty}+\beta_{2}^{\infty}&=&0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi)\\
\alpha_{1}^{\infty}+2\alpha_{2}^{\infty}-2\beta_{0}^{\infty}-\beta_{1}^{\infty}&=&0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi)\\
2\alpha_{0}^{\infty}-2\alpha_{2}^{\infty}&=&\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi)\\
\alpha_{2}^{\infty}+\beta_{2}^{\infty}&=&\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\mu_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi).\end{array}\right.

The first three relations are the only constraints on
\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{\infty},\beta_{k}^{\infty}
, where
\displaystyle k\in\{0,1,2\}
(in particular there is no contribution of the argument of the classical dilation factors
\displaystyle\arg(\delta_{w_{N}})
in
\displaystyle\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\cdot))
). Clearly then
\displaystyle(e^{i\alpha_{0}^{\infty}},e^{i\alpha_{1}^{\infty}},e^{i\beta_{0}^{\infty}},e^{i\beta_{1}^{\infty}})\in\mathcal{A}(T,b)
, which shows that the sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\Theta_{N})(G_{hyp,N}(T,b))
degenerate to
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(T,b)
as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
.∎

Assigning angles
\displaystyle(\alpha_{0}^{\infty},\alpha_{1}^{\infty},\alpha_{2}^{\infty}-\pi)
and
\displaystyle(\beta_{0}^{\infty},\beta_{1}^{\infty},\beta_{2}^{\infty}-\pi)
to
\displaystyle\Delta^{0}
and
\displaystyle\Delta^{1}
, the system (
42
) shows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(T,b)
is a space of generalized angle structures on
\displaystyle T
, in a sense similar to
[
43
]
. These angle structures determine an asymptotic angular holonomy
\displaystyle\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\cdot))\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{R}/2\pi)
by the relations (equivalent to the last two ones in (
42
)):

(43)

\displaystyle\displaystyle 4\alpha_{0}^{\infty}+2\alpha_{1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi),

(44)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\alpha_{0}^{\infty}+\alpha_{1}^{\infty}+\beta_{0}^{\infty}+\beta_{1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\mu_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi).

Remark 2.11
.

(1) In the terminology of tropical geometry (
[
60
]
) the set
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|\Theta_{1}|(G_{hyp}(T,b))
is the
amoeba
of
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)
the
tropical curve
of
\displaystyle G_{hyp}(T,b)
. Also, let us identify
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{4}
with the interior of its closed unit ball
\displaystyle D^{4}
by means of the map
\displaystyle x\mapsto x/(1+||\cdot||)
, where
\displaystyle||\cdot||
is the euclidian norm in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then the closure
\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{C}(T,b)}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)
in
\displaystyle D^{4}
has boundary
\displaystyle\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{C}(T,b):=\overline{\mathcal{C}(T,b)}\setminus\mathcal{C}(T,b)\subset S^{3}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)
is the cone over
\displaystyle\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{C}(T,b)
, and
\displaystyle\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{C}(T,b)
is the
logarithmic limit set
of
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|\Theta_{1}|(G_{hyp}(T,b))
in the sense of
[
15
]
.

By means of Culler-Shalen theory (
[
25
,
54
]
) such spaces play an important role in the detection of boundary slopes of incompressible surfaces in cusped manifolds
\displaystyle M
. These slopes are projective classes
\displaystyle[p\mu_{K}+q\lambda_{K}]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{R}))
. In particular, using also the Neumann-Zagier combinatorics
[
49
]
, Yoshida showed that for
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
the logarithmic limit set
\displaystyle\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{C}(T,b)
determines the slopes (see
[
62
]
, p. 160):

(45)

{\rm slope}(L_{1})={\rm slope}(L_{3})=[4\mu_{K}-\lambda_{K}]\ ,\ {\rm slope}(L_{2})={\rm slope}(L_{4})=[4\mu_{K}+\lambda_{K}].

The map
\displaystyle\tilde{hol}\colon G_{hyp}(T,b))\rightarrow\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)
of Section
2.2.2
identifies these boundary slopes with those detected by the Newton polygon of the factor of the
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle A
-polynomial of
\displaystyle M
corresponding to the curve
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)

[
23
,
17
,
20
]
. We refer to
[
57
,
58
,
36
]
for further developments and applications of this method to arbitrary cusped manifolds.

(2) As in Lemma
2.10
one can define a limit space
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M))\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{2}
of the tower of curves
\displaystyle\{{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)\}_{N}
; the
\displaystyle(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{2}
factor is realized by the pairs
\displaystyle(e^{i\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))},e^{\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\mu_{K}))})
. As
\displaystyle N\to+\infty
, the maps
\displaystyle\widetilde{hol}\colon G_{hyp,N}(T,b)\rightarrow{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)
of Section
2.2.2
yields a map
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(T,b)\times\mathcal{A}(T,b)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M))\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{2}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M))
is the tropical curve assigned to the amoeba of
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{hyp}(M)
, as in (1) above.

3.
The QHI

3.1.
Functions in the QHI state sums

Consider the rational functions
\displaystyle\omega_{N}(.,.|n)
,
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
, defined on the curve
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}|\ x^{N}+y^{N}=1\}
by

(46)

\omega_{N}(x,y|0)=1\ ,\ \omega_{N}(x,y|n)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{y}{1-x\zeta^{j}}\ {\rm if}\ n\geq 1.

Since
\displaystyle(1-x)(1-x\zeta)\ldots(1-x\zeta^{N-1})=1-x^{N}=y^{N}
, we have

\omega_{N}(x,y|N+n)=\omega_{N}(x,y|n).

These functions are closely related to Faddeev’s
[
28
,
29
]

non compact quantum dilogarithm

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm\bf b}
, and therefore to the classical Euler dilogarithm
\displaystyle{\rm Li_{2}}
. The function
\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm\bf b}
is defined as follows; it can be regarded as a kind of double Barnes’ sine function (
[
31
]
). Let
\displaystyle{\rm\bf b}\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}({\rm\bf b})\neq 0
. Define

\sqrt{\hbar}:=\frac{1}{{\rm\bf b}+{\rm\bf b}^{-1}}.

Denote by
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}+i0
the real axis directed from
\displaystyle-\infty
to
\displaystyle\infty
, slightly deformed near
\displaystyle 0
so as to pass above
\displaystyle 0
. Then
\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm\bf b}
is the holomorphic function defined in the strip
\displaystyle|\mathfrak{I}(z)|<|\mathfrak{I}(i/2\sqrt{\hbar})|
by the integral formula

(47)

\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}(z)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-2izw}\ dw}{w\sinh({\rm\bf b}w)\sinh({\rm\bf b}^{-1}w)}\right).

Denote by
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}(z))
the integral on the RHS. It satisfies the functional equation

(48)

{\rm Log}\left(\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}\left(z-i\frac{{\rm\bf b}^{\pm 1}}{2}\right)\right)={\rm Log}\left(\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}\left(z+i\frac{{\rm\bf b}^{\pm 1}}{2}\right)\right)+{\rm Log}(1+e^{2\pi{\rm\bf b}^{\pm 1}z}).

Therefore
\displaystyle\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}
can be extended to a meromorphic function of
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{C}
, satisfying the functional equation

(49)

\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}\left(z-i\frac{{\rm\bf b}^{\pm 1}}{2}\right)=(1+e^{2\pi{\rm\bf b}^{\pm 1}z})\Phi_{{{\rm\bf b}}}\left(z+i\frac{{\rm\bf b}^{\pm 1}}{2}\right).

The poles and zeros of
\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm\bf b}
are the points
\displaystyle\textstyle z=\frac{i}{2\sqrt{\hbar}}+mi{\rm\bf b}+ni{\rm\bf b}^{-1}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle z=-(\frac{i}{2\sqrt{\hbar}}+mi{\rm\bf b}+ni{\rm\bf b}^{-1})
respectively, where
\displaystyle m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will use the following version of
\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm\bf b}
. Let
\displaystyle\textstyle{\rm\bf b}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
, and set

(50)

\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}(z):=\Phi_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{N}}{2\pi}\left(z-i(\pi-\frac{\pi}{N})\right)\right).

Denote by
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))
the integral obtained from the one in (
47
) by the change of variable in (
50
). Then
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))
is a holomorphic function of
\displaystyle z
in the strip
\displaystyle\textstyle-\frac{2\pi}{N}<\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi
, and it satisfies the functional equation

(51)

\textstyle{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))={\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}))+{\rm Log}(1-e^{z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}}).

Therefore it can be extended to an analytic function on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
with cuts
\displaystyle\textstyle[0,+\infty[+i(2\pi+\frac{2\pi p}{N})
,
\displaystyle p\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\displaystyle\textstyle[0,+\infty[-i(l+1)\frac{2\pi}{N}
,
\displaystyle l\in\mathbb{N}
. All this implies that
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}
is holomorphic and without zeros in the strip
\displaystyle\textstyle-\frac{2\pi}{N}<\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi
, it satisfies the functional equation

(52)

\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}(z)=\hat{S}_{N}(z+\frac{2i\pi}{N})(1-e^{z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}}),

and it can be extended to a meromorphic function on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
with sets of poles and simple poles

(53)

\textstyle P=\{2i\pi+i\frac{2p\pi}{N},\ p\in\mathbb{N}\}\ ,\ P_{1}=\{2i\pi+i\frac{2p\pi}{N},\ p\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\}

respectively, and zeroes

\textstyle Z=\{-(l+1)i\frac{2\pi}{N},\ l\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Moreover the functional equation (
52
) yields

(54)

\omega_{N}(e^{z},y|n)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{y}{1-e^{z}\zeta^{j}}=y^{n}\ \frac{\hat{S}_{N}(z+2ni\pi/N)}{\hat{S}_{N}(z)}

for every
\displaystyle y,z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\displaystyle y^{N}=1-e^{Nz}
.
The function
\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm\bf b}
has a number of remarkable properties, see
[
29
,
1
]
. In particular, its ”semi-classical limit”, as
\displaystyle{\rm\bf b}\rightarrow 0^{+}
, recovers the Euler dilogarithm function (see
[
63
]
and Lemma
3.3
below),

(55)

{\rm Li_{2}}(x)=-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{{\rm Log}(1-t)}{t}dt,\quad x\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[1,+\infty[.

Basically, the connection between the two functions can be guessed by comparing the formula (
47
) with the following integral representation. It is well-known, but we recall a proof for it is short and illuminating.

Lemma 3.1
.

For all
\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}+i(-\pi,\pi)
we have
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}{\rm Li_{2}}(-e^{y})=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{v^{2}\sinh(v)}.

Proof.

The integral above is a holomorphic function of
\displaystyle y
. Taking the derivative twice we get

\partial_{y}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{v^{2}\sinh(v)}\right)=-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}=-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}(1+e^{y})}\ \biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}-\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0+\pi i}\biggr)\ \frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}

where, using a simple change of variable, we note that

\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0+\pi i}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}=-e^{y}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}.

Now, let
\displaystyle R>0
and consider the contour
\displaystyle\Gamma_{R}
formed by the concatenation of the oriented segments
\displaystyle[-R,R]+i0
,
\displaystyle R+i\pi[0,1]
,
\displaystyle-([-R,R]+i0)+i\pi
and
\displaystyle-R-i\pi[0,1]
. We have

\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\pm R+i\pi[0,1]}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}=\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{-i\frac{y}{\pi}(\pm R+\pi it)}}{\sinh(\pm R+\pi it)}\ dt=0

and therefore

\displaystyle\displaystyle\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}-\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0+\pi i}\biggr)\ \frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\biggl(\int_{\Gamma_{R}}-\int_{R+i\pi[0,1]}-\int_{-R-i\pi[0,1]}\biggr)\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Gamma_{R}}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{\sinh(v)}.

This integral is constant in
\displaystyle R
, and equal to
\displaystyle\textstyle 2\pi i.{\rm Res}\left(\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}}{\sinh(v)},v=i\pi\right)=-2\pi ie^{y}
by the residue theorem. We deduce

\partial_{y}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{v^{2}\sinh(v)}\right)=\frac{2ie^{y}}{\pi(1+e^{y})}.

Finally, we can integrate twice along a path running from
\displaystyle-\infty
along the real axis up to
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(y)
, and then along a straight segment up to
\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}+i(-\pi,\pi)
, since the integrands are continuous in this strip:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{y}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{v^{2}\sinh(v)}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{2i}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{e^{s}}{1+e^{s}}ds=\frac{2i}{\pi}{\rm Log}(1+e^{y})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0}\frac{e^{-i\frac{yv}{\pi}}\ dv}{v^{2}\sinh(v)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{2i}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{y}{\rm Log}(1+e^{s})ds=-\frac{2i}{\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}(-e^{y}).

The result follows.
\displaystyle\Box

3.2.
Asymptotics of
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}(z)
and
\displaystyle g_{N}(z)

Let us start with a straightforward lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

For all
\displaystyle\delta\in]0,\pi[
, for all
\displaystyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i([\delta,2\pi-\delta]+2\pi k)
,
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have:

\left|\dfrac{e^{z}}{1-e^{z}}\right|\leq\dfrac{e^{M/2}}{\delta\sqrt{1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}}}.

Proof.

It is enough to do the case
\displaystyle k=0
. For
\displaystyle\delta\in]0,\pi[
and
\displaystyle z=x+iy\in]-\infty,M]+i[\delta,2\pi-\delta]
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left|\dfrac{e^{z}}{1-e^{z}}\right|^{2}=\dfrac{e^{2x}}{|1-e^{z}|^{2}}=\dfrac{e^{2x}}{1+e^{2x}-2e^{x}\cos(y)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\dfrac{e^{x}}{2\cosh(x)-2\cos(y)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{e^{M}}{2\cosh(x)-2\cos(y)}.

For
\displaystyle y\in[\delta,2\pi-\delta]
fixed,

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2\cosh(x)-2\cos(y)}
is maximal at
\displaystyle x=0
, where it is equal to

\dfrac{1}{2-2\cos(y)}=\dfrac{1}{2-2+4\sin(y/2)^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{4\sin(y/2)^{2}}.

Moreover, for
\displaystyle y\in[\delta,2\pi-\delta]
we have
\displaystyle\sin(y/2)\geq\sin(\delta/2)
, and since
\displaystyle 0<\delta/2<\pi/2
, a Taylor-Lagrange expansion with remainder at order
\displaystyle 4
gives
\displaystyle\textstyle\sin(\delta/2)\geq\delta/2-\frac{1}{6}(\delta/2)^{3}>\delta/2\left(1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\right)>0
. Therefore

\dfrac{1}{4\sin(y/2)^{2}}\leq\dfrac{1}{4(\delta/2-\frac{1}{6}(\delta/2)^{3})^{2}}\leq\dfrac{1}{\delta^{2}(1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24})}.

The lemma follows.
∎

We will use the following
uniform
estimate of the relation between
\displaystyle{\rm Li}_{2}
and the semi-classical limit of the function
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists constants
\displaystyle B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}>0
such that for all
\displaystyle\delta\in]0,\pi[
,
\displaystyle M\in\mathbb{R}
, large enough
\displaystyle N\geq 3
odd, and
\displaystyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i[\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}]
we have

(56)

{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))=\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})+\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N}(z),

where
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
is a holomorphic function of
\displaystyle z
, with formula given by (
59
)-(
62
), satisfying

(57)

|\Psi_{N}(z)|\leq B_{\delta}+\dfrac{\pi e^{M/2}}{2\delta\sqrt{1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}}},\ \mathrm{with}\ B_{\delta}:=B^{\prime}/\delta+B^{\prime\prime}.

Moreover, setting
\displaystyle\mathrm{c}_{M}:=\sqrt{1+e^{M}}
, we have

\left|\mathfrak{R}\left({\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})\right)\right|\leq{\rm Log}(\mathrm{c}_{M})+\frac{B_{\delta}}{N}

or equivalently

\dfrac{1}{\mathrm{c}_{M}}\exp\left(-\frac{B_{\delta}}{N}\right)\leq\left|\hat{S}_{N}(z)\exp\left(-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{c}_{M}\exp\left(\frac{B_{\delta}}{N}\right).

Remark 3.4
.

(i) The condition “large enough
\displaystyle N\geq 3
” is in place to ensure that
\displaystyle\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}>0
, so that the half-strip
\displaystyle\textstyle]-\infty,M]+i[\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}]
avoids cuts in the domain (
63
). Thus, if
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<0
this condition is unnecessary and
\displaystyle\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}\leq 0
is admissible.

(ii) The definition of
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
from
\displaystyle\Xi_{N}
(see (
59
)-(
62
)) shows that the identity (
56
) holds true for all
\displaystyle z
in the strip
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}+i]\textstyle-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{\pi}{N}[
.

(iii) The lemma generalizes immediately to the situation where one considers distinct constants
\displaystyle\delta^{-},\delta^{+}\in]0,\pi[
instead of
\displaystyle\delta
, and takes
\displaystyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i[\textstyle\delta^{-}-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta^{+}-\frac{\pi}{N}]
. Then one has (
57
) with
\displaystyle\delta:=\min(\delta^{-},\delta^{+})
. This may be useful when one is willing sharp uniform bounds on specific sets.

(iv) We can give explicit numerical values for the constants
\displaystyle B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}>0
in Lemma
3.3
, following the computations in
[
11
, Lemma 7.13]
(see the formula of
\displaystyle B_{\delta}
on page 367 of that paper).

Proof.

It follows from Lemmas 7.13 and 7.14 in
[
11
]
by a change of variable and a very small adaptation. Let us give a few details. First we consider the inequalities, since for them the adaptation is simpler to describe.

Lemmas 7.13 and 7.14 in
[
11
]
deal with
\displaystyle\textstyle\Phi_{{\rm\bf b}}(\frac{y}{2\pi{\rm\bf b}})
, with the assumptions
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(y)\in[\delta,\pi-\delta]
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(y)\in[-\pi+\delta,-\delta]
respectively. From the proof of these lemmas, it is easy to see that these assumptions, keeping the same conclusions, can be extended to
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(y)\in[0,\pi-\delta]
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(y)\in[-\pi+\delta,0]
respectively. By setting
\displaystyle{\rm\bf b}:=1/\sqrt{N}
and
\displaystyle y=z-i\pi+i\pi/N
, these conclusions imply immediately that, given
\displaystyle\delta>0
,
\displaystyle N
, and
\displaystyle\textstyle z\in\mathbb{R}+i[\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{\pi}{N}-\delta]
, there exists a constant
\displaystyle B_{\delta}>0
as in the statement such that

\left|\mathfrak{R}\left({\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}})\right)\right|\leq\frac{B_{\delta}}{N}.

More precisely, denoting

\epsilon(z):=\dfrac{1}{z^{2}}\left(\dfrac{z}{\sinh(z)}-1\right)

(the Taylor remainder at
\displaystyle 0
of order
\displaystyle 2
of the function
\displaystyle z\mapsto z/\sinh(z)
), and
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
a contour equal to
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
except near
\displaystyle 0
where it avoids
\displaystyle 0
upwards while staying in a disk
\displaystyle D(0,R)
with center
\displaystyle 0
and radius
\displaystyle R\in]0,\pi[
, we have

(58)

{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}})=\dfrac{1}{N}\Xi_{N}(z)

for
\displaystyle\textstyle z\in\mathbb{R}+i[\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{\pi}{N}-\delta]
, where

(59)

\Xi_{N}(z):=\int_{v\in\Omega}\epsilon\left(\frac{v}{N}\right)\dfrac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+\frac{v}{N})}{4\sinh(v)}dv

is holomorphic in the open strip
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}+i]\textstyle-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{\pi}{N}[
and satisfies
\displaystyle|\Xi_{N}(z)|\leq B_{\delta}
on the closed substrip
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}+i[\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}]
. (We stress that in Lemma 7.13 of
[
11
]
the stated upper bound is
\displaystyle|\mathfrak{R}(\Xi_{N}(z))|\leq B_{\delta}
, where the constant
\displaystyle B_{\delta}:=B^{\prime}/\delta+B^{\prime\prime}
is described explicitly on page 367, but the conclusion is deduced from the inequality
\displaystyle|\mathfrak{R}(\Xi_{N}(z))|\leq|\Xi_{N}(z)|
and an upper bound for
\displaystyle|\Xi_{N}(z)|
).

We want to replace the term
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}})
with
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})
. For this, we will use a Taylor expansion.

Recall that we take
\displaystyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i[\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}]
, with
\displaystyle N
large enough so that
\displaystyle\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}>0
. In particular, the arguments of the logarithms and dilogarithms in the following formulas will avoid the cuts of these functions. Since

(60)

{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}})-{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})=-\frac{i\pi}{N}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}(1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}t})dt,

we have

\mathfrak{R}\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}})-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}\left|1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}t}\right|dt=-\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}\left|1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}t}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}dt.

Now
\displaystyle|1-e^{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sqrt{1+|e^{z}|}\leq\sqrt{1+e^{M}}
for all
\displaystyle\textstyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i[\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta]
. Define
\displaystyle\mathrm{c}_{M}:=\sqrt{1+e^{M}}
. We get, for every
\displaystyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i[\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}]
:

\left|\mathfrak{R}\left({\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})\right)\right|\leq{\rm Log}(\mathrm{c}_{M})+\frac{B_{\delta}}{N}.

The pair of inequalities in the statement immediately follows.

Let us now prove the equality (
56
) and the upper bound (
57
).
From (
58
) and (
60
), we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}})+\dfrac{1}{N}\Xi_{N}(z)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}(1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}t})dt+\dfrac{1}{N}\Xi_{N}(z).

Another Taylor expansion gives us

(61)

{\rm Log}(1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}t})={\rm Log}(1-e^{z})-\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{E}_{N}(z,t),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{N}(z,t):=(i\pi t)\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}{1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}ds
. Hence we obtain

{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))=\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})+\frac{1}{2N}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{E}_{N}(z,t)dt+\dfrac{1}{N}\Xi_{N}(z).

This gives (
56
), by setting

(62)

\Psi_{N}(z):=\Xi_{N}(z)+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{E}_{N}(z,t)dt=\Xi_{N}(z)+\dfrac{i\pi}{2}\int_{t=0}^{1}t\int_{s=0}^{1}\dfrac{e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}{1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}dsdt.

Consider the strip
\displaystyle]-\infty,M]+i[\textstyle\delta-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}]
. We know that
\displaystyle|\Xi_{N}(z)|\leq B_{\delta}
, and from Lemma
3.2
the second term gives:

\left|\dfrac{i\pi}{2}\int_{t=0}^{1}t\int_{s=0}^{1}\dfrac{e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}{1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}dsdt\right|\leq\dfrac{\pi}{2}\int_{s=0}^{1}\left|\dfrac{e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}{1-e^{z+\frac{i\pi}{N}ts}}\right|ds\leq\dfrac{\pi e^{M/2}}{2\delta\sqrt{1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}}}.

By the triangle inequality, (
57
) follows at once.
\displaystyle\Box

Let us draw the following important though easy consequence of Lemma
3.3
and the functional equation (
51
). It will be used in Lemma
5.5
.

By the domains where
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z))
(deduced after (
51
)) and
\displaystyle{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})
and
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})
are holomorphic functions (here,
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\cup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2i\pi k+[0,+\infty[\right)\right)
), the identity (
56
) immediately shows that
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
has a holomorphic extension on the domain

(63)

\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\left([0,+\infty[+i\left(2\pi+\frac{2\pi p}{N}\right)\right)\bigcup\left(\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\left([0,+\infty[-i(l+1)\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)\right)\bigcup[0,+\infty[\right).

In particular
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
is holomorphic on the domain (see Figure
2
)

U:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ \mathfrak{R}(z)<0\right\}\bigcup\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ 0<\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi\right\}.

In the following lemma, for every
\displaystyle M\in\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle\alpha\in]0,1[
,
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
,
\displaystyle K>2\pi
, and
\displaystyle c>0
we consider the sets
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}\subset\mathbb{C}
formed by the points
\displaystyle z
such that (see Figure
2
):

•

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)\leq M
and
\displaystyle\textstyle(\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}<\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi-(\alpha+1)\frac{\pi}{N}
, or

•

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<0
and
\displaystyle\textstyle 2\pi k+(\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}\leq\mathfrak{I}(z)\leq(\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}
, or

•

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<-c
and
\displaystyle\textstyle 2\pi-(\alpha+1)\frac{\pi}{N}\leq\mathfrak{I}(z)\leq K
.

\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 2i\pi
\displaystyle U
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle-c+iK
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle-c+i\left(2\pi-(\alpha+1)\frac{\pi}{N}\right)
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle M+i\left(2\pi-(\alpha+1)\frac{\pi}{N}\right)
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle M+i(\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle i(\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle i\left((\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}+2k\pi\right)
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}

Figure 2.
The domain
\displaystyle U
and the sets
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

(i) The sequence of functions
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
converges simply to a holomorphic function on the strip
\displaystyle 0<\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi
.

(ii) The function
\displaystyle\textstyle|\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N})|
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
on each set
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}
.

(iii) The sequence of functions
\displaystyle\textstyle\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N})
converges uniformly to the constant function with value
\displaystyle 1
on compact subsets of
\displaystyle U
.

Remark 3.6
.

(i) The condition
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<-c
for some
\displaystyle c>0
when
\displaystyle\textstyle 2\pi-(\alpha+1)\frac{\pi}{N}\leq\mathfrak{I}(z)\leq K
is in place to ensure that the half-space stays at distance from the singular points
\displaystyle 2i\pi+2i\pi p/N
,
\displaystyle p\in\mathbb{N}
, of
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
.

(ii) The proof below provides an explicit upper bound of
\displaystyle\textstyle|\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N})|
on
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}
.

Proof.

(i) Any point of
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{R}+i]0,2\pi[
is contained in
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{R}+i]-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{\pi}{N}[
for
\displaystyle N
large enough, where
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
has the integral formula given by (
62
) and (
59
). By using Lemma
3.2
and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem it is clear that the second summand in the formula (
62
) converges simply on
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{R}+i]0,2\pi[
. Thus it remains to prove it also for
\displaystyle\Xi_{N}
.

Recall the function
\displaystyle\epsilon
in (
59
). It is easy to see that
\displaystyle\epsilon
is holomorphic on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\cup D(0,R)
, that
\displaystyle\textstyle\lim_{x\to 0}\epsilon(x)=-\frac{1}{6}
, and that there is a scalar
\displaystyle\sigma_{R}>0
such that
\displaystyle|\epsilon(x)|\leq\sigma_{R}
for all
\displaystyle x\in\Omega\cup D(0,R)
(see
[
11
, pp. 364-365]
for details). Let us split
\displaystyle\Xi_{N}(z)
as

(64)

\Xi_{N}(z)=\int_{v\in\Omega\cap D(0,R)}\epsilon\left(\frac{v}{N}\right)\dfrac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+\frac{v}{N})}{4\sinh(v)}dv\\
+\int_{v\in]-\infty,-R]}\epsilon\left(\frac{v}{N}\right)\dfrac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+\frac{v}{N})}{4\sinh(v)}dv+\int_{v\in[R,+\infty[}\epsilon\left(\frac{v}{N}\right)\dfrac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+\frac{v}{N})}{4\sinh(v)}dv.

Note that for all
\displaystyle N
the integrand is a holomorphic function of
\displaystyle z
and
\displaystyle v
. It has modulus less than
\displaystyle\sigma_{R}\textstyle|\frac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+R)}{4\sinh(v)}|
for
\displaystyle v\in\Omega\cap D(0,R)
, where this function is continuous and hence integrable. Now set
\displaystyle z=x+iy
, so that
\displaystyle 0<y<2\pi
. For a real
\displaystyle v\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left|\epsilon\left(\frac{v}{N}\right)\dfrac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+\frac{v}{N})}{4\sinh(v)}\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{R}\left|\frac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v+\frac{v}{N})}{4\sinh(v)}\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sigma_{R}\frac{\exp(v(-1+\frac{y}{\pi}+\frac{1}{N}))}{4\sinh(v)}.

Let
\displaystyle c^{-},c^{+}
be positive real numbers such that
\displaystyle\textstyle 0<c^{-}<\frac{y}{\pi}<c^{+}<2
, and assume that
\displaystyle N
is large enough so that
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{y}{\pi}+\frac{1}{N}<c^{+}
. Then the RHS is
\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\sigma_{R}\frac{\exp(v(c^{+}-1))}{4\sinh(v)}
on
\displaystyle[+R,+\infty[
and
\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\sigma_{R}\frac{\exp(v(c^{-}-1))}{4\sinh(v)}
on
\displaystyle[-\infty,-R[
, where these functions are integrable on these intervals. Then we can apply Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem to the three integrals. By summing the results we get

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\Xi_{N}(z)=-\frac{1}{6}\int_{v\in\Omega}\dfrac{\exp(-i\frac{zv}{\pi}-v)}{4\sinh(v)}dv,

The arguments above show also that the integral on the RHS is a holomorphic function of
\displaystyle z
in the strip
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}+i]0,2\pi[
, which concludes the proof of (i).

(ii) First consider
\displaystyle\textstyle|\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N})|
on
\displaystyle\textstyle]-\infty,M]+i]-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{\pi}{N}[
. Let
\displaystyle\alpha>0
be fixed, and apply (
57
) with
\displaystyle\delta
of the form
\displaystyle\delta_{N}:=\textstyle\alpha\frac{\pi}{N}
. For any
\displaystyle z\in\textstyle]-\infty,M]+i[\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N}]
, we find

(65)

\frac{1}{N}|\Psi_{N}(z)|\leq\frac{B^{\prime}}{\alpha\pi}+\dfrac{\pi e^{M/2}}{2\alpha\pi\sqrt{1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}}}+\frac{B^{\prime\prime}}{N},

which is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
. This proves the claim on the strip
\displaystyle\textstyle(\alpha-1)\frac{\pi}{N}<\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi-(\alpha+1)\frac{\pi}{N}
.

Assume now that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<0
. Denote by
\displaystyle C_{M,\alpha,B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}}
the constant on the RHS of (
65
). By repeated use of (
51
) we can shift
\displaystyle z
by
\displaystyle-2i\pi
, thus getting
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}({\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z-2i\pi)))=\mathfrak{R}({\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z)))+\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\rm Log}|1-e^{z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}}|=\mathfrak{R}({\rm Log}(\hat{S}_{N}(z)))+{\rm Log}|1-e^{Nz}|
. Then the difference of (
56
) with the same identity where
\displaystyle z
is replaced by
\displaystyle z-2i\pi
has real part

(66)

\mathfrak{R}(\Psi_{N}(z))-\mathfrak{R}(\Psi_{N}(z-2i\pi))=-N{\rm Log}|1-e^{Nz}|.

Therefore
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{N}(z-2i\pi)\right)\leq\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{N}(z)\right)+N{\rm Log}(1+|e^{Nz}|)\leq\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{N}(z)\right)+N{\rm Log}(2)
, and hence
\displaystyle\textstyle|\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N}(z))|=\exp(\frac{1}{N}\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{N}(z)\right))\leq\exp(C_{M,\alpha,B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}}+k{\rm Log}(2))
for any
\displaystyle z
in a half-strip
\displaystyle\textstyle]-\infty,0[+i(2\pi k^{\prime}+[\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N}])
, where
\displaystyle k\leq k^{\prime}\leq 0
.

To fix the ideas take now for instance
\displaystyle\textstyle\alpha:=\frac{1}{2}
(namely, in the following discussion
\displaystyle\alpha
must be chosen so that the width of the “narrow” half-strips defined below is
\displaystyle\textstyle<\frac{2\pi}{N}
, hence the width
\displaystyle 2\pi-2\delta_{N}
of the above half-strips is
\displaystyle>\textstyle 2\pi-\frac{2\pi}{N}
, whence
\displaystyle\alpha\in]0,1[
). Denote by
\displaystyle U^{\prime}
the union of the above half-strips,
\displaystyle\textstyle]-\infty,0[+i(2\pi k^{\prime}+[-\frac{\pi}{2N},2\pi-\frac{3\pi}{2N}])
, where
\displaystyle k\leq k^{\prime}\leq 0
. The set
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}
is the union of
\displaystyle U^{\prime}
with the “narrow” half-strips
\displaystyle\textstyle]-\infty,0[+i\left(2\pi k^{\prime}+[-\frac{3\pi}{2N},-\frac{\pi}{2N}]\right)
,
\displaystyle k-1\leq k^{\prime}\leq 0
, and
\displaystyle\textstyle]-\infty,-c[+i[2\pi-\frac{3\pi}{2N},K]
.

Thus it remains to prove the bound when
\displaystyle z
lies within these half-strips. Consider first the “narrow” ones, with
\displaystyle k-1\leq k^{\prime}\leq 0
. For any such a point
\displaystyle z
, we have
\displaystyle z+\textstyle{\frac{2i\pi}{N}}\in U^{\prime}
. Therefore we consider the identity (
51
). By means of (
56
) it yields

\frac{1}{N}\left(\Psi_{N}(z-\textstyle{\frac{2i\pi}{N}})-\Psi_{N}(z)\right)\\
=\frac{N}{2i\pi}\left({\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})-{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z-\frac{2i\pi}{N}})\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm Log}(1-e^{z-\frac{2i\pi}{N}})-{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})\right)+{\rm Log}(1-e^{z}).

Using the Taylor expansion
\displaystyle\textstyle{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z-\frac{2i\pi}{N}})-{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})=-\frac{2i\pi}{N}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}(1-e^{z-\frac{2i\pi}{N}t})dt
and changing
\displaystyle z
to
\displaystyle\textstyle z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}
this can be written

\frac{1}{N}\left(\Psi_{N}(z)-\Psi_{N}\left((z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}\right)\right)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}(1-e^{z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}t})dt+\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm Log}(1-e^{z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}})+{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})\right).

Since we assume
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<0
, the real part of the RHS is bounded from above by
\displaystyle 2{\rm Log}(2)
, and hence

\left|\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N}(z)\right)\right|\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{N}\left(z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}\right)\right)+2\log(2)\right)\\
\leq\exp(C_{M,\alpha,B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}}+(k+2){\rm Log}(2)).

Finally, assume that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<-c
,
\displaystyle c>0
. Since
\displaystyle{\rm Log}|1-e^{z}|\geq{\rm Log}|1-|e^{z}||\geq{\rm Log}(1-e^{-c})
, similarly as above we obtain
\displaystyle\textstyle|\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N}(z))|\leq\exp(\frac{1}{N}\mathfrak{R}(\Psi_{N}(z-\frac{2i\pi}{N}))-2{\rm Log}(1-e^{-c}))\leq\exp(C_{M,\alpha,B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}}-2{\rm Log}(1-e^{-c}))
for
\displaystyle z\in\textstyle]-\infty,-c[+i[2\pi-\frac{3\pi}{2N},2\pi]
. When
\displaystyle 2\pi\leq\mathfrak{I}(z)\leq K
, the bound of
\displaystyle\textstyle|\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N}(z))|
is obtained similarly by using (
66
). This concludes the proof of (ii).

(iii) First note that any compact subset of
\displaystyle U
is contained in
\displaystyle U_{M,\alpha,k,K,c}
for some
\displaystyle M,\alpha,k,K
and
\displaystyle c
, for
\displaystyle N
large enough. By (ii) above and Montel’s theorem, it follows that the sequence of functions
\displaystyle\textstyle\exp(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{N}(z))
is a
normal family
(that is, it has a subsequence which converges uniformly on compact subsets) on
\displaystyle U
. Since by (i) it converges simply to
\displaystyle 1
on a subset of
\displaystyle U
having a limit point, by Vitali’s Theorem on the convergence of holomorphic functions, the convergence is uniform on all compact subsets of
\displaystyle U
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Recall that
\displaystyle y^{1/N}:=\exp({\rm Log}(y)/N)
for any
\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Put

(67)

g_{N}(z):=\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}(1-z\zeta^{-j})^{j/N}.

In order to prove Theorem
1.1
, we will need to study asymptotics of
\displaystyle g_{N}(e^{z})
for
\displaystyle z={\bf l}_{0,N}
of the form (
39
). First note that, by collecting conjugate terms it is immediate that
\displaystyle|g_{N}(1)^{N}|=N^{\frac{N}{2}}
, and factorizing by
\displaystyle\zeta^{-j/2}
each term in the product yields

g_{N}(1)^{N}=e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}(N-2)(N-1)(2N-1)}\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}2^{j}\sin\left(\frac{\pi j}{N}\right)^{j}

where the product is real positive. Therefore

(68)

g_{N}(1)^{N}=N^{\frac{N}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}(N-2)(N-1)(2N-1)}.

Before considering
\displaystyle g_{N}(e^{z})
for more general
\displaystyle z
, let us do some rewriting. We have, for any odd integer
\displaystyle N
and any
\displaystyle z\notin(2i\pi/N)\mathbb{Z}
(so that the following quotients are defined):

\displaystyle\displaystyle g_{N}(e^{z})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}\bigl(1-e^{z}\zeta^{-k}\bigr)^{k/N}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}\biggl(\frac{\hat{S}_{N}(z+2(N-(k+1))i\pi/N)}{\hat{S}_{N}(z+2(N-k)i\pi/N)}\biggr)^{k/N}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\hat{S}_{N}(z)^{(N-1)/N}\ \prod_{k=1}^{N-1}\hat{S}_{N}\left(z+2(N-k)i\pi/N\right)^{-1/N}

(69)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\hat{S}_{N}(z)\ \prod_{k=1}^{N}\hat{S}_{N}\left(z+2ki\pi/N\right)^{-1/N}.

Now, it is tempting to use Lemma
3.3
to compare
\displaystyle g_{N}(e^{z})
to

(70)

\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{N}\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+2ki\pi/N})\right)^{-1/N},

an expression that can be simplified thanks to the following factorization formula (see for example
[
63
, page 7]
):

Proposition 3.7
.

For any
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{C}
, and any positive integer
\displaystyle N
, we have:

{\rm Li_{2}}(x)=N\sum_{y\in\mathbb{C},\ y^{N}=x}{\rm Li_{2}}(y).

In particular, for any
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{C}
and any positive integer
\displaystyle N
, we have:

\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z+2ki\pi/N})=\dfrac{1}{N}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{Nz}).

It immediately follows that
\displaystyle g_{N}(e^{z})
should be “comparable” (again, we are speaking non-rigorously for now) to

(71)

\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{z})-\frac{1}{2i\pi N}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{Nz})\right),

whose asymptotics look much simpler to study.

Let us now turn back to rigor. We need to be careful about both the requirements that
\displaystyle z\notin(2i\pi/N)\mathbb{Z}
and that all the points
\displaystyle z+2ki\pi/N
live in a domain where Lemma
3.3
can be applied (and if not, we need to keep track of correction terms).

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\displaystyle u_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, and for any odd integer
\displaystyle N
, let
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}
be such that
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{0,N}:=e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}}
satisfies
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{0,N}^{N}=u_{0}
, and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}={\bf l}_{0,\infty}+O_{N\to\infty}\left(N^{-1}\right)
, where
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}\in i\mathbb{R}
. We have:

(i)
\displaystyle|g_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N})|
is bounded from below and above by the product of
\displaystyle e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\mathfrak{I}({\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}))}
with a positive constant independent of
\displaystyle N
. In particular, if
\displaystyle{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}})\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\displaystyle|g_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N})|
is bounded from below and above by a positive constant independent of
\displaystyle N
.

(ii) the same result holds true by replacing
\displaystyle g_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N})
with
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})
.

Proof.

First assume that
\displaystyle\Im(u_{0})>0
. Set

\textstyle{\bf l}_{0,N}=\dfrac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(u_{0})+2\pi ia_{0,N}^{\prime})

where
\displaystyle a_{0,N}^{\prime}\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}
. Define

(72)

\delta_{N}:=\dfrac{1}{N}\min\{\Im({\rm Log}(u_{0})),\pi-\Im({\rm Log}(u_{0}))\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ M:=|\Re({\rm Log}(u_{0}))|.

We want to apply the reasoning preceding Lemma
3.8
to the terms
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}\left(z+2ki\pi/N\right)
on the right side of (
69
), taking
\displaystyle z={\bf l}_{0,N}
. We first notice that
\displaystyle\delta_{N}>0
, and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}\notin(2i\pi/N)\mathbb{Z}
as required (since
\displaystyle u_{0}\neq 1
). We then immediately see that

(73)

{\bf l}_{0,N},{\bf l}_{0,N}+\frac{2i\pi}{N},\ldots,{\bf l}_{0,N}+\frac{2i\pi(N-1-a^{\prime}_{0,N})}{N}\in[-M,M]+i[\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N}]

and

{\bf l}_{0,N}+\frac{2i\pi(N-a^{\prime}_{0,N})}{N},\ldots,{\bf l}_{0,N}+\frac{2i\pi(N-1)}{N}\in[-M,M]+i[2\pi+\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N},4\pi-\delta_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N}].

These two sequences of scalars realize all the arguments on the right side of (
69
) but the last of the product,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}+2i\pi
. We can directly apply the inequalities of Lemma
3.3
for
\displaystyle z
being any one among the
\displaystyle N-a^{\prime}_{0,N}
values of the sequence (
73
). The error bound is

(74)

{\rm Log}(\mathrm{c}_{M})+\dfrac{B_{\delta_{N}}}{N}={\rm Log}(\mathrm{c}_{M})+\dfrac{B^{\prime}}{\delta_{N}N}+\dfrac{B^{\prime\prime}}{N},

which by (
72
) is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
. For
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}+2i\pi
or any of the
\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{0,N}
values of the sequence below (
73
), we need to modify the corresponding terms
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}\left(z+2ki\pi/N\right)
on the right side of (
69
), by re-shifting
\displaystyle z_{k}:=z+2ki\pi/N
using
\displaystyle N
times the formula (
52
). We have

\hat{S}_{N}(z_{k})=\dfrac{\hat{S}_{N}(z_{k}-2i\pi)}{1-e^{Nz_{k}}},

each factor
\displaystyle 1-e^{Nz_{k}}=1-u_{0}
is raised at the power
\displaystyle-1/N
in (
69
), and these factors are no more numerous than
\displaystyle N
. So the re-shiftings add at worst a bounded non zero term to the error bound (
74
), which changes only
\displaystyle\mathrm{c}_{M}
. Eventually, the ratio of (
69
) over (
70
), or equivalently (
71
), is bounded from above and below by the product of
\displaystyle N+1
positive constants independent of
\displaystyle N
, with
\displaystyle N
of them raised at the power
\displaystyle 1/N
. Hence the ratio (
69
)/(
71
) is bounded by a constant. To conclude, it therefore suffices to prove that

(75)

\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}})-\frac{1}{2i\pi N}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{N{\bf l}_{0,N}})\right)

is bounded from above and below by constants independent of
\displaystyle N
multiplied with
\displaystyle e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\mathfrak{I}({\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}))}
. But
\displaystyle{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{N{\bf l}_{0,N}})={\rm Li_{2}}(u_{0})
, and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}={\bf l}_{0,\infty}+O_{N\to\infty}\left(N^{-1}\right)
implies

{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}})={\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}})+O_{N\to\infty}(1/N)

by a Taylor expansion as in (
60
) (which is valid, since
\displaystyle{\rm Li_{2}}
is differentiable on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\setminus[1,+\infty[
, and
\displaystyle e^{N{\bf l}_{0,N}}=u_{0}\notin]1,+\infty[
implies
\displaystyle e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}}\notin]1,+\infty[
). It follows

\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}})\right)=\exp\left(\frac{N}{2\pi}\mathfrak{I}({\rm Li_{2}}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}))\right)\exp(O_{N\to\infty}(1)).

This proves the last claim, and concludes the proof of the lemma when
\displaystyle\Im(u_{0})>0
. Similar arguments apply when
\displaystyle\Im(u_{0})<0
: take
\displaystyle\delta_{N}=\pi/N
, and start the sequence (
73
) at
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}+2i\pi/N
. Only the arguments
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}+2i\pi
on the right side of (
69
) are missing from this sequence and the following one. With these changes one can conclude by reasoning as when
\displaystyle\Im(u_{0})>0
.
\displaystyle\Box

3.3.
The QHI of cusped manifolds

The aim of this section is to introduce some general features of the QHI, provide links to the literature (thus explaining notations), and to explain the splitting formula of the QHI into the symmetry defects and the reduced QHI. We refer to
[
4
]
for the definition of the QHI of cusped manifolds by means of state sums over geometric branched ideal triangulations, and to
[
7
]
for the general situation of non necessarily branched triangulations. The QHI of cusped manifolds used in this paper are those of
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
, in the case where the bulk weights
\displaystyle h_{f},h_{c}
are zero, or are determined by the boundary weights
\displaystyle k_{f},k_{c}
(as for link complements). (By
[
8
, Corollary 1.8 (1)]
, another choice of bulk weight would at worst modify these QHI by multiplication with a
\displaystyle 4N
-th root of unity, which has no consequence on the results of this paper.)

Let
\displaystyle M
be a cusped manifold. As usual we denote by
\displaystyle\bar{M}
the compact manifold with interior
\displaystyle M
,
\displaystyle X(M)
is the variety of
\displaystyle PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters of
\displaystyle M
, and
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)\subset X(M)
is the “geometric” irreducible component, containing the character
\displaystyle\rho_{hyp}
of the complete hyperbolic holonomy (see Section
2.2.2
).

The QHI of
\displaystyle M
are sequences of complex numbers
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c},k_{f})
modulo multiplication by
\displaystyle 2N
-th roots of unity, indexed by the odd positive integers
\displaystyle N\geq 3
. The arguments of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c},k_{f})
are:

(i)

a character
\displaystyle\rho\in X_{hyp}(M)
,

(ii)

classes
\displaystyle k_{c}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M},\mathbb{Z})
and
\displaystyle k_{f}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M},\mathbb{C})
satisfying (
20
).

The invariant
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c},k_{f})
is
computed
by the total contractions, denoted
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}
and called
QHI state sums
, of certain tensor networks associated to the ideal triangulations
\displaystyle T
of
\displaystyle M
, similarly as in the Turaev-Viro TQFTs. The QHI state sums are functions of combinatorial data on
\displaystyle T
encoding
\displaystyle\rho\in X(M)
and
\displaystyle k_{c},k_{f}
as in (i)-(ii):
\displaystyle\rho
is encoded by a point
\displaystyle w_{\rho}
of the gluing variety
\displaystyle G(T,b)
(for some
\displaystyle b
), which is possible for most characters
\displaystyle\rho
in
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
(see Remark
2.9
), and the classes
\displaystyle k_{f}
and
\displaystyle k_{c}
are a flattening weight and a charge weight, encoded by flattenings
\displaystyle f
and charges
\displaystyle c
as in Remark
2.4
-
2.5
. When
\displaystyle\rho
is contained in
\displaystyle X_{hyp}(M)
all the choices of
\displaystyle(T,b,w_{\rho},c,f)
eventually give the QHI state sums the same value, which by definition is
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c},k_{f})
.

It is shown in
[
8
]
that, up to a possible stronger ambiguity modulo multiplication by
\displaystyle 4N
th-roots of unity, which we denote by
\displaystyle=_{\mu_{4N}}
, we have a factorization into refined invariants (recall that
\displaystyle l\kappa:=k_{f}-i\pi k_{c}
)

(76)

{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{f},k_{c})=_{\mu_{4N}}\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c};\mathfrak{s}){\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,l\kappa;\mathfrak{s}),

called respectively the
symmetry defect
and the
reduced
QHI. These refined invariants depend on an additional structure
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}
on
\displaystyle M
, called a
non ambiguous
structure, which is of topological nature (
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}
is an equivalence class of ideal triangulations under a restricted set of branched Pachner
\displaystyle 2-3
moves). The argument
\displaystyle l\kappa
on the right side is the log weight, see (
18
) and (
21
) (in
[
8
]
it was denoted
\displaystyle\kappa
, and called a fused weight).

Remark 3.9
.

When
\displaystyle M
fibers over
\displaystyle S^{1}
,
any
fibration
\displaystyle\psi\colon M\rightarrow S^{1}
(with oriented fibers) defines a canonical non ambiguous structure
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{\psi}
on
\displaystyle M
, carried by the ideal triangulations of
\displaystyle M
which are layered triangulations for the fibration (
[
8
, Prop. 6.3(1)]
). Such triangulations are taut, which allows to associate to them a canonical charge, as in Remark
2.4
, whence a canonical charge weight
\displaystyle k_{\psi}
, as in Remark
2.5
, to the fibration (
[
8
, Prop. 4.3(2) and 4.8(2)]
). From the definition of the symmetry defect (
[
8
, page 808]
), it is quite immediate to check that
\displaystyle\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{\psi};\mathfrak{s}_{\psi})=_{4N}1
for every
\displaystyle\rho
, whence
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{f},k_{\psi})=_{\mu_{4N}}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,k_{f}-i\pi k_{\psi};\mathfrak{s}_{\psi})
. Note, however, that the classes
\displaystyle k_{\psi}
are very peculiar, and a layered triangulation can carry several (possibly taut) non ambiguous structures, that the symmetry defect
\displaystyle\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c};\mathfrak{s})
can distinguish (see the examples in
[
8
, Section 9]
).

Recall the relations (
16
)-(
17
). By setting

(77)

h_{\rho}(\gamma):=l\kappa(\gamma)-{\rm Log}(\delta_{w_{\rho}}(\gamma))\in i\pi\mathbb{Z}

and using that
\displaystyle l\kappa=k_{f}-i\pi k_{c}
(see (
21
)), we can explain the notations of Theorem
1.1
:

(78)

{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c}):={\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,k_{f},k_{c}).

Now, for every class
\displaystyle a\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\pi iN\mathbb{Z})
we have
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,l\kappa+a;\mathfrak{s})={\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,l\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
(see
[
8
, Theorem 1.6 (3)]
). From this and (
17
) it follows that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,l\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
, regarded as a function of
\displaystyle l\kappa
, eventually depends only the boundary weight
\displaystyle\kappa\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{C}^{*})
, defined in (
23
). We shall therefore denote

(79)

{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s}):={\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,l\kappa;\mathfrak{s}).

Recall that, since
\displaystyle\kappa
is given by its values on a basis of
\displaystyle H_{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
, the relations (
16
)-(
17
) show that
\displaystyle\kappa
is a choice of
\displaystyle N
-th roots of the values of
\displaystyle\delta_{w_{\rho}}
on these basis elements. Combining (
76
), (
78
) and (
79
) we can write

(80)

{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})=_{\mu_{4N}}\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c};\mathfrak{s}){\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s}).

The invariant
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
or
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
, for fixed
\displaystyle k_{c}
and varying couple
\displaystyle(\rho,k_{f})
, or fixed
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}
and varying couple
\displaystyle(\rho,\kappa)
respectively, defines a rational function on the variety
\displaystyle{}_{N}X_{hyp}(M)
of Section
2.2.2
(
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
8
, Corollary 1.8]
). This function is obtained from the QHI state sums
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}
: these are rational functions on the space
\displaystyle G_{hyp,N}(T,b)
defined in (
26
), and factor through the map
\displaystyle\widetilde{hol}
in (
28
).

3.3.1.
The case of
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}

It follows immediately from definitions (see
[
8
, Section 8]
) that
\displaystyle\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c};\mathfrak{s})
is in
\displaystyle PM(N)
, as defined in Section
1.3
, for every fixed
\displaystyle\rho\in X_{hyp}(M)
,
\displaystyle k_{c}\in H^{1}(\partial\bar{M};\mathbb{Z})
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}
. Because of the splitting (
80
), the proof of Theorem
1.1
is therefore reduced to the asymptotic analysis of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
.

Let us give the details of this argument for
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
. The invariant
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
has a simple state sum
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N})
on the branched triangulation
\displaystyle(T,b)
of Figure
1
, given in (
82
) below. In the notations of Section
2.3
the couple
\displaystyle(\rho,\kappa)
is encoded by the parameters

{\bf u}_{k,N}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\left({\rm Log}(u_{k})+\pi i(N+1)a_{k}\right)\right)\ ,\ {\bf v}_{k,N}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\left({\rm Log}(v_{k})+\pi i(N+1)b_{k}\right)\right)

for
\displaystyle k\in\{0,1\}
, where
\displaystyle u_{k},v_{k}\in\mathbb{C}_{*}
are fixed shape parameters of the tetrahedra of
\displaystyle T
. The non ambiguous structure
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}
is defined by the branching
\displaystyle b
of
\displaystyle T
, and was studied in
[
8
, Section 9.2]
(where it was denoted
\displaystyle[(T,\omega_{1})]
). The symmetry defect
\displaystyle\alpha_{N}(M,\rho,k_{c};\mathfrak{s})
is computed on
\displaystyle(T,b)
by the monomial
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})^{\frac{N-1}{2}}
for a certain charge
\displaystyle c
.

From (
80
) and this discussion it follows that the state sum expression of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(M,\rho,h_{\rho},k_{c})
on
\displaystyle(T,b)
is

(81)

{\mathcal{H}}_{N,(T,b,c)}({\bf u}_{N},{\bf v}_{N}):=({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})^{\frac{N-1}{2}}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf v}_{0,N},{\bf v}_{1,N}).

Lemma 3.10
.

We have:

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\right|=0.

Proof.

Indeed

\displaystyle\displaystyle\dfrac{2\pi}{N}{\rm Log}\left|({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\dfrac{2\pi}{N}\frac{N-1}{2}{\rm Log}\left|({\bf u}_{0,N}^{-1}{\bf v}_{0,N}^{-1})\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\pi\frac{N-1}{N^{2}}\mathfrak{R}({\rm Log}(u_{0})+{\rm Log}(v_{0}))

using the above expressions of
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{k,N}
,
\displaystyle{\bf v}_{k,N}
in the last equality. The result follows.∎

3.4.
The QHI state sum of
\displaystyle M=S^{3}\setminus 4_{1}

In this section we describe the formula of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
in the case of the figure-eight knot complement
\displaystyle M
, using the branched ideal triangulation
\displaystyle(T,b)
of Figure
1
.

Recall the lifted holonomy map
\displaystyle\widetilde{hol}
in (
28
); by (
32
) we have
\displaystyle G_{hyp,N}(T,b)=G_{N}(T,b)
. For any
\displaystyle\rho\in hol(G(T,b))\subset X_{hyp}(M)
and compatible boundary weight
\displaystyle\kappa
, let
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1},{\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})\in G_{N}(T,b)
(as in Section
2.3
) be such that
\displaystyle\widetilde{hol}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1},{\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})=(\rho,\kappa)
. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}
the non ambiguous structure carried by
\displaystyle(T,b)
.

By
[
5
, Section 6.4, page 912]
and the formula (
80
), the reduced QHI of
\displaystyle(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})
can be expressed by the state sum

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}(M,\rho,\kappa;\mathfrak{s})

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{red}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1},{\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{g_{N}({\bf u}_{0})g_{N}({\bf v}_{0}^{*})^{*}}{|g(1)|^{2}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{N-1}\zeta^{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}\omega_{N}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1}^{-1}|\beta)\ \omega_{N}({\bf v}_{0}^{*},{\bf v}_{1}^{*}{}^{-1}|\alpha)^{*}

(82)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{g_{N}({\bf u}_{0})g_{N}({\bf v}_{0}^{*})^{*}}{|g(1)|^{2}}\ \Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1})\ \Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0}^{*},{\bf v}_{1}^{*})^{*}.

where
∗
denotes the complex conjugation, the functions
\displaystyle\omega_{N}
and
\displaystyle g_{N}
were defined in (
46
) and (
67
), and we put

(83)

\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1})=\sum_{\beta=0}^{N-1}\zeta^{\beta^{2}}\omega_{N}({\bf u}_{0},{\bf u}_{1}^{-1}|\beta).

3.5.
Integral representation

As we are going to consider the functions in (
82
) when
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
, we re-introduce now the hidden dependence on
\displaystyle N
in the notations, thus letting
\displaystyle({\bf u}_{N})_{N}:=(({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N},{\bf u}_{2,N}))_{N}
be a sequence of triples of quantum shape parameters on
\displaystyle\Delta^{0}
as in Section
2.3
. Contrary to the more general situation considered in Section
2.4
, here we assume that
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{k,N}^{N}
is constant. Thus, as in (
34
) we write

(84)

{\bf u}_{k,N}=\exp({\bf l}_{k,N}),\ {\bf l}_{k,N}:=\frac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(u_{k})+\pi ia_{k})+\pi ia_{k},a_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}.

Using the formula (
54
) one can write (
83
) as

(85)

\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})=1+\frac{1}{\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N-1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\beta^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}\beta}\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+2\beta i\pi/N).

Note that for
\displaystyle\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
\displaystyle e^{{\bf l}_{1,N}\beta}=e^{({\bf l}_{1,N}+2i\pi k)\beta}
for any
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
, so the formula (
85
) is invariant under translation of
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,N}
by
\displaystyle 2i\pi
. In fact, because the QHI solely depend on the quantum shape parameters
\displaystyle{\bf u}_{k,N}
and
\displaystyle{\bf v}_{k,N}
, by adding even integers to
\displaystyle a_{0}
or
\displaystyle a_{1}
if necessary we can assume that in the above formula we have

(86)

\forall N,\ \mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,N})\in]-2\pi,0]\ ,\ \mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{1,N})\in]0,2\pi].

Recall that the poles of
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}(z)
are the points
\displaystyle z=2i\pi+2pi\pi/N
,
\displaystyle p\in\mathbb{N}
, the simple poles being those with
\displaystyle p\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
(see (
53
)). On another hand the poles of
\displaystyle\coth(Nz/2)
are the points
\displaystyle z=2i\pi\beta/N
,
\displaystyle\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
, and they are all simple poles, with residue
\displaystyle 2/N
. Because
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,N})\subset]-2\pi,0]
, it follows that the function

\textstyle\phi\colon z\mapsto\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})\frac{N}{2}

is meromorphic in the half-space
\displaystyle\{\textstyle\mathfrak{I}(z)<2\pi\}
, and has poles
\displaystyle\textstyle z_{\beta}:=2i\pi\beta/N
where
\displaystyle\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\displaystyle\beta\leq N-1
. These poles are simple, and
\displaystyle{\rm Res}(\phi,z_{\beta})=\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+2\beta i\pi/N)
.

Let
\displaystyle C_{N}
be the boundary of a rectangle with counter clockwise orientation, sides parallel to the real and imaginary axes, containing the segment
\displaystyle\textstyle i[\frac{2\pi}{N},2\pi-\frac{2\pi}{N}]
in its interior, and close to it so that we have constants
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
and
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-},s_{N}^{+}\in]0,\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}[
satisfying

(87)

C_{N}\subset\{|\mathfrak{R}(z)|\leq\varepsilon\}\cap\{s_{N}^{-}\leq\mathfrak{I}(z)\leq 2\pi-s_{N}^{+}\}\ .

See Figure
6
. An immediate application of Cauchy’s residue theorem gives:

Lemma 3.11
.

Assuming (
86
) and (
87
), we have

(88)

\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})=1+\frac{1}{\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N})}\frac{N}{4i\pi}\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz.

3.6.
Some logarithmic limits of log-parameters

In this section we focus on the peculiar logarithmic limits of log-parameters (
40
) selected by the two situations (a)-(b) of Theorem
1.1
. These log-parameters have the form (
84
) (at least for
\displaystyle N
large enough); that is, they have constant shape parameters
\displaystyle u_{k}\in\mathbb{C}_{*}
and constant edge colors
\displaystyle a_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this situation, and assuming (
86
), as usual we put, for
\displaystyle k\in\{0,1\}
:

{\bf l}_{k,\infty}^{\bf u}:=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf l}_{k,N}.

The edge colors
\displaystyle a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}
solve the linear system (
36
)-(
37
)-(
38
), where
\displaystyle a_{0}
is a free variable. Then, taking
\displaystyle a_{0}
even and
\displaystyle\geq 2
if necessary (so that
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{I}({\rm Log}(u_{0})+\pi ia_{0})>0
), the assumption (
86
) implies

(89)

{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{\bf u}=-2i\pi.

By the equations (
38
) and the comments thereafter, when
\displaystyle a_{0}
is positive and big enough,
\displaystyle a_{1}
is negative. Then, again by (
86
), we have the following alternative:

(90)

{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{\bf u}=\left\{\begin{matrix}i\pi&\text{(case}(a):a_{1}\ \text{is\ odd}),\\
2i\pi&\text{(case}(b):a_{1}\ \text{is\ even}).\end{matrix}\right.

Note that (
89
)-(
90
) describe the possible limits of the parameters of the integral representation of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf u}_{0,N},{\bf u}_{1,N})
in (
88
).

Next consider
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*},{\bf v}_{1,N}^{*})
. As usual set
\displaystyle{\bf v}_{k,N}:=\exp({\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf v})
,
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf v}:=\frac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(v_{k})+\pi ib_{k})+\pi ib_{k}
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k,\infty}^{\bf v}:=\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf l}_{k,N}^{\bf v}
. Then
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{k,N}^{{\bf v}^{*}}=\frac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(v_{k})^{*}-\pi ib_{k})-\pi ib_{k}
, and we consider
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k,\infty}^{{\bf v}^{*}}:=\textstyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\bf l}_{k,N}^{{\bf v}^{*}}
assuming
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(({\bf l}_{k,N}^{{\bf v}^{*}}))
satisfies (
86
). By using the expressions of
\displaystyle b_{0}
,
\displaystyle b_{1}
in the equations (
38
), the same analysis as above shows that, again for
\displaystyle a_{0}
even, positive, and big enough, we have

(91)

{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}^{*}}=-2i\pi\quad,\quad{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}^{*}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}i\pi&\text{(case}(a):a_{1}\ \text{is\ odd}),\\
2i\pi&\text{(case}(b):a_{1}\ \text{is\ even}).\end{matrix}\right.

These values give the possible limits of the parameters of the integral representation of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}({\bf v}_{0,N}^{*},{\bf v}_{1,N}^{*})
.

Lemma 3.12
.

The two cases (a) and (b) of Theorem
1.1
correspond respectively to the cases (a) and (b) of Theorem
1.2
and to the cases (a):
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{\bf x}=-2i\pi
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{\bf x}=i\pi
and (b):
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{\bf x}=-2i\pi
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{\bf x}=2i\pi
, for both
\displaystyle{\bf x}={\bf u}
and
\displaystyle{\bf v}^{*}
.

Proof.

By the equations (
38
) the parity of
\displaystyle a_{1}
and
\displaystyle b_{1}
is the parity of
\displaystyle h_{\rho}(\lambda_{K})/2i\pi
. The conclusion then follows from (
89
)-(
90
)-(
91
).
∎

4.
Asymptotics of classical integrals on a vertical contour

The analysis of the integral on the RHS of (
88
), with the logarithmic limits
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}
specified by Lemma
3.12
, will be done in Section
5
. Here, as a warm-up situation we focus on simpler integrals
\displaystyle I_{\pm,N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
, that we call “classical” integrals, with integrands given by an exponential term taken from the asymptotics of the integrand in (
88
) as
\displaystyle N\to+\infty
. These classical integrals are defined in Section
4.1
; there, we provide also motivations for the rest of Section
4
. In particular, in Lemma
4.1
we will see that the critical sets of the integrands recover a neighborhood of the geometric point
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
of the gluing variety
\displaystyle G(T,b)
. The rest of Section
4
is devoted to the asymptotics of
\displaystyle I_{\pm,N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
, again with the logarithmic limits specified by Lemma
3.12
; the proofs will make no use of Lemma
4.1
(all related facts are checked in the given situations).

4.1.
Classical integrals and their critical sets

Recall the contour
\displaystyle C_{N}
from (
87
). By the assumption (
86
), when
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
both cases
\displaystyle 0<\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)<2\pi
and
\displaystyle-2\pi<\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)<0
may happen. If
\displaystyle 0<\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)<2\pi
, then
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)
is given by the formulas (
47
)-(
50
), and Lemma
3.3
yields

(92)

\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)=e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})}\mathcal{O}_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1).

If
\displaystyle-2\pi<\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)<0
, we get
\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)=(1-e^{N({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)})\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z+2\pi i)
by using
\displaystyle N
times the functional equation (
52
); so in this case we have

(93)

\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}e^{N{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})+2i\pi z)}\mathcal{O}_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)&{\rm if}\ \mathfrak{R}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)>0\\
e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})}\mathcal{O}_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)&{\rm if}\ \mathfrak{R}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)<0.\end{array}\right.

It is easily checked that
\displaystyle\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})
is bounded on
\displaystyle C_{N}
, and therefore the integrand on the RHS of (
88
) satisfies, for
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
:

(94)

e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})=\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}e^{N{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})}\mathcal{O}_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)&{\rm if}\ \mathfrak{R}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)>0\ {\rm and}\\
&-2\pi<\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z)<0,\\
e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})}\mathcal{O}_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)&{\rm otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where

(95)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})+z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}z,&\\
\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})+z^{2}-({\bf l}_{1,\infty}-2i\pi)z.&\end{array}\right.

Note that the two halves of
\displaystyle C_{N}
, in the left and right half spaces of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
, are homotopic to
\displaystyle i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
(with opposite orientations) relatively to their endpoints. We call

(96)

I_{\pm,N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})=\int_{i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{\pm}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})}dz

the
classical integrals
.

Because of (
94
), it is reasonable to expect that the asymptotics of the integrals
\displaystyle I_{\pm,N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
have a lot to do with those of the integral on the RHS of (
88
) (see Lemma
5.1
for a rigourous statement). Then, in view of the saddle point method (see Section
4.2
), we are naturally let to consider the critical sets of the functions
\displaystyle z\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\pm}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{\bf x},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{\bf x})
, where the parameters
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{\bf x}
,
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{\bf x}
, with
\displaystyle{\bf x}={\bf u}
and
\displaystyle{\bf v}
, are associated to the tetrehedra
\displaystyle\Delta^{0}
and
\displaystyle\Delta^{1}
, as usual. In particular, by Lemma
2.10
it is interesting to consider the situations where
\displaystyle(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}}},e^{{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}}},e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}}},e^{{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}}})\in\{0\}\times\mathcal{A}(T,b)\subset\mathcal{C}(T,b)\times\mathcal{A}(T,b)
. By (
41
) and (
44
), such a tuple
\displaystyle({\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}},{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}})
satisfies the sole relation

(97)

{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+2{\bf l}_{2,\infty}^{{\bf u}}-2{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}}-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}}=0\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i),

and it determines an asymptotic angular holonomy by the system

(98)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}}+{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}}&=-i\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\mu_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i)\\
4{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+2{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}}&=i\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i).\end{array}\right.

Denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(T,b)
the set of solutions of the equation (
97
). Let us define a sign

(99)

\star:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}+1&{\rm if}\ \ \textstyle 2{\bf l}_{0}^{{\bf u},\infty}+{\bf l}_{1}^{{\bf u},\infty}=\frac{i\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))}{2}\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i)\\
-1&{\rm if}\ \ \textstyle 2{\bf l}_{0}^{{\bf u},\infty}+{\bf l}_{1}^{{\bf u},\infty}=\frac{i\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))}{2}+\pi i\quad{\rm mod}(2\pi i).\end{array}\right.

Lemma 4.1
.

The function
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\pm}(\cdot;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
has a unique critical point
\displaystyle z_{0}:=z_{0}^{\pm}({\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
, and this point satisfies the equation:

(100)

\star e^{-\frac{i\arg(\kappa^{\infty}(\lambda_{K}))}{2}}e^{2({\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z_{0})}+e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z_{0}}-1=0.

Moreover, the image of the map

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\Xi:&\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(T,b)&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{C}^{2}\\
&({\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}},{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}})&\longmapsto&\left(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}}+z_{0}^{-}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf u}},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf u}})},e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}}+z_{0}^{+}({\bf l}_{0,\infty}^{{\bf v}},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}^{{\bf v}})}\right)\end{array}

is contained in
\displaystyle G(T,b)
and covers a neighborhood of
\displaystyle w_{hyp}
, the geometric solution to the gluing equations.

Proof.

We have

(101)

\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{z}\mathcal{L}_{-})(z_{0};{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})=0\Longleftrightarrow 2z_{0}-{\rm Log}(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z_{0}})-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=0,\\
(\partial_{z}\mathcal{L}_{+})(z_{0};{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})=0\Longleftrightarrow 2z_{0}-{\rm Log}(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z_{0}})+2i\pi-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=0.\end{array}

Passing to the exponential, both equations yield the following degree two equation in
\displaystyle e^{z_{0}}
:

(102)

e^{-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}}e^{2z_{0}}+e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}e^{z_{0}}-1=0.

The two roots have product
\displaystyle-e^{{\bf l}_{1,\infty}}
, hence their arguments sum to
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{1,\infty})+\pi
mod
\displaystyle(2\pi)
. On the other hand, the first equation of (
101
) imposes
\displaystyle 2\mathfrak{I}(z_{0})\in]-\pi+\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{1,\infty});\pi+\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{1,\infty})]
, and the second imposes
\displaystyle 2\mathfrak{I}(z_{0})\in]-3\pi+\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{1,\infty});-\pi+\mathfrak{I}({\bf l}_{1,\infty})]
. Combining the two facts, we see that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
have exactly one critical point for every fixed values of
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}
. The identity (
100
) follows immediately from (
102
) and the second relation in (
98
). This proves the first claim.

From (
102
) we get

(103)

e^{-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}-2{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}=e^{-2(z_{0}+{\bf l}_{0,\infty})}(1-e^{z_{0}+{\bf l}_{0,\infty}}).

It is also an immediate check that this identity and the first of (
97
) imply that
\displaystyle(u_{0},v_{0}):=(e^{{\bf l}_{0}^{{\bf u},\infty}+z_{0}^{\pm}({\bf l}_{0}^{{\bf u},\infty},{\bf l}_{1}^{{\bf u},\infty})},e^{{\bf l}_{0}^{{\bf v},\infty}+z_{0}^{\pm}({\bf l}_{0}^{{\bf v},\infty},{\bf l}_{1}^{{\bf v},\infty})})
is a solution of the defining equation (
29
) of
\displaystyle G(T,b)
. This proves the second claim.

Finally note that
\displaystyle\Xi(-2\pi i,\pi i,2\pi i,-\pi i)=(e^{\pi i/3},e^{-\pi i/3})
. A straightforward computation shows that the Jacobian of
\displaystyle\Xi
at
\displaystyle(-2\pi i,\pi i,2\pi i,-\pi i)
has real rank
\displaystyle 2
. Since
\displaystyle G(T,b)
is a curve and
\displaystyle(e^{\pi i/3},e^{-\pi i/3})
is a smooth point, this shows that
\displaystyle\Xi
is a submersion, and the last claim follows.
\displaystyle\Box

We note the following simple caracterization of critical points
\displaystyle z_{0}
which are saddle points of order
\displaystyle 1
:

\partial_{z}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{\pm}(z_{0};{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})=1+(1-e^{{\rm l}_{0}+z_{0}})^{-1}\neq 0.

4.2.
The saddle-point method

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
denote a piecewise continuously differentiable path contained in an open
set
\displaystyle\Delta\subset\mathbb{C}
, and let
\displaystyle z\mapsto g(z)
and
\displaystyle z\mapsto f(z)
denote two holomorphic functions on
\displaystyle\Delta
.
We consider the asymptotics of the integral

(104)

I_{n}:=\int_{\lambda}g(z)e^{n(-i)f(z)}dz\quad,\ n\to+\infty.

Let us recall a concise version of the one-dimensional saddle point method theorem (see also
[
51
]
). Note that usually the condition (iii) below is written as a maximum of the real part of the potential function. Here we consider
\displaystyle n(-i)f(z)
instead of
\displaystyle nf(z)
, so we look for a maximum of the imaginary part instead (this is mostly in order to work with
\displaystyle{\rm Li}_{2}
instead of
\displaystyle i{\rm Li}_{2}
in the following sections).

Proposition 4.2

(
[
55
]
, Section 2)
.

Assume there exists an interior point
\displaystyle z_{0}\in\lambda
such that

(i)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z_{0})=0
,

(ii)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})\neq 0
,

(iii)

\displaystyle\Im f(z)<\Im f(z_{0})
for all
\displaystyle z\neq z_{0}
on the closure of
\displaystyle\lambda
in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
,

and
\displaystyle g(z_{0})\neq 0
. Then we have:

I_{n}=\dfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{i\theta}g(z_{0})\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{|f^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})|}}e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}\left(1+o_{n\to\infty}(1)\right),

for some explicit
\displaystyle\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n}\right|=\Im f(z_{0}).

In the following sections we will use this result to study the integrals
\displaystyle I_{\pm,N}({\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty})
in (
96
) for the particular values
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}\in\{i\pi,2i\pi\}
obtained in Section
3.6
. Therefore we set

n:=\frac{N}{2\pi}

(as usual with
\displaystyle N\geq 3
odd);
\displaystyle g
will be the constant map equal to
\displaystyle 1
, and
\displaystyle f
one of the following two functions, introduced in (
95
):

(105)

f(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{-}(z):=\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})+z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,\infty}z&\\
f_{+}(z):=\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;{\bf l}_{0,\infty},{\bf l}_{1,\infty}):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{{\bf l}_{0,\infty}+z})+z^{2}-({\bf l}_{1,\infty}-2i\pi)z.\end{array}\right.

With these notations, and for
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{-},s_{N}^{+}\in]0,\frac{2\pi}{N}[
, we are going to describe some asymptotics of the integrals

(106)

I_{n,\pm}:=\int_{i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]}e^{n(-i)f_{\pm}(z)}dz.

Note that the endpoints
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-},s_{N}^{+}
of the contour are moving to
\displaystyle 0
or
\displaystyle 2\pi i
as
\displaystyle N\rightarrow+\infty
, which lie on branch cuts of the functions
\displaystyle f_{\pm}
. Eventually, our analysis will be achieved by taking
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}
not too small, namely (see the end of Section
4.3.2
)

(107)

s_{N}^{-}:=\alpha\frac{\pi}{N},\ \alpha\in]0,1[.

We begin in Section
4.3
with the asymptotics of integrals along small contours in
\displaystyle i\mathbb{R}
, near
\displaystyle 0
or
\displaystyle 2i\pi
. These asymptotics will be used several times in the following sections.

4.3.
Asymptotics of integrals
\displaystyle\textstyle\int e^{\scriptscriptstyle{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}}dt

4.3.1.
Along
\displaystyle[\pi,2\pi]+2k\pi
,
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Recall that Clausen’s function

(108)

{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}{\rm Log}|2\sin(t/2)|dt

is
\displaystyle 2\pi
-periodic and odd (
[
63
]
). Therefore it is enough to make our analysis over
\displaystyle[-\pi,0]
. Since
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}^{\prime}_{2}(t)=-{\rm Log}|2\sin(t/2)|
for all
\displaystyle t\notin 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}^{\prime\prime}_{2}(t)=-\cot(t/2)/2
for the same
\displaystyle t
, which implies that the Clausen function is convex on
\displaystyle[-\pi,0]
. It vanishes at
\displaystyle-\pi
and
\displaystyle 0
, is negative on
\displaystyle]-\pi,0[
, and has a strict minimum
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}_{2}\left(-\pi/3\right)=-1.10149...
that we will denote
\displaystyle-m
.

Since the graph of a convex function lies under its chords, it follows

{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)\leqslant-\dfrac{3m}{2\pi}t-\dfrac{3m}{2}\ \text{for\ all}\ t\in\left[-\pi,-\frac{\pi}{3}\right],\ \text{and}\ {\rm Cl}_{2}(t)\leqslant\dfrac{3m}{\pi}t\ \text{for\ all}\ t\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{3},0\right].

Hence we can compute the upper bound:

\displaystyle\displaystyle 0<\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{-\pi/3}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt+\int_{-\pi/3}^{0}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{-\pi}^{-\pi/3}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\left(-\frac{3m}{2\pi}t-\frac{3m}{2}\right)}dt+\int_{-\pi/3}^{0}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\frac{3m}{\pi}t}dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{3Nm}{4\pi}}\frac{(-4)\pi^{2}}{3mN}\left(e^{\frac{Nm}{4\pi}}-e^{\frac{3Nm}{4\pi}}\right)+\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3mN}\left(1-e^{-\frac{Nm}{2\pi}}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{mN}\left(1+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

Since
\displaystyle e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}}\geqslant 0
, this generalizes to

\int_{I}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)

for any sub-part
\displaystyle I
of
\displaystyle[-\pi,0]
or
\displaystyle[\pi,2\pi]
.

4.3.2.
Along
\displaystyle[0,s_{N}^{-}]

The Clausen function is positive and increasing on
\displaystyle[0,\pi/3]
, so to get an upper bound it is enough to discuss the asymptotical behaviour of the constant integral

\int_{[0,s_{N}^{-}]}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}=s_{N}^{-}\cdot e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}.

We want to prove that
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}\cdot e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)
.

Recall that
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{-}\in]0,\frac{2\pi}{N}[
. For all
\displaystyle x\in(0,1)
we have

\frac{x}{2}<x-\frac{x}{3}<x-\frac{x^{3}}{3}<\sin(x)<x.

Thus, for any
\displaystyle N\geqslant 7
and any
\displaystyle t\in(0,s_{N}^{-}]
we have
