\frac{t}{2}=\frac{2(\frac{t}{2})}{2}<2\sin\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)<2\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)=t,

and therefore
\displaystyle{\rm Log}(t)-{\rm Log}(2)<{\rm Log}\left(2\sin\left(t/2\right)\right)<{\rm Log}(t)
. By integrating the opposite of this inequality, we obtain for all
\displaystyle t\in(0,s_{N}^{-}]
,

t-t{\rm Log}(t)<{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)<{\rm Log}(2)t+t-t{\rm Log}(t).

(This notably implies that
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)\sim_{t\to 0^{+}}-t{\rm Log}(t)
but we will use the more precise double inequality in what follows.) By evaluating the previous double inequality at
\displaystyle t=s_{N}^{-}
we get

-s_{N}{\rm Log}(s_{N}^{-})<{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})<{\rm Log}(2)s_{N}^{-}+s_{N}^{-}-s_{N}^{-}{\rm Log}(s_{N}^{-})

and thus

s_{N}^{-}\cdot e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}<s_{N}^{-}\cdot e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}s_{N}\left({\rm Log}(2)+1-{\rm Log}(s_{N}^{-})\right)}.

In particular, if
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}=\alpha\frac{\pi}{N}
, where
\displaystyle\alpha\in]0,1[
, then we get

s_{N}^{-}\cdot e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}<\alpha\frac{\pi}{N}\cdot e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}\left({\rm Log}(2)+1+{\rm Log}(N)-{\rm Log}(\pi)-{\rm Log}(\alpha)\right)}=\dfrac{\text{Constant}}{N^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

4.4.
The limit in case (a)

Recall (
89
)-(
90
) and (
105
)-(
106
). In case (a) we have
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=i\pi
, so

(109)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{-}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,i\pi):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}-i\pi z,&\\
f_{+}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,i\pi):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}+i\pi z.\end{array}\right.

We will prove, using Proposition
4.2
for
\displaystyle I_{n,-}
, that

(110)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,-}\right|=\dfrac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M)\ \ \text{and}\ \ I_{n,+}=O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1).

By Lemma
1.3
this will imply asymptotics of linear combinations of
\displaystyle I_{n,-}
and
\displaystyle I_{n,+}
, such as

(111)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,-}\pm I_{n,+}\right|=\dfrac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M).

4.4.1.
Case (a), integral
\displaystyle I_{n,-}

Let us prove that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,-}\right|=\dfrac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M)
.

First we show that
\displaystyle f_{-}(z)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}-i\pi z
and
\displaystyle\textstyle z_{0}:={i\frac{\pi}{3}}
satisfy the properties of Proposition
4.2
for the contour
\displaystyle\lambda=i[\eta,2\pi-\eta^{\prime}]
, where
\displaystyle\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
.

We have
\displaystyle\partial_{z}{\rm Li}_{2}(z)=-{\rm Log}(1-z)/z
, so
\displaystyle\partial_{z}{\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})=-{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})e^{-z}.e^{z}=-{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})
, and therefore

(f_{-})^{\prime}(z)=2z-{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})-i\pi,\ \text{and}\ (f_{-})^{\prime\prime}(z)=2+\frac{e^{z}}{1-e^{z}}=1+\frac{1}{1-e^{z}}.

It follows
\displaystyle(f_{-})^{\prime}(z_{0})=(2i\pi/3)-{\rm Log}(1-e^{i\pi/3})-i\pi=(-i\pi/3)-{\rm Log}(e^{-i\frac{\pi}{3}})=0
, which checks (i). Also
\displaystyle(f_{-})^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})=1+(1-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}})^{-1}=1+e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\neq 0
, which checks (ii).

Let us check property (iii). We parametrize the contour
\displaystyle\lambda=i[\eta,2\pi-\eta^{\prime}]
by
\displaystyle z=it
, where
\displaystyle t\in[\eta,2\pi-\eta^{\prime}]
. Let us prove that
\displaystyle[0,2\pi]\ni t\mapsto\Im(f_{-})(it)
has a strict maximum at
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\pi}{3}
.

Recall (
[
63
]
) that

(112)

\forall y\in\mathbb{C},\ \mathfrak{I}({\rm Li}_{2}(y))=-\arg(1-y){\rm Log}|y|+D(y),

where
\displaystyle\arg
is the branch of the argument with values in
\displaystyle]-\pi,\pi]
, and
\displaystyle D(y)
is the Bloch-Wigner dilogarithm, which computes the algebraic volume of an oriented hyperbolic ideal tetrahedron with cross–ratio modulus
\displaystyle y
. Hence we have

\Im(f_{-})(it)=\Im\left({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{it})+(it)^{2}-i\pi it\right)=\Im\left({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{it})\right)=D(e^{it})={\rm Cl}_{2}(t),

where
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}_{2}
is Clausen’s function (
108
). Since it achieves its strict local maxima at the points
\displaystyle\pi/3+2\pi n
,
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{Z}
, (iii) follows.

We can now apply Proposition
4.2

and conclude that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|\int_{[i\eta,2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}]}e^{n(-i)f_{-}(z)}dz\right|=\Im(f_{-})(z_{0})=D\left(e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\right)=\dfrac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M),

as the volume of the figure-eight knot complement is well-known to be twice the volume of a regular ideal tetrahedron, with angles
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\pi}{3}
.

Finally, consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{n,-}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\int_{i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]}e^{n(-i)f_{-}(z)}dz

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\int_{i[s_{N}^{-},\eta]}e^{n(-i)f_{-}(z)}dz+\int_{i[\eta,2\pi-\eta^{\prime}]}e^{n(-i)f_{-}(z)}dz+\int_{i[2\pi-\eta^{\prime},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]}e^{n(-i)f_{-}(z)}dz.

By the results of Section
4.3
, an immediate triangle inequality shows that
the first and third integrals are
\displaystyle O_{n\to\infty}(\exp(nK))
where
\displaystyle K>0
is a value of
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)
outside of its strict maximum, and thus
\displaystyle K<{\rm Vol}(M)/2
. Hence

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,-}\right|=\dfrac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M).

4.4.2.
Case (a), integral
\displaystyle I_{n,+}

Let us prove that
\displaystyle I_{n,+}=O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)
.

One can easily compute that the function
\displaystyle f_{+}(z)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}+i\pi z
admits a unique critical point at
\displaystyle\textstyle z=-i\frac{\pi}{3}
(on which
\displaystyle\Im f_{+}|_{i\mathbb{R}}
has a minimum, not a maximum), but we will not use this.

We deform the contour
\displaystyle i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
. Let
\displaystyle x
be a large negative real number, and define
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
as the path from
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
to
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}
formed by the concatenation of the open oriented intervals
\displaystyle C_{s_{N}^{-}}:=i(s_{N}^{-},0)
,
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{0}:=(0,x)\subset\mathbb{R}_{-}
,
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{x}:=x+i(0,2\pi)
,
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{2\pi}:=(x,0)+i2\pi
, and
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{s_{N}^{+}}:=(2i\pi,2i\pi-is_{N}^{+})
(see Figure
3
). Since
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
and
\displaystyle i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
are homotopic relatively to their endpoints we have

(113)

I_{n,+}=\int_{C^{\infty}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi i}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2\pi i,\pi i)}dz.

\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle x
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle x+2i\pi
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 2i\pi
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{0}
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{2\pi}
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{x}
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{s_{N}^{+}}
\displaystyle C_{s_{N}^{-}}

Figure 3.
The deformed contour
\displaystyle C^{\infty}=C_{s_{N}^{-}}\cup C^{\infty}_{0}\cup C^{\infty}_{x}\cup C^{\infty}_{2\pi}\cup C^{\prime}_{s_{N}^{+}}
(blue parts are where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
and the red part is where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is positive but very small)

From the formula
\displaystyle f_{+}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2\pi i,\pi i)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}+\pi iz
we compute

\Im(f_{+}(z))=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z}))+2\Re(z)\Im(z)+\pi\Re(z).

For
\displaystyle z\in C_{0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{-}
we thus have
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+}(z))=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z}))+\pi\Re(z)=\pi\Re(z)<0
since
\displaystyle\Re(z)<0
and the dilogarithm of a real number
\displaystyle<1
is real.

For
\displaystyle z\in C_{2\pi}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{-}+i2\pi
we have
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+}(z))=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z}))+5\pi\Re(z)=5\pi\Re(z)<0
since again
\displaystyle\Re(z)<0
and the dilogarithm of
\displaystyle e^{z}\in\exp(]-\infty;0[+2i\pi)\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus]1;+\infty[
is real.

For
\displaystyle z=x+it\in C^{\infty}_{x}
we have
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+}(x+it))=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{x+it}))+2xt+\pi x.
Moreover
\displaystyle{\rm Li}_{2}(y)\sim y
as
\displaystyle y\rightarrow 0
in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
, and
therefore there exists
\displaystyle x_{0}<0
such that
for every
\displaystyle t\in[0,2\pi]
and
\displaystyle x\leq x_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle|{\rm Li}_{2}(e^{x+it})|<\frac{1}{2}
.
Hence, taking for instance
\displaystyle x\leqslant x_{0}-1
, for every
\displaystyle t\in[0,2\pi]
we have

\Im(f_{+}(x+it))=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{x+it}))+2xt+\pi x\leqslant\dfrac{1}{2}+\pi(-1)<0.

We conclude that for
\displaystyle|x|
large enough,
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
on the contour
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{x}
.

Now we remark that, having fixed for example
\displaystyle x=x_{0}-1<0
, and denoting by
\displaystyle\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
a parametrization of the path
\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{0}\cup C^{\infty}_{x}\cup C^{\infty}_{2\pi}
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left|\int_{C^{\infty}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi i}f_{+}(z)}dz\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}|e^{\frac{N}{2\pi i}f_{+}(\gamma(t))}\gamma^{\prime}(t)|dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im f_{+}(\gamma(t))}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}0}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|dt=2|x|+2\pi=O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1).

Finally, the remaining two integrals on the small vertical contours
\displaystyle C_{s_{N}^{-}},C^{\prime}_{s_{N}^{+}}
are also
\displaystyle O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)
from the conclusions of Section
4.3
. Hence
\displaystyle I_{n,+}=O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)
.

4.5.
The limit in case (b)

For case (b) we have
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=2i\pi
. Hence

(114)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{-}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,2i\pi):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}-2i\pi z,&\\
f_{+}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,2i\pi):={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}.&\end{array}\right.

We will again use Proposition
4.2
to prove that
\displaystyle I_{n,-}
and
\displaystyle I_{n,+}
are
of the form

(115)

I_{n,-}=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{-}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{+}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)),\ \ I_{n,+}=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{+}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{-}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)),

where
\displaystyle\text{Constant}_{-}
and
\displaystyle\text{Constant}_{+}
are distinct non-zero complex numbers and
\displaystyle r_{+},r_{-}\in\mathbb{R}
are distinct as well (see (
117
),(
118
)).
In particular, this will imply that, e.g.,

(116)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,-}\right|=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,+}\right|=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\dfrac{1}{n}{\rm Log}\left|I_{n,-}\pm I_{n,+}\right|=0.

4.5.1.
Case (b), integral
\displaystyle I_{n,+}

Let us study the asymptotics of
\displaystyle I_{n,+}=\int_{i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz
.

From the formula
\displaystyle f_{+}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2\pi i,2\pi i)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}

we compute

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Im(f_{+}(z))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z}))+2\Re(z)\Im(z),

\displaystyle\displaystyle(f_{+})^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2z-{\rm Log}(1-e^{z}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle(f_{+})^{\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2+\dfrac{e^{z}}{1-e^{z}}=1+\dfrac{1}{1-e^{z}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle f_{+}(x+it)

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{x+it})+(x+it)^{2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\Im(f_{+}(x+it))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Im\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}f_{+}(x+it)\right)=2t-\Im\left({\rm Log}(1-e^{x+it})\right)=2t-\arg\left(1-e^{x+it}\right),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{+}(x+it))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Im\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}f_{+}(x+it)\right)=2x-\Re\left({\rm Log}(1-e^{x+it})\right)=2x-{\rm Log}|1-e^{x+it}|.

One can easily compute that
\displaystyle(f_{+})^{\prime}(z_{0})=0
if and only if

z_{0}=a:={\rm Log}((-1+\sqrt{5})/{2})\approx-0.48.

We will deform the contour
\displaystyle i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
(in blue in Figure
4
) to a new contour
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
(in dotted blue in Figure
4
) on which
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is negative everywhere but in the point
\displaystyle a
where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})(a)=0
and on the small subcontour
\displaystyle[0,is_{N}]
where it is nonnegative. Let us prove this is possible.

We construct
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
in six parts:

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{1}
is vertical, going from
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
to
\displaystyle 0
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
is vertical, going from
\displaystyle 0
to
\displaystyle-i\eta
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}
is almost horizontal, going from
\displaystyle-i\eta
to
\displaystyle a
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{4}
is vertical, going from
\displaystyle a
to
\displaystyle a+2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}
is horizontal, going from
\displaystyle a+2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
to
\displaystyle 2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{6}
is vertical, going from
\displaystyle 2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
to
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}
.

\displaystyle\Im f_{+}>0
\displaystyle\Im f_{+}<0
\displaystyle\Im f_{+}>0
\displaystyle\Im f_{+}<0
Cut
Cut
\displaystyle a
\displaystyle a+2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
\displaystyle i\pi
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}
\displaystyle 2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
\displaystyle 2i\pi
\displaystyle-i\eta

Figure 4.
The deformed contour (in dashed blue) through zones where
\displaystyle\Im f_{+}<0
and the point
\displaystyle a
(in red) where
\displaystyle\Im f_{+}=0
.

First,
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is zero on the half-line
\displaystyle(-\infty,0]
since
\displaystyle{\rm Li}_{2}
takes real values on
\displaystyle[0,1]
. Moreover,
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})(z)
vanishes at
\displaystyle z=2i\pi
(and also at
\displaystyle z=i\pi
but we will not use this). The set of points where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
vanishes is drawn in red in Figure
4
; the red dotted line’s general form was computed via
Mathematica
, but we will not use its exact position in our arguments.

Defining
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{1}
, and asymptotics:
We define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{1}:=[0,is_{N}^{-}]
oriented downwards. Recall that for all
\displaystyle t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle z:=it
we have
\displaystyle\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z}))={\rm Cl}_{2}(t)
, thus
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+}(it))={\rm Cl}_{2}(t)
. From Section
4.3.2
we have:

\int_{C^{\prime}_{1}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz=\int_{0}^{s_{N}^{-}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im(f_{+}(it))}dt=\int_{0}^{s_{N}^{-}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt=O_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\right)=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

Defining
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
, and asymptotics:
We define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}:=[-i\eta,0]
oriented downwards. From Section
4.3.1
, since
\displaystyle[-\eta,0]\subset[-\pi,0]
we have:

\left|\int_{C^{\prime}_{2}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz\right|\leqslant\int_{-\eta}^{0}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im(f_{+}(it))}dt=\int_{-\eta}^{0}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

Defining
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}
:

The critical point
\displaystyle z=a
of
\displaystyle f_{+}
is simple, since
\displaystyle(f_{+})^{\prime\prime}(z)
only vanishes at
\displaystyle z={\rm Log}(2)
. Thus, by standard complex analysis, there is a disk neighbourhood
\displaystyle D_{a}
of
\displaystyle a
(drawn as a grey disk) split into four zones by two transverse level lines where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})=0
, and
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is alternatively positive and negative in these four zones by circling around
\displaystyle a
. We already know that one level line is the horizontal line (drawn in full red), thus the other level line (part of the dotted red curve) crosses transversely this horizontal line.

Now, for any point
\displaystyle x\in(a,0]
we have

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{+}(x+it))|_{t=0}=2x-{\rm Log}|1-e^{x}|=2x-{\rm Log}(1-e^{x})>0,

by definition of
\displaystyle a
and the fact that

\displaystyle 2x-{\rm Log}|1-e^{x}|>0\Leftrightarrow(e^{x})^{2}+(e^{x})-1>0
.
Hence,
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is increasing on every small enough vertical line (oriented upwards) above every
\displaystyle x\in(a,0]
. This confirms that a negative zone for
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is below the red horizontal line and a positive zone is above it.

We can now define the part
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}
of the new contour
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
(in Figure
4
this is the part which is between
\displaystyle-i\eta
and
\displaystyle a
, oriented from right to left, where the value of
\displaystyle\eta>0
will be decided in what follows). For any
\displaystyle x\in(a,0]
, define a small enough disk
\displaystyle D_{x}
around
\displaystyle x
so that the real line cuts
\displaystyle D_{x}
into two half-disks, where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is negative on the bottom half-disk and positive on the upper half-disk. Such a small disk exists since
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{+}(x+it))|_{t=0}>0
.

The union
\displaystyle\textstyle D_{a}\cup\bigcup_{a<x\leqslant 0}D_{x}
covers the compact set
\displaystyle[a,0]
, so there is a finite family of points
\displaystyle x_{0}=a<x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{p}=0
such that
\displaystyle\textstyle D_{a}\cup\bigcup_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}D_{x_{i}}
covers
\displaystyle[a,0]
. Take
\displaystyle\eta>0
small enough to be smaller than the radius of
\displaystyle D_{x_{p}}=D_{0}
.
We can now define the part
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}
as a smooth curve going from
\displaystyle-i\eta
to
\displaystyle a
while remaining in all bottom half-disks; it can even be chosen outside of
\displaystyle D_{a}
as an horizontal line of imaginary part
\displaystyle-\eta
(thus higher than the opposite of half the minimum of all radii of
\displaystyle D_{x_{i}}
), and smoothed out afterwards.

Defining
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{4}
:

We define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{4}:=[a,a+2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}]
, oriented upwards. Let us prove that
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
on the vertical line
\displaystyle(a,a+2i\pi]
.
To do this we look for all
\displaystyle t\in(0,2\pi)
at the real derivative

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{+}(a+it))=2a-{\rm Log}|1-e^{a+it}|={\rm Log}(1-e^{a})-{\rm Log}|1-e^{a+it}|.

We remark that this quantity is negative for all
\displaystyle t\in(0,2\pi)
, by a simple geometric argument:
\displaystyle 1-e^{a}
is the distance between
\displaystyle 1
and
\displaystyle e^{a}\in(0,1)
, which is smaller than the distance
\displaystyle|1-e^{a+it}|
between
\displaystyle 1
and any other point
\displaystyle e^{a+it}
of the circle of center
\displaystyle 0
and radius
\displaystyle e^{a}
(since
\displaystyle 1
lives outside of the closed disk
\displaystyle\bar{D}(0,e^{a})
). Hence,
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
decreases on
\displaystyle[a,a+2i\pi]
oriented upwards, and thus
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})<0
on the vertical line
\displaystyle(a,a+2i\pi]
.

Asymptotics on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}
:

From what precedes, we have all the tools to apply the saddle point method (see Section
4.2
) on the contour
\displaystyle\lambda=C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}
for
\displaystyle g(z)=1,f(z)=f_{+}(z)
and
\displaystyle z_{0}=a
. Since
\displaystyle N=2\pi n
, we obtain:

\int_{C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz=\dfrac{{2\pi}}{\sqrt{N}}e^{i\theta}\cdot 1\cdot\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{|f_{+}^{\prime\prime}(a)|}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(a)}\left(1+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\right).

For the curious reader, we remark that (
[
63
]
)

{\rm Li}_{2}(e^{a})={\rm Li}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{5}-1}{2}\right)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{10}-{\rm Log}^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{5}+1}{2}\right),

and thus
\displaystyle f_{+}(a)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{a})+a^{2}
has the unexpectedly simple expression

\displaystyle\textstyle f_{+}(a)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{10}
. For our purposes, we only need to know that this is a real number, i.e.
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+}(a))=0
which we knew already.

Moreover,
\displaystyle(f_{+})^{\prime\prime}(a)=1+\frac{1}{1-e^{a}}=\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{2}
. Hence we have

\int_{C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{+}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{+}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)),

where
\displaystyle\text{Constant}_{+}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
has modulus
\displaystyle 2\pi\left(\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=3.303...
and
\displaystyle r_{+}=-\frac{\pi}{20}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Defining
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}
, and asymptotics:
We define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}:=[a+2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime},2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}]
oriented from left to right.
Since
\displaystyle\partial_{x}\Im(f_{+}(x+it))=2t-\arg\left(1-e^{x+it}\right)
for all
\displaystyle x+it
in the domain of definition of
\displaystyle f_{+}
, and since
\displaystyle\arg
lies in
\displaystyle(-\pi,\pi]
, we deduce that for any fixed
\displaystyle t>\pi/2
the function
\displaystyle\left(x\mapsto\Im(f_{+}(x+it))\right)
is strictly increasing on the horizontal line of altitude
\displaystyle t
oriented from left to right.

In particular for
\displaystyle t=2\pi-\eta^{\prime}
,
\displaystyle\Im(f_{+})
is maximal on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}
at its right endpoint
\displaystyle 2i\pi-i\eta^{\prime}
where it takes the value
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}_{2}(2\pi-\eta^{\prime})<0
.
Hence

\left|\int_{C^{\prime}_{5}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz\right|\leqslant|a|e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(2\pi-\eta^{\prime})}=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

Defining
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{6}
, and asymptotics:
We define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{6}:=i[2\pi-\eta^{\prime},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
oriented upwards. From Section
4.3
, since
\displaystyle[2\pi-\eta^{\prime},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]\subset[\pi,2\pi]
we have:

\left|\int_{C^{\prime}_{6}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz\right|\leqslant\int_{2\pi-\eta^{\prime}}^{2\pi-s_{N}^{+}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im(f_{+}(it))}dt=\int_{2\pi-\eta^{\prime}}^{2\pi-s_{N}^{+}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

This concludes the construction of the new contour
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
, and by summing the integrals on each part, we obtain

I_{n,+}=\int_{C^{\prime}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{+}(z)}dz=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{+}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{+}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)),

where

(117)

|\mathrm{Constant}_{+}|=2\pi\left(\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=3.303...,\ r_{+}=-\frac{\pi}{20}\in\mathbb{R}.

4.5.2.
Case (b), integral
\displaystyle I_{n,-}

From the formula
\displaystyle f_{-}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2\pi i,2i\pi)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}-2i\pi z
we compute

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Im(f_{-}(z))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Im({\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z}))+2\Re(z)\Im(z)-2\pi\Re(z),

\displaystyle\displaystyle(f_{-})^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2z-{\rm Log}(1-e^{z})-2i\pi,

\displaystyle\displaystyle(f_{-})^{\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2+\dfrac{e^{z}}{1-e^{z}}=1+\dfrac{1}{1-e^{z}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle f_{-}(x+it)

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{x+it})+(x+it)^{2}-2i\pi(x+it),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\Im(f_{-}(x+it))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Im\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}f_{-}(x+it)\right)=2(t-\pi)-\Im\left({\rm Log}(1-e^{x+it})\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2(t-\pi)-\arg\left(1-e^{x+it}\right),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{-}(x+it))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Im\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}f_{-}(x+it)\right)=2x-\Re\left({\rm Log}(1-e^{x+it})\right)=2x-{\rm Log}|1-e^{x+it}|.

One can easily compute that
\displaystyle(f_{-})^{\prime}(z_{0})=0
if and only if

z_{0}=b+i\pi:={\rm Log}((1+\sqrt{5})/2)+i\pi\approx 0.48+i\pi.

As in the previous section, we will deform the contour
\displaystyle i[s_{N}^{-},2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
(in blue in Figure
5
) to a new contour
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
(in dotted blue in Figure
5
). In Figure
5
, red dots and lines are points where one can easily prove that
\displaystyle\Im(f_{-})=0
, and the red dotted curve is suggested by
Mathematica
.

We construct
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
in five parts:

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{1}
is horizontal, going from
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
to
\displaystyle 1+is_{N}^{-}
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
is vertical, going from
\displaystyle 1+is_{N}^{-}
to
\displaystyle 1+i(\pi-\epsilon)
, where
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
is small enough.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}
is almost horizontal, going from
\displaystyle 1+i(\pi-\epsilon)
to the saddle point
\displaystyle b+i\pi
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{4}

\displaystyle b+i\pi

\displaystyle i(\pi+\epsilon)
.

•

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}
is vertical, going from
\displaystyle i(\pi+\epsilon)
to
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}
.

\displaystyle\Im f_{-}<0
\displaystyle\Im f_{-}<0
\displaystyle\Im f_{-}>0
\displaystyle\Im f_{-}>0

\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
\displaystyle i\pi
\displaystyle 2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}

\displaystyle 1+is_{N}^{-}

\displaystyle i(\pi+\epsilon)

Figure 5.
The deformed contour
\displaystyle C^{\prime}

Asymptotics on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{1}
:
On the horizontal line of altitude
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}
, for all
\displaystyle x\geqslant 0
, we have

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\Im(f_{-}(x+is_{N}^{-}))=2s_{N}^{-}-2\pi-\arg\left(1-e^{x+is_{N}^{-}}\right)<-\frac{\pi}{2},

where the last inequality comes from
\displaystyle|\arg|\leqslant\pi
and the fact that
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}
is small. Thus for all
\displaystyle x>0
we have

\Im(f_{-}(x+is_{N}^{-}))<\Im(f_{-}(0+is_{N}^{-}))-\frac{\pi}{2}x={\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})-\frac{\pi}{2}x.

Since
\displaystyle{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})<{\rm Cl}_{2}(\pi/3)\approx 1.10149
, it follows that for all
\displaystyle x\geqslant 1
we have
\displaystyle\Im(f_{-}(x+is_{N}^{-}))\leqslant\Im(f_{-}(1+is_{N}^{-}))<0
. Moreover, on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{1}=[is_{N}^{-},1+is_{N}^{-}]
we have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left|\int_{C^{\prime}_{1}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{-}(z)}dz\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im(f_{-}(x+is_{N}^{-}))}dx

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\left({\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})-\frac{\pi}{2}x\right)}dx

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}x\right)}dx

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}\cdot\dfrac{4}{N}\left(1-e^{-\frac{N}{4}}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right),

where the last asymptotics comes from
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(s_{N}^{-})}=o_{N\to\infty}(1/\sqrt{N})
established in Section
4.3.2
.

Asymptotics on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}
:
We proceed similarly as we did in the previous section for
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}
, constructing this time two almost horizontal curves
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3},C^{\prime}_{4}
, going respectively from
\displaystyle 1+i\pi-i\epsilon
to
\displaystyle b+i\pi
and from
\displaystyle b+i\pi
to
\displaystyle i\pi+i\epsilon
. For this we construct a small disk
\displaystyle D_{x}
around each point
\displaystyle x+i\pi
of
\displaystyle[i\pi,1+i\pi]
, where
\displaystyle\Im(f_{-})
is negative on the bottom half-disk and
positive on the upper half-disk if
\displaystyle x>b
, and conversely if
\displaystyle x<b
. This is possible, because for any
\displaystyle x\geqslant 0
the vertical derivative at
\displaystyle x+i\pi
is

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{-}(x+it))|_{t=\pi}=2x-{\rm Log}|1-e^{x+i\pi}|=2x-{\rm Log}(1+e^{x}).

The reader will recognize the golden ratio, and agree that
\displaystyle 2x-{\rm Log}(1+e^{x})<0
for
\displaystyle x\in[0,b)
and
\displaystyle 2x-{\rm Log}(1+e^{x})>0
for
\displaystyle x>b
. This justifies the location of the zones of signs of
\displaystyle\Im(f_{-})
in Figure
5
(the
valleys
and the
hills
of
\displaystyle f_{-}
at
\displaystyle b
), whence the construction of the disks
\displaystyle D_{x}
. By compacity we can reduce to a finite family of such disks which cover
\displaystyle[i\pi,1+i\pi]
; they are drawn in grey in Figure
5
. Then we define
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
small enough to be smaller than the radii of all grey disks.

Finally, in order to apply the saddle point method of Proposition
4.2
for the contour
\displaystyle\lambda=C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}
, with
\displaystyle g=1
,
\displaystyle f=f_{-}
, and
\displaystyle z_{0}=b+i\pi
, we only need to check that
\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})\neq 0
. We have

(f_{-})^{\prime\prime}(b+i\pi)=1+\frac{1}{1-e^{b+i\pi}}=1+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}}=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{2}=1.38...\neq 0

so we are good to go. For the sake of completeness, we can compute
\displaystyle f_{-}(b+i\pi)=9\pi^{2}/10
, once again from the special values of the dilogarithm. Hence we get

\int_{C^{\prime}_{3}\cup C^{\prime}_{4}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{-}(z)}dz=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{-}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{-}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)),

where
\displaystyle\text{Constant}_{-}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
has modulus
\displaystyle 2\pi\left(\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=5.34...
and
\displaystyle r_{-}=-\frac{9\pi}{20}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Asymptotics on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
:
Now that
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
has been fixed, we define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
as the vertical contour from
\displaystyle 1+is_{N}^{-}
to
\displaystyle 1+i\pi-i\epsilon
.
To see that
\displaystyle\Im(f_{-})
is strictly increasing on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
going upwards, one can for example prove that the vertical derivative is positive:

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Im(f_{-}(x+it))|_{x=1}=2-{\rm Log}|1-e^{1+it}|\geqslant 2-{\rm Log}(1+e)>0,

where the last inequality stems from the fact that
\displaystyle 1>b
. Hence the negative number
\displaystyle\Im\left(f_{-}\left(1+i\pi-i\epsilon\right)\right)
is an upper bound for
\displaystyle\Im(f_{-})
on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{2}
, and

\left|\int_{C^{\prime}_{2}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{-}(z)}dz\right|\leqslant\pi\cdot e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im\left(f_{-}\left(c+i\pi-i\epsilon\right)\right)}=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

Asymptotics on
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}
:
We define
\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{5}:=i[\pi+\epsilon,2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]
, oriented upwards. From Section
4.3
, since
\displaystyle i[\pi+\epsilon,2\pi-s_{N}^{+}]\subset[\pi,2\pi]
, we have:

\left|\int_{C^{\prime}_{5}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{-}(z)}dz\right|\leqslant\int_{\pi+\epsilon}^{2\pi-s_{N}^{+}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}\Im(f_{-}(it))}dt=\int_{\pi+\epsilon}^{2\pi-s_{N}^{+}}e^{\frac{N}{2\pi}{\rm Cl}_{2}(t)}dt=o_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

This concludes the construction of the new contour
\displaystyle C^{\prime}
, and by summing the integrals on each part, we obtain

I_{n,-}=\int_{C^{\prime}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}f_{-}(z)}dz=\dfrac{\text{Constant}_{-}}{\sqrt{N}}(e^{ir_{-}N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)),

where

(118)

|\mathrm{Constant}_{-}|=2\pi\left(\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=5.34...,\ r_{-}=-\frac{9\pi}{20}\in\mathbb{R}.

5.
Asymptotics of the quantum integral

Recall that in Section
4
we defined the functions

•

(case (a), function
\displaystyle f_{-}
)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,i\pi)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}-i\pi z
,

•

(case (a), function
\displaystyle f_{+}
)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,i\pi)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}+i\pi z
,

•

(case (b), function
\displaystyle f_{-}
)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,2i\pi)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}-2i\pi z
,

•

(case (b), function
\displaystyle f_{+}
)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,2i\pi)={\rm Li}_{2}(e^{z})+z^{2}
,

and we studied the asymptotics of the integrals
\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{\pm}(z,-2i\pi,\ell)}dz
for
\displaystyle\ell=i\pi
(case (a)) or
\displaystyle\ell=2i\pi
(case (b)).

In this section we extend this study to obtain asymptotics of the integral on the right side of (
88
),
\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz
, thus finishing the proof of Theorem
1.1
(see the steps IX and X in Section
1.3
).

Because the integrand depends on
\displaystyle N
in a complicated way, we need a preliminary result, Lemma
5.1
below, to approximate the integral.

To state this lemma, denote by
\displaystyle C^{+}_{N}
and
\displaystyle C^{-}_{N}
the intersections of the contour
\displaystyle C_{N}
(defined in (
87
)) with the right and left half-spaces; see Figure
6
. Recall that we assume
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{-}:=\alpha\frac{\pi}{N}
with
\displaystyle\alpha\in]0,1[
(see (
107
)), and
\displaystyle s_{N}^{+}\in]0,\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}[
. Consider the following shift of the function
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}
that we studied in the lemmas
3.3
-
3.5
:

(119)

{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}:=\Psi_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right).

It is holomorphic on the domain (
63
) shifted by
\displaystyle\textstyle-\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})
. Finally, recall the log-parameters
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{k}^{\prime}:={\rm Log}(u_{k})+i\pi a_{k}
from (
7
), and that the edge colors
\displaystyle a_{0},a_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
were specified in Sections
2.3
and
3.6
. Define

(120)

\rho(z):=e^{-\frac{{\bf l}_{1}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}z}(1-e^{z})^{-\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}-\frac{1}{2}}.

Note that our choice of
\displaystyle a_{0}\geq 2
makes
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}+\frac{1}{2})>0
, so
\displaystyle\rho(z)
has poles at the points
\displaystyle z\in(2i\pi)\mathbb{Z}
. This will have important consequences in the analysis of the integrals in Lemma
5.1
, which will be done in Lemma
5.5
.

\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle\varepsilon
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle 2\pi i
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle\textstyle 2\pi i-\frac{2\pi}{N}i
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}i
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle C^{+}_{N}
\displaystyle\cdot
\displaystyle C^{-}_{N}
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
\displaystyle 2\pi i-is_{N}^{+}

Figure 6.
The contour
\displaystyle C_{N}
and its subcontours
\displaystyle C^{+}_{N}
and
\displaystyle C^{-}_{N}

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{0,\infty}=-2i\pi
and
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{1,\infty}=:\ell\in\{\pi i,2\pi i\}
(as in Lemma
3.12
) we have

\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz\\
=\int_{C^{+}_{N}}\left(e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}-u_{0}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}\right)\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)dz\\
-\int_{C^{-}_{N}}\left(e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}-u_{0}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}\right)\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)dz\\
=2\int_{C^{+}_{N}}\left(e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}-u_{0}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}\right)\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)dz,

where the function
\displaystyle R_{N}
, with formula (
130
), is holomorphic on the domain

(121)

\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2i\pi k-\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+[0,+\infty[\right)\bigcup\left(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2i\pi k+[0,+\infty[\right)\right)\right),

and
\displaystyle|R_{N}|
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
on any compact subset of a strip
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{R}+i(2\pi k+[\delta+\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}])
,
\displaystyle\textstyle\delta\in]0,\pi[
,
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
, contained in this domain.

Proof.

Step 1: Rephrasing
\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)
.

Recall from (
84
)-(
86
)-(
89
) that
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{0,N}=-2i\pi+\frac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(u_{0})+\pi ia_{0})
where
\displaystyle a_{0}
is even and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}({\rm Log}(u_{0})+\pi ia_{0})>0
. We can use the functional equation
\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}(z)=\hat{S}_{N}(z+\frac{2i\pi}{N})(1-e^{z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}})
(see (
52
)) in order to shift
\displaystyle N-\textstyle\frac{a_{0}}{2}
times the argument
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0,N}+z
: since
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{0,N}+z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}(N-\frac{a_{0}}{2})=\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z
we have

(122)

\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)=\hat{S}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)\prod_{k=1}^{N-a_{0}/2}\left(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}+z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}k}\right).

Step 2: Approximating
\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)
.

Let us approximate
\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{S}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)
. Since
\displaystyle-\pi<\mathfrak{I}\left({\rm Log}(u_{0})\right)<\pi
, for every
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
we have

(123)

-\frac{\pi}{N}+s_{N}^{-}\leq\mathfrak{I}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)\leq 2\pi-s_{N}^{+}+\frac{\pi}{N}.

We can get finer bounds, depending on
\displaystyle u_{0}
. Indeed, assume that
\displaystyle\Im({\rm Log}(u_{0}))
satisfies

(124)

-\pi\upsilon<\Im({\rm Log}(u_{0}))<\pi\upsilon^{\prime},

where
\displaystyle 0<\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}<1
. Let
\displaystyle\upsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
be such that
\displaystyle\upsilon^{\prime}<\upsilon^{\prime\prime}<1
. Take

(125)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}s_{N}^{-}:=\alpha^{-}\frac{\pi}{N},\mathrm{with\ }\alpha^{-}:=\frac{1+\upsilon}{2},\\
\\
s_{N}^{+}:=\alpha^{+}\frac{\pi}{N},\mathrm{with\ }\alpha^{+}:=1+\upsilon^{\prime\prime}.\end{array}\right.

Then the inequalities (
123
) become:

(126)

0<\frac{(1-\upsilon)\pi}{2N}=\frac{-\pi\upsilon}{N}+s_{N}^{-}\leq\mathfrak{I}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)\leq 2\pi-s_{N}^{+}+\frac{\upsilon^{\prime}\pi}{N}<2\pi-\frac{(\upsilon^{\prime\prime}-\upsilon^{\prime})\pi}{N}-\frac{\pi}{N}.

Note that the choices of
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{-}=\alpha^{-}\frac{\pi}{N}
, with
\displaystyle\alpha^{-}\in]0,1[
, and of
\displaystyle\textstyle s_{N}^{+}=\alpha^{+}\frac{\pi}{N}
, with
\displaystyle\alpha^{+}\in]1,2[
, satisfy the assumptions of Section
4
(see (
107
)); moreover
\displaystyle\textstyle\alpha^{-}=\frac{1+\upsilon}{2}
ensures that strips like (
126
) avoid cuts. The choice of
\displaystyle\alpha^{+}
, which must lie within
\displaystyle]0,2[
by the definition of
\displaystyle s_{N}^{+}
, in the subinterval
\displaystyle]1,2[
is relevant only for Remark
5.2
below.

By Remark
3.4
(ii) we can use the equality (
56
). Taking the exponential we get

\hat{S}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)=\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z}\right)-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Log}(1-e^{\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z})+\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)

where
\displaystyle\textstyle{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}=\Psi_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)
(see (
119
)). Using that

(127)

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{z}\right)-\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}\left(1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}t+z}\right)dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\rm Log}\left(1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}t+z}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\rm Log}(1-e^{z})-\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}e^{z}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s}}{1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s+z}}ds

we can write

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{z}\right)\right)\exp\left({\mathcal{T}_{u_{0},N}(z)}+\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{z}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{i\pi}\right){\rm Log}(1-e^{z})\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\left({\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}+{\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\right),

where

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}_{u_{0},N}(z)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{z}\right)+\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z}\right)-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Log}(1-e^{\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Log}\left(1-e^{\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z}\right)-\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{2i\pi}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Log}\left(1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}t+z}\right)dt

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{i\pi}\right){\rm Log}(1-e^{z})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}e^{z}\left(\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{2i\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s}}{1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s+z}}ds\right)dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s}}{1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s+z}}ds\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{i\pi}\right){\rm Log}(1-e^{z})+\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)

and

\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z):=\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}e^{z}\left(\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{2i\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s}}{1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s+z}}ds\right)dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s}}{1-e^{\frac{{\rm Log}(u_{0})}{N}s+z}}ds\right)

is the last summand of
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{u_{0},N}(z)
made of the integrals.

By its definition the function
\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}_{u_{0},N}(z)}
is holomorphic on the domain (
121
). Then so is
\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)}
. Applying several times the triangle inequality on
\displaystyle\textstyle|{\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)}|
, and using Lemma
3.2
as at the end of Lemma
3.3
, we find

(128)

|{\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)}|\leq\dfrac{B_{M}}{\delta\sqrt{1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}}}

for every
\displaystyle\textstyle z\in]-\infty,M]+i(2\pi k+[\delta+\frac{\pi}{N},2\pi-\delta-\frac{\pi}{N}])
,
\displaystyle\textstyle\delta\in]0,\pi[
,
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
(so that
\displaystyle\textstyle\delta\leq\mathfrak{I}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})s+z\right)\leq 2\pi-\delta
for every
\displaystyle s\in[0,1]
), where
\displaystyle B_{M}>0
is a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
,
\displaystyle\delta
and
\displaystyle k
.

Step 3: Approximating
\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{k=1}^{N-a_{0}/2}\left(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}+z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}k}\right)
.

Next consider the product in (
122
). By factoring out

\displaystyle a_{0}/2

terms, and using that
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{0,N}=-2i\pi+O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\frac{1}{N})
, for every
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{N-a_{0}/2}(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}+z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}k})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}+z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}k})\prod_{k=N+1-a_{0}/2}^{N}(1-e^{{\bf l}_{0,N}+z+\frac{2i\pi}{N}k})^{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(1-e^{N(z+{\bf l}_{0,N})})\prod_{k=N+1-a_{0}/2}^{N}\left(1-e^{z+\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(1-u_{0}e^{Nz})\left(1-e^{z+\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)}\right)^{-a_{0}/2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{1-u_{0}e^{Nz}}{(1-e^{z})^{a_{0}/2}}\ .\ \frac{1}{(1-\frac{e^{z}}{e^{z}-1}\ .\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1))^{a_{0}/2}}

(129)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{1-u_{0}e^{Nz}}{(1-e^{z})^{a_{0}/2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right)\right),

where the last
\displaystyle\textstyle O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)
term is a sequence of holomorphic functions on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\setminus\{2i\pi k,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where each singularity
\displaystyle 2i\pi k
is removable, and with moduli bounded from above by some constant independent of
\displaystyle N
on any fixed compact subset of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
.

Step 4: Approximating
\displaystyle\textstyle\coth\left(\frac{Nz}{2}\right)
and
\displaystyle e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}
.

Finally, for every
\displaystyle z
in
\displaystyle\textstyle C_{N}\setminus\{is_{N},2i\pi-is_{N}-\frac{2i\pi}{N}\}
we have

\coth\left(\frac{Nz}{2}\right)=\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)\left(1+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\right),

where the
\displaystyle\textstyle o_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)
term is a sequence of holomorphic functions on
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\setminus\{2i\pi k,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where each singularity
\displaystyle 2i\pi k
is a simple pole, and with moduli bounded from above by some constant independent of
\displaystyle N
on any compact subset of this domain.

Moreover, since
\displaystyle\textstyle{\bf l}_{1,N}=\frac{1}{N}{\bf l}^{\prime}_{1}+\pi ia_{1}
, with
\displaystyle{\bf l}^{\prime}_{1}={\rm Log}(u_{1})+\pi ia_{1}
, and
\displaystyle\pi ia_{1}=\ell
mod
\displaystyle(2i\pi)
, with
\displaystyle\ell\in\{\pi i,2\pi i\}
, by the comment after (
85
) for every
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
we can replace
\displaystyle e^{-\frac{N}{2i\pi}{\bf l}_{1,N}z}
with

e^{-\frac{N}{2i\pi}(\frac{1}{N}({\rm Log}(u_{1})+\pi ia_{1})+\ell)z}=e^{-\frac{1}{2i\pi}({\rm Log}(u_{1})+\pi ia_{1})z}e^{-\frac{N}{2i\pi}\ell z}=e^{-\frac{{\bf l}^{\prime}_{1}}{2i\pi}z}e^{-\frac{N}{2i\pi}\ell z}.

Step 5: Putting all together towards the RHS.

Recalling the formula (
120
), the approximations we have obtained
in Steps 2 to 5
imply that for all
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
one has:

\displaystyle\displaystyle e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)\exp\left(\frac{N}{2i\pi}\left({\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{z}\right)+z^{2}-\ell z\right)\right){(1-u_{0}e^{Nz})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)\left(e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}-u_{0}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right),

where

(130)

1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right)\right){\dfrac{\coth\left(\frac{Nz}{2}\right)}{\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)}}

satisfies the expected properties (the term
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}(1)
comes from (
129
), and recall that
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\coth\left(\frac{Nz}{2}\right)}{\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)}=\left(1+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\right)

from Step 5). More precisely,
\displaystyle R_{N}(z)
is of the form

R_{N}(z)=\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)+O_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right)+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right)+O_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),

where the second, third and fourth terms in the right hand side are of course functions of
\displaystyle z
as before. Denote by
\displaystyle C^{+}_{N}
and
\displaystyle C^{-}_{N}
the intersections of
\displaystyle C_{N}
with the right and left half-spaces. Because of the term
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)
in the above expressions, we can write

\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz=\\
\int_{C^{+}_{N}}\left(e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}-u_{0}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}\right)\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)dz\\
-\int_{C^{-}_{N}}\left(e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{-}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}-u_{0}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{+}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}\right)\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)dz.

The integrand is holomorphic on the intersection
\displaystyle U
of the domains (
63
) shifted by
\displaystyle\textstyle-\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})
and the domain (
121
). Since
\displaystyle-\pi<\mathfrak{I}({\rm Log}(u_{0}))<\pi
one can choose
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}
,
\displaystyle s_{N}^{+}
in (
125
) so that
\displaystyle s_{N}^{\pm}>\textstyle\frac{1}{N}\mathfrak{I}({\rm Log}(u_{0}))
, and therefore the contours
\displaystyle C^{+}_{N}
and
\displaystyle C^{-}_{N}
are homotopic within
\displaystyle U
to
\displaystyle[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]
relatively to their endpoints, with upwards and downwards orientations respectively. So the opposite of the integral over
\displaystyle C_{N}^{-}
coincides with the integral over
\displaystyle C_{N}^{+}
, and the last expression reduces to
\displaystyle 2
times the integral over
\displaystyle C_{N}^{+}
on the RHS. This concludes the proof.
\displaystyle\Box

Remark 5.2
.

We can give sharp uniform bounds on
\displaystyle|{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}|
for points
\displaystyle z\in C_{N}
, as follows. Define (with
\displaystyle\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime\prime}
as in (
124
)–(
125
)):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\delta^{-}_{N}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\frac{(1-\upsilon)\pi}{2N}+\frac{\pi}{N},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\delta^{+}_{N}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\frac{(\upsilon^{\prime\prime}-\upsilon^{\prime})\pi}{N}.

Then the inequalities (
126
) become

(131)

\delta^{-}_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N}\leq\mathfrak{I}\left(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z\right)\leq 2\pi-\delta^{+}_{N}-\frac{\pi}{N},

and thus define strips as in Lemma
3.3
and Remark
3.4
(iii) (with the variable
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z
in place of
\displaystyle z
). In that lemma take now
\displaystyle M
equal to the constant
\displaystyle\varepsilon
used in the definition of the contours
\displaystyle C_{N}
. Then, by (
57
), for every
\displaystyle z
in the strip (
131
) satisfying
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(z)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
for some
\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}>\varepsilon
(so that
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z)\leq\varepsilon
for
\displaystyle N
large enough), we have

(132)

|{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}|\leq\frac{B^{\prime}}{\delta_{N}^{+}}+\dfrac{\pi e^{\varepsilon/2}}{2\delta_{N}^{+}}\sqrt{1-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}}+B^{\prime\prime},

for constants
\displaystyle B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}>0
independent of
\displaystyle N
and of
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{+}
(note that
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{+}=\min(\delta_{N}^{-},\delta_{N}^{+})
). Since
\displaystyle N\delta_{N}^{+}=\pi(\upsilon^{\prime\prime}-\upsilon^{\prime})>0
, (
132
) shows that
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{N}|{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}|
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
,
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{-}
, and
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{+}
on any half-strip given by (
131
) and
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(z)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

By Remark
3.4
(i), the bound (
132
) holds true also when
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{-}
in (
131
) is replaced with any
\displaystyle\delta\in]0,\pi[
, as long as
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})+z
is not contained in a cut of the domain (
63
) shifted by
\displaystyle\textstyle-\frac{1}{N}{\rm Log}(u_{0})
. In particular we can take
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{-}
of the form
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{-}=\alpha^{\prime}\textstyle\frac{\pi}{N}
, with
\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}>0
arbitrary. Since
\displaystyle N\delta_{N}^{-}=\alpha^{\prime}\pi>0
, as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
(ii) we find that
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{N}|{\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)}|
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle N
,
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{+}
, and such a
\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{-}
on any half-strip given by (
131
) and
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(z)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
5.1
we have

(133)

\int_{C_{N}}e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}(z^{2}-{\bf l}_{1,N}z)}\hat{S}_{N}({\bf l}_{0,N}+z)\textstyle\coth(\frac{Nz}{2})dz=2I_{N,-}(\ell)-2u_{0}I_{N,+}(\ell),

where

(134)

I_{N,\pm}(\ell):=\int_{C_{N}^{+}}g_{N}(z)e^{\frac{N}{2i\pi}\mathcal{L}_{\pm}(z;-2i\pi,\ell)}dz,

and we put

(135)

\displaystyle\displaystyle g_{N}(z)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(z)\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\rho(z)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\Psi_{u_{0},N}(z)\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{I}_{u_{0},N}(z)\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N}O_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right)\right)\dfrac{\coth\left(\frac{Nz}{2}\right)}{\mathrm{sign}\left(\mathfrak{R}(z)\right)}.

Lemma
5.5
below extends to the integrals
\displaystyle I_{N,\pm}(\ell)
in (
134
) the asymptotics obtained for the integrals in Section
4
. The proof requires the following generalizations of Proposition
4.2
; why we need these generalizations is explained before Lemma
5.5
. First, we will need a special case of Perron’s method for a contour starting at a maximum.

Proposition 5.3
.

(i)
(
[
51
, Theorem 1.2, case of
\displaystyle\mu=1
and
\displaystyle S=1
]
)
Assume that
\displaystyle\lambda
is a compact piecewise
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{1}
contour from
\displaystyle z_{0}
to
\displaystyle z_{1}
in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
, and
\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g
are holomorphic functions on a domain containing
\displaystyle\lambda
. Assume furthermore that
\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z_{0})\neq 0
, and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(f(z))<\mathfrak{I}(f(z_{0}))
for all
\displaystyle z\in\lambda
,
\displaystyle z\neq z_{0}
. Then we have

(136)

\int_{\lambda}g(z)e^{n(-i)f(z)}dz=e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}\left(\dfrac{g(z_{0})}{if^{\prime}(z_{0})}\dfrac{1}{n}+O_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{K_{g}}{n^{2}}\right)\right),

where
\displaystyle\textstyle K_{g}:=\sup_{z\in\lambda\cup\mathcal{B}}|g(z)|
and
\displaystyle\textstyle O_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{K_{g}}{n^{2}}\right)\leq C\cdot\frac{K_{g}}{n^{2}}
for a constant
\displaystyle C>0
independent of
\displaystyle g
and
\displaystyle n
.

(ii) Suppose that the assumptions in (i) on
\displaystyle\lambda
and
\displaystyle f
hold, and a sequence of holomorphic functions
\displaystyle(g_{n})
converges uniformly to a function
\displaystyle g
on a domain containing
\displaystyle\lambda
. Then

(137)

\int_{\lambda}g_{n}(z)e^{n(-i)f(z)}dz=e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}\left(\dfrac{g(z_{0})}{if^{\prime}(z_{0})}\dfrac{1}{n}+o_{n\to\infty}\left(\dfrac{1}{|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\right)\right),

where
\displaystyle\textstyle o_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{n}\frac{1}{|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|}\cdot\frac{1}{n}
for a sequence of real positive numbers
\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{n})
converging to
\displaystyle 0
and independent of
\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle n
.

(iii) The
\displaystyle\textstyle O_{n\to\infty}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle o_{n\to\infty}
error terms in (
136
) and (
137
) do not depend on
\displaystyle z_{0}
when
\displaystyle z_{0}
varies in a fixed compact set which does not contain critical points of
\displaystyle f
.

Note that (iii) says that the expansions (
136
) and (
137
) are
uniform
with respect to
\displaystyle z_{0}
in the given range; see
[
61
, Chapter VII]
for the notion of uniform asymptotic expansions.

Proof of (ii) and (iii).
The statement (ii) is a direct consequence of the proof of (i) given in
[
51
]
. Indeed write
\displaystyle g_{n}=g+(g_{n}-g)
, and apply (i) to the integrals with integrands
\displaystyle g
and
\displaystyle g_{n}-g
. The uniform convergence of
\displaystyle(g_{n}-g)
to the
\displaystyle 0
function implies an asymptotic expansion like (
136
) for the integral with integrand
\displaystyle g_{n}-g
, with coefficients
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{(g_{n}-g)(z_{0})}{if^{\prime}(z_{0})}
and
\displaystyle K_{g_{n}-g}
arbitrarily close to
\displaystyle 0
as
\displaystyle n\to+\infty
. As there is no assumption on the convergence speed of the sequence
\displaystyle(g_{n})
, the error term is only known to be an
\displaystyle\textstyle o_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\right)
.

For the statement (iii) one uses an estimate of the constant
\displaystyle C
in (i). Namely, by the equations (3-10) to (3-15) of
[
51
]
in the case
\displaystyle\mu=1
and
\displaystyle S=1
, we have a lower bound for
\displaystyle C
of the following form, described below:

(138)

O_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{K_{g}}{n^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{16K_{\phi}}{|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|^{2}}\cdot\frac{K_{g}}{n^{2}}+O_{n\to\infty}\left(K_{g}e^{-\varepsilon n}\right).

Here
\displaystyle O_{n\to\infty}\left(K_{g}e^{-\varepsilon n}\right)\leq C^{\prime}K_{g}e^{-\varepsilon n}
, with
\displaystyle C^{\prime}>0
a constant bounded by the length of
\displaystyle\lambda
, and
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
depends only on the contour
\displaystyle\lambda
and a constant
\displaystyle R_{f}(z_{0})>0
; precisely,
\displaystyle\varepsilon
is chosen so that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(f(z)-f(z_{0}))\leq-\varepsilon
for all
\displaystyle z
satisfying
\displaystyle|z-z_{0}|\geq R_{f}(z_{0})
and belonging to a small deformation of
\displaystyle\lambda
, where the deformation occurs in the disk
\displaystyle D(z_{0},R_{f}(z_{0})]
(see the proof of
[
51
, Proposition 1.3]
). The constant
\displaystyle R_{f}(z_{0})
is such that the following properties hold (
[
51
, Proposition 2.1]
): (*) the solutions
\displaystyle(r,\theta)
of the equation
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}(f(z_{0}+re^{i\theta})-f(z_{0}))/r=0
have the form
\displaystyle(r,\theta)=(r,h_{k}(r))
when
\displaystyle r
belongs to on an interval containing
\displaystyle[0,R_{f}(z_{0})]
, where the functions
\displaystyle h_{k}(r)
,
\displaystyle k\in\{0,1\}
, are all defined and differentiable, and
\displaystyle\textstyle h_{k}(0)=-\arg(if^{\prime}(z_{0}))+\pi(k+\frac{1}{2})
.

Now assume that
\displaystyle z_{0}
varies in a compact set
\displaystyle E
which does not contain critical points of
\displaystyle f
. Then
\displaystyle R_{f}(z_{0})
as above is defined and can be chosen continuously as a function of
\displaystyle z_{0}\in E
. We have
\displaystyle\textstyle R_{f}:=\min_{z_{0}\in E}(R_{f}(z_{0}))>0
, and the above properties (*) hold true uniformly for all
\displaystyle z_{0}\in E
, replacing
\displaystyle R_{f}(z_{0})
with
\displaystyle R_{f}
. Using
\displaystyle R_{f}
to define the
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
above, it depends only on the compact set
\displaystyle E
. Also, in (
138
) we have (see
[
51
, equations (2-1) and (3-1)]
)

K_{\phi}:=\max_{z\in D_{z_{0}}}\left|1-\frac{f(z)-f(z_{0})}{f^{\prime}(z_{0})(z-z_{0})}\right|,

where
\displaystyle D_{z_{0}}
is a small closed disk neighborhood of
\displaystyle z_{0}
. Then
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{16K_{\phi}}{|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|^{2}}
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle z_{0}\in E
.

It follows that the error term
\displaystyle\textstyle e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}O_{n\to\infty}\left(K_{g}/n^{2}\right)
in (
136
) is a
\displaystyle\textstyle o_{n\to\infty}
of the leading order term
\displaystyle\textstyle e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}e^{i\theta}\frac{g(z_{0})}{f^{\prime}(z_{0})}\frac{1}{n}
, independent of
\displaystyle z_{0}\in E
, as claimed.
\displaystyle\Box

The second generalization of Proposition
4.2
that we will need is for contours passing
through
a maximum. It is a classical result that the general form of Perron’s method, which generalizes Proposition
5.3
(i), implies Proposition
4.2
(see
[
51
, Corollary 1.4, case of
\displaystyle\mu=2
and
\displaystyle k_{2}=k_{1}+1
]
). In the situation of a sequence
\displaystyle(g_{n})
converging uniformly to
\displaystyle g
as in Proposition
5.3
(ii), the proof of this classical result generalizes immediately to give the statement (ii) in the next Proposition. In particular, the arguments show that if we are in the situation of a sequence
\displaystyle(g_{n})
converging to
\displaystyle g
as in the statement (i), the error term
\displaystyle\textstyle o_{n\to\infty}\left(1/\sqrt{n}\right)
is in fact a
\displaystyle O_{n\to\infty}\left(1/n\right)
, as stated. We point out this particular case, as it can be found verbatim in the litterature.

Proposition 5.4
.

Keep the same assumptions on the path
\displaystyle\lambda
and the function
\displaystyle f
as in Proposition
4.2
.

(i)
(
[
50
, Remark 3.3]
,
[
61
, page 145, note of
\displaystyle\lx@sectionsign
5]
)
If there is a neighborhood of the critical point
\displaystyle z_{0}
of
\displaystyle f
where for all
\displaystyle n
large enough
\displaystyle\textstyle g_{n}(z)=g(z)+g_{n,1}(z)\frac{1}{n}
, with
\displaystyle g
holomorphic and independent of
\displaystyle n
, and
\displaystyle|g_{n,1}|
bounded from above by a constant independent of
\displaystyle n
, then we have

\int_{\lambda}g_{n}(z)e^{n(-i)f(z)}dz=e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}\left(\sqrt{2\pi}e^{i\theta}\dfrac{g(z_{0})}{\sqrt{|f^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})|}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+O_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)

for some explicit constant
\displaystyle\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii) Suppose
\displaystyle(g_{n})
is a sequence of holomorphic functions converging uniformly to a function
\displaystyle g
on a domain containing
\displaystyle\lambda
. Then we have, with
\displaystyle\theta
as in (i):

\int_{\lambda}g_{n}(z)e^{n(-i)f(z)}dz=e^{n(-i)f(z_{0})}\left(\sqrt{2\pi}e^{i\theta}\dfrac{g(z_{0})}{\sqrt{|f^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})|}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+o_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right).

We now describe in Lemma
5.5
some asymptotics of the integrals
\displaystyle I_{N,\pm}(\ell)
in (
134
). The proof will follow the main lines of the analysis of Section
4
. We will use Proposition
5.3
because
\displaystyle\rho(z)
(and hence
\displaystyle g_{N}(z)
, see (
135
)) has poles at
\displaystyle z=0
and
\displaystyle z=2i\pi
; indeed, some subintegrals of
\displaystyle I_{N,\pm}(\ell)
, which were negligible in the context of Section
4
(by the results of Sections
4.3.1
-
4.3.2
), will now dominate its asymptotics in case (b) (
\displaystyle\ell=2i\pi
). Proposition
5.4
(ii) will play the same role as Proposition
4.2
in Section
4
, to estimate the integrals (where now
\displaystyle g_{N}
depends on
\displaystyle N
) on subcontours with endpoints away from
\displaystyle 0
and
\displaystyle 2i\pi
. Recall that
\displaystyle{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}:={\rm Log}(u_{0})+i\pi a_{0}
, and the endpoint
\displaystyle s_{N}^{-}
of
\displaystyle C_{N}^{\pm}
is written as
\displaystyle\textstyle\alpha^{-}\frac{\pi}{N}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

For
\displaystyle g_{N}(z)
as in (
135
) we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{2\pi}{N}\left|I_{N,-}(i\pi)\right|

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\rm Vol}(M),

\displaystyle\displaystyle|I_{N,+}(i\pi)|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sim_{N\to+\infty}\dfrac{N^{\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{-}}{2}}}{{\rm Log}(N)}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{-}\pi}\right)^{\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{-}}{2}}|v_{N}|,

\displaystyle\displaystyle|I_{N,+}(2i\pi)|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sim_{N\to+\infty}\dfrac{N^{\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{-}}{2}}}{{\rm Log}(N)}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{-}\pi}\right)^{\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{-}}{2}}|v_{N}|,

\displaystyle\displaystyle|I_{N,-}(2i\pi)|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sim_{N\to+\infty}\dfrac{N^{\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{-}}{2}}}{{\rm Log}(N)}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{-}\pi}\right)^{\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{-}}{2}}e^{-\alpha^{-}\pi}|v_{N}|,

where
\displaystyle(v_{N})
(the same in the last three asymptotic formulas) is a bounded sequence, bounded from below by a constant
\displaystyle>0
, and we choose
\displaystyle a_{0}\geq 4
(even, as usual) so that
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(\frac{{\bf l}_{0}^{\prime}}{2i\pi}-\frac{1}{2})>-\frac{1}{2}
.

The choice of
\displaystyle a_{0}\geq 4
will be explained in Step 6 of the proof. When
\displaystyle a_{0}=2
the asymptotics of the integrals
\displaystyle I_{N,+}(i\pi)
,
\displaystyle I_{N,+}(2i\pi)
and
\displaystyle I_{N,-}(2i\pi)
depend on the sign of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}({\rm Log}(u_{0}))
; for instance, if it is negative, then
\displaystyle I_{N,+}(2i\pi)
and
\displaystyle I_{N,-}(2i\pi)
are of the form in (
115
). The choice
\displaystyle a_{0}=2
would not affect the conclusions of the subsequent results.

Remark 5.6
.

One could worry about the factor
\displaystyle e^{-\alpha^{-}\pi}
in the asymptotic formula of
\displaystyle I_{N,-}(2i\pi)
but not of
\displaystyle I_{N,+}(2i\pi)
; indeed, by (
133
) these formulas combine to give the leading order term as
\displaystyle N\to+\infty
of the LHS integral for
\displaystyle\ell={\bf l}_{1,\infty}=2i\pi
, which does not depend on
\displaystyle\alpha^{-}
. However, one should be careful that a full asymptotic expansion of this integral is needed to describe it faitfully. In the present situation
\displaystyle I_{N,-}(\ell)
and
\displaystyle I_{N,+}(\ell)
emerged from the different asymptotical behaviours of the integrand on the half-spaces
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)<0
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)>0
, with terms becoming exponentially small in the former region and exponentially large in the latter (see (
129
), and also (
94
)). Therefore
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(z)=0
is an anti-Stokes line of the asymptotics of the integrand, and exponentially small terms coming from a full asymptotic expansion of
\displaystyle I_{N,-}(\ell)
and
\displaystyle I_{N,+}(\ell)
are required to recover the LHS integral of (
133
) as
\displaystyle N\to+\infty
.

Proof.

Step 1: Conditions on moving the contours.

We adapt the analysis of Sections
4.4
(case (a),
\displaystyle\ell=i\pi
) and
4.5
(case (b),
\displaystyle\ell=2i\pi
) to the integrals
\displaystyle I_{N,\pm}(\ell)
. Recall that the integrals in Section
4.4
-
4.5
have
\displaystyle g_{N}\equiv 1
and
\displaystyle C_{N}^{-}
or
\displaystyle C_{N}^{+}
replaced by
\displaystyle[is_{N}^{-},2i\pi-is_{N}^{+}]
(the two being homotopic relatively to their endpoints, with the opposite or the same orientation respectively).

First we show that the function
\displaystyle g_{n}:z\mapsto g_{N}(z)
,
\displaystyle\textstyle n:=\frac{N}{2\pi}
, is holomorphic on a domain where we can deform
\displaystyle C_{N}^{-}
or
\displaystyle C_{N}^{+}
to a contour very close to those in Figure

\displaystyle 0
or
\displaystyle 2i\pi

These deformations occur in the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
formed by the strip
\displaystyle-\eta\leq\mathfrak{I}(z)\leq 2\pi
with cuts
\displaystyle[0,+\infty[
and
\displaystyle 2i\pi+[0,+\infty[

\displaystyle\eta>0

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{3}

See Figure

for an illustration of this. One should understand Figure
9
as a zoomed part of Figure
8
around the origin.

\displaystyle-\upsilon^{\prime\prime}
\displaystyle is_{N}^{-}
\displaystyle-i\eta
Cut
Shifted cut
\displaystyle L_{1}
(if
\displaystyle\arg(u_{0})<0
)
Shifted cut
\displaystyle L_{1}
(if
\displaystyle\arg(u_{0})>0
)
\displaystyle C_{a}^{-}
